# THE OCCUPANCY GAME

### Fundamental Analysis from the Ground Up — One Company, Every Lesson

*A step-by-step education using G8 Education (ASX:GEM) as the running example*

---

## Before you read: what this book is, and is not

**This book is education, not financial advice.** It teaches a method of analysis, using one real company's public history as the worked example. It does not recommend the purchase or sale of any security, and nothing in it is tailored to any reader's circumstances. The author is not a licensed adviser. Readers make their own decisions and own their consequences — teaching exactly that is one of the book's aims.

**Its facts were gathered honestly and will age.** Figures were verified against public sources where possible (each is listed in the Endnotes & Sources); estimates and unverified figures are marked ≈ throughout. The story it follows was unresolved when the book was written — deliberately so.

**On the 2025–26 criminal case** discussed in Chapter 21: this book records charges as charges and allegations as allegations. The accused is entitled to a fair trial and is presumed innocent of what he has denied. The book contains no detail of the alleged offences; its subject is the business consequence of the crisis, and its first thought is with the families affected.

**Style note.** The book is written under the writing rules of ASD-STE100 Simplified Technical English (Issue 9, 2025), adapted for narrative teaching: short sentences, active voice, one idea at a time, and a fixed lexicon of named metaphors — defined at first use and listed in the glossary — in place of casual figures of speech.

**How to read it.** The book is a strict hierarchy: every chapter builds only on the chapters before it, and nothing is used before it is taught. Part I builds the machine. Part II maps the playing field. Part III reads one company's sixteen years through the machinery. Part IV builds the instrument panel. Part V turns readings into decisions. Each chapter ends with the one thing to remember; Chapter 36 compresses the whole apparatus onto a single page.

# PART I — FOUNDATIONS: HOW A BUSINESS MAKES AND LOSES MONEY {#part1}


## Chapter 1 — The Profit Machine {#ch01}

*Every idea in this book stands on one simple picture. Here it is.*

### A business is a machine

Think of a business as a machine. You put money in one side. Money comes out the other side.

The money that goes in is the **costs**. Costs are all the payments a business makes to stay open: rent, wages, electricity, insurance.

The money that comes out is the **revenue**. Revenue is all the money that customers pay the business.

If more money comes out than goes in, the difference is **profit**. If more money goes in than comes out, the difference is a **loss**.

That is the whole game:

> **Profit = revenue − costs**

Every crash and every recovery in this book comes from this one formula. Every clever indicator in this book watches a part of this formula.

Now we will build one specific machine: a childcare centre. We will call this picture **the Profit Machine**, and we will use the name through the whole book.

### The toy centre

Meet Sunny Corner, a childcare centre that we invented for this book. We will use round, toy numbers. The toy numbers keep the mathematics easy. The real numbers come later in the chapter.

Sunny Corner has a licence for **50 children each day**. These 50 positions are its "places". Each family pays a fee of **$150 for each day of care**.

The revenue side of the machine is one line of arithmetic:

> **Revenue = children × fee**

If children fill all 50 places, Sunny Corner collects 50 × $150 = **$7,500 each day**.

If only 25 children attend, Sunny Corner collects 25 × $150 = **$3,750 each day**.

The percentage of places that children fill has a name: **occupancy**. Fifty children in 50 places is 100% occupancy. Twenty-five children in 50 places is 50% occupancy. Occupancy answers one question: how full is the machine? It is the most important word in this book. It is in the title for a reason.

That was the simple side. Now look at the cost side of the machine. The cost side is where the story becomes interesting.

### The cost side: the machine runs, full or empty

Sunny Corner has two large costs: rent and staff. We will examine them one at a time.

**Rent.** Sunny Corner leases its building. A **lease** is a long-term rental contract. Say the rent is $1,000 each day. The landlord does not care how many children came today. Fifty children, five children, zero children — the rent is $1,000. The contract runs for years, and the bill always arrives.

A cost that stays the same, busy or empty, is a **fixed cost**.

**Staff.** You could expect staff costs to be different. Fewer children should mean fewer carers and lower wages. In most businesses, that is true. In Australian childcare, it is mostly not true. Here is the reason.

Australia has strict national rules on staff numbers in childcare. The rules set a legal minimum number of educators for each group of children. The minimum changes with the age of the children:

- Babies (birth to 24 months): **1 educator for each 4 children**, in all states.
- Toddlers (24 to 36 months): **1 educator for each 5 children** in most states. Victoria requires 1 for each 4.
- Older children (36 months and up): **1 educator for each 10 or 11 children**, dependent on the state.

The educator must also be in the room and directly involved with the children. An educator who does paperwork in the office does not count. We will call these rules the **ratio law**. The ratio law exists for a good reason: young children need close care. But the ratio law has a side effect on the Profit Machine.

Take Sunny Corner's baby room, with a licence for 8 babies. The ratio law says: 8 babies need 2 educators. Now say that only 5 babies are enrolled. How many educators does the law require? Still 2. One educator can care for a maximum of 4 babies, and you have 5. One educator became too few when the fifth baby arrived.

So the room collects 5 fees but pays 2 full wages. The cost did not decrease to match the revenue. The wages behave almost like rent: they are close to fixed. Staff costs decrease in large steps, not smoothly. That idea is so important that it gets its own chapter — Chapter 2.

Add the other costs: electricity, insurance, the centre director's salary, cleaning. The centre pays almost all of them whether the rooms are full or half empty.

Only a small part of the costs truly moves with the number of children: food, nappies, craft supplies. A cost that rises and falls with the number of customers is a **variable cost**. In childcare, variable costs are the minor player.

Keep this picture:

> **Revenue moves with occupancy. Costs mostly do not.**

You pay for the machine whether one child attends or fifty.

### A tale of two businesses

Compare Sunny Corner with a fruit stall at a weekend market. The comparison shows how unusual a childcare centre is.

The fruit stall's largest cost is the fruit. On a quiet day, the stallholder buys less fruit, sells less fruit, and earns a little less. On a busy day, she buys more and earns more. Her costs rise and fall with her sales. If customers stay away for a month, she stops buying stock. Her business can shrink almost to zero and wait.

Sunny Corner cannot shrink like that. Its costs are locked in place. The lease runs for years. The ratio law sets the staff roster. The building needs light, insurance, and cleaning every day. If enrolments stop for a month, almost none of the spending stops. The owner can only escape the machine if she walks away from the lease. And a broken lease has its own heavy price.

Neither business is better than the other. But they fail in different ways. The fruit stall shrinks slowly and safely. The fixed-cost machine makes good money when it is full and loses money fast when it is empty. It changes from one state to the other faster than most people expect.

Can the centre lift its fees to close the gap? A little, sometimes. But fees have limits. Parents compare prices, and rival centres operate nearby. Government rules now also limit how fast large operators can lift fees. Part II explains those rules. When occupancy falls, higher fees cannot pull the machine back to profit. The number of children is what counts.

### The toy numbers, three ways

Here is a simple daily cost sheet for Sunny Corner. The numbers are toy numbers, deliberately round:

| Cost | Each day | Type |
|---|---|---|
| Rent | $1,000 | Fixed |
| Wages (educators + director) | $4,500 | Mostly fixed |
| Power, insurance, admin | $500 | Fixed |
| Food and supplies | $10 for each child | Variable |

Now run the machine at three occupancy levels.

**Full — 50 children (100% occupancy).**
Revenue: 50 × $150 = $7,500.
Costs: $1,000 + $4,500 + $500 + (50 × $10) = $6,500.
**Profit: $1,000 each day.**

**Comfortable — 45 children (90% occupancy).**
Revenue: 45 × $150 = $6,750.
Costs: $1,000 + $4,500 + $500 + (45 × $10) = $6,450.
**Profit: $300 each day.** That is still fine. But look closely. Occupancy fell 10%, and profit fell 70%. Hold that thought. It is the subject of Chapter 3.

**Half empty — 25 children (50% occupancy).**
Revenue: 25 × $150 = $3,750.
Costs: $1,000 + $4,500 + $500 + (25 × $10) = $6,250.
**Loss: $2,500 each day.** (In real life, the centre would release some staff at this point. But it can release far fewer than you hope, because of the ratio law. Chapter 2 shows why.)

Same building. Same fee. Same landlord. Only occupancy changed. The machine moved from a profit of $1,000 each day to a loss of $2,500 each day.

Every business with large fixed costs works this way. Hotels, gyms, airlines, cinemas — and childcare. The machine is expensive to keep open. One question decides whether the machine makes money or loses money: **how full is it?**

### The real machine: G8 Education

Now the real numbers. The example company for this whole book is **G8 Education**. Its code on the Australian stock exchange is GEM. G8 is Australia's largest listed childcare company. A **listed company** is a company whose shares people can buy and sell on a stock exchange. (Shares get a full explanation in Chapters 4 and 5.) At its 2025 full-year results, G8 operated about **395 centres** under many local brand names.

Think of G8 as about 395 Sunny Corners in one giant machine. Each centre has its own lease, its own rooms, and its own roster under the ratio law. Everything you learned about one toy centre applies 395 times over.

There is one difference. The owner of a single centre watches her own front door. She knows exactly how full her centre is today. The owner of G8 shares must judge 395 front doors at once. She only has the numbers that the company publishes, months later. That gap — between the front doors today and the published numbers later — is where most of this book lives.

In calendar year 2025, G8's revenue was **$948.2 million**. That was 7.2% below the year before.

Why did revenue fall? Return to the formula: revenue = children × fee. Fees did not fall. Fees rose a little. The number of children fell. G8's average occupancy across 2025 was **65.8%**. That was almost 5 percentage points below the year before.

Remember what 65.8% occupancy meant for Sunny Corner. It is a machine that is built and staffed for 50 children, with about 33 children in it. The company pays rent on every building. The rosters obey the ratio law and cannot shrink smoothly. About one place in three earns nothing and still costs almost everything.

One more number makes the point. In 2025, G8 kept less than $10 of underlying operating profit from each $100 of revenue. Costs consumed the rest before that small remainder appeared. A machine that large, that empty, keeps only a thin margin. Each extra empty place makes the margin thinner. (Profit fell much faster than revenue in 2025 — the exact mechanics are Chapter 3's job.)

Why was the machine so empty? That question is the story of Part III: the loss of trust, the flood of rival centres, the whole painful history. We are not there yet. Chapter 1 only needs you to see the machine.

### Where the machine takes us

Here is a preview of the road ahead.

Costs are fixed, so small occupancy changes cause large profit changes (Chapter 3). Profit swings hard, so the share price swings harder (Chapter 4). Crashes force companies to raise emergency money, so owners can lose most of their stake while the company survives (Chapter 5). And occupancy is the last event in a long chain: trust, then enquiries, then tours, then enrolments, then occupancy. A person who watches the early links sees trouble long before the profit report shows it (Chapter 6, and all of Part IV).

Each of those chapters is today's formula — profit = revenue − costs — at work in a machine that costs the same, full or empty.

One honest note before we continue. This book is education, not financial advice. It teaches you how to analyse a business, with one real and bruised company as the worked example. It does not tell you to buy or sell anything. You make your own decisions.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** A childcare centre pays for the whole machine — rent and legally required staff — full or empty, so everything depends on occupancy.

---


## Chapter 2 — Staircase Costs {#ch02}

*Chapter 1 showed that wages behave almost like rent. This chapter shows exactly how — and why "almost" matters.*

### Costs that climb in steps

Picture a staircase. You cannot stand at a height between two steps. You are on step one, or you are on step two. There is nothing in between.

Staff costs in childcare work the same way. They do not rise smoothly, one dollar at a time. They jump in whole-wage steps. One extra child can force the centre to hire one extra educator. We will call this idea **Staircase Costs**, and we will use the name through the whole book.

The ratio law from Chapter 1 builds the staircase. Here is the simplest version.

### The fifth baby

Return to Sunny Corner's baby room. The ratio law for babies is 1 educator for each 4 children.

Watch the room grow, one baby at a time:

- 1 baby: the law requires 1 educator.
- 2 babies: still 1 educator.
- 3 babies: still 1 educator.
- 4 babies: still 1 educator.
- **5 babies: the law now requires 2 educators.**
- 6, 7, 8 babies: still 2 educators.
- **9 babies: 3 educators.**

Baby number 5 changed everything. Babies 2, 3, and 4 each added a fee and almost no cost. Baby 5 added one fee and one full wage.

Now put toy money on the staircase. Say the fee is $150 each day, and an educator's wage is $250 each day. (These are toy numbers. Real wages follow an award, which is a legal minimum pay scale. Part II returns to wages.)

Look at what each baby does to daily profit in that room:

- Baby 4 brings in $150. Extra cost: about $10 of food and supplies. The room gains about $140.
- Baby 5 brings in $150. Extra cost: $10, plus a $250 wage. The room **loses** about $110.

The same product, the same fee, the same day. One baby is highly profitable. The next baby loses money. That is a staircase business.

### Full-room wages for half-full rooms

Now walk down the staircase instead of up. This direction matters more for our story, because falling occupancy is what breaks childcare companies.

Say the baby room has 8 enrolled babies and 2 educators. Two families leave the area. Now 6 babies attend. How many educators does the law require? Still 2. Six babies are more than 4, so one educator is not enough.

The room lost 2 fees, which is $300 each day. It saved almost nothing. The wages stayed at 2 educators. Revenue walked down the smooth slope. Costs stayed on the same step.

Costs only drop when enrolments fall past a step edge. The room must fall from 8 babies to 4 before the second wage can go. That is a fall of 50% in that room's revenue before the big cost moves at all.

This is the meaning of the phrase from Chapter 1: **half-full rooms carry full-room wages**.

### Why the real staircase is even stickier

The toy staircase already looks harsh. The real one is worse, for five reasons.

**1. Children come in mixed patterns.** Real families book 2, 3, or 5 days each week. Monday can have 8 babies and Thursday can have 5. The roster must cover the busiest pattern. The centre staffs for Monday and loses money on Thursday.

**2. Rooms cannot merge easily.** A centre with 5 babies and 6 toddlers cannot simply make one room of 11 children. Each age group has its own ratio, its own room standards, and its own space rules. Some mixing is legal, but the limits arrive fast.

**3. Qualified staff are hard to find.** For years, Australia has had a shortage of qualified educators. A centre that releases a good educator in a weak month may not find one in a strong month. Many operators hold extra staff through weak periods for this reason. The staircase says "release the wage". The labour market says "you will regret it".

**4. Quality ratings punish cuts.** Australian centres receive public quality ratings. Parents read them. A centre that runs at the bare legal minimum, with constant staff churn, risks a weak rating. A weak rating scares parents away and lowers occupancy further. Cutting staff to save the machine can starve the machine.

**5. People are not steps.** Educators build bonds with children. Parents choose a centre because of a person. When that person goes, families sometimes follow. A wage saved can become three fees lost.

So real childcare operators cannot even walk down the legal staircase freely. They hold wages above the legal minimum in weak times. This makes staff costs behave even more like rent than the ratio law alone requires.

### The whole centre on one page

We can now build all of Sunny Corner from its rooms. The centre has three rooms:

| Room | Licence | Ratio | Educators at full |
|---|---|---|---|
| Babies | 8 places | 1:4 | 2 |
| Toddlers | 15 places | 1:5 | 3 |
| Older children | 27 places | ≈1:11 (toy: 1:9) | 3 |

That is 50 places and 8 educators at full occupancy. At $250 each wage, the educators cost $2,000 each day, plus the director. Round it to the $4,500 total wage line from Chapter 1, which also covers leave, superannuation, and weekend-free penalty averages. Toy numbers, but honest in shape.

Now empty the centre to 70% occupancy, which is 35 children. Spread the loss evenly: 6 babies, 10 toddlers, 19 older children.

Count the educators the law still requires:

- Babies: 6 children at 1:4 → still **2 educators**.
- Toddlers: 10 children at 1:5 → **2 educators** (down from 3).
- Older children: 19 children at 1:9 → **3 educators** (no change).

The centre lost 15 children. That is 30% of its revenue, $2,250 each day. It shed exactly one wage, $250 each day. Revenue fell 30%. Educator wages fell 12.5% — one wage in eight. Rent fell 0%.

Do the arithmetic with Chapter 1's cost sheet. Revenue: 35 × $150 = $5,250. Costs: $1,000 rent + $4,250 wages + $500 other + $350 supplies = $6,100. The centre now loses about $850 each day. At full occupancy, it earned $1,000. A 30% revenue fall deleted the profit twice over.

No manager made a mistake in this story. The staircase did all of it.

### The casual-staff pressure valve — and its limits

Operators do have one tool of flexibility: casual staff. A **casual** is a worker with no fixed weekly hours, paid a higher hourly rate in exchange. Agencies also hire out relief educators by the day.

Casuals let a centre match Thursday's roster to Thursday's children. When a room falls below a step edge for one day, the centre simply books one less casual. This softens the staircase. It does not remove it.

The limits are real. Casual rates cost more for each hour, often much more through an agency. The permanent core of the roster must stay, because rooms need consistent, qualified leaders every day. And children do not attend in tidy patterns that casuals can neatly fill. A centre that leans too hard on casuals pays more, scores lower on quality, and churns the faces that parents trust.

So think of casuals as padding on the step edges, not as a ramp. The staircase stands.

### The staircase at company scale

Now zoom out from one room to G8 Education's roughly 395 centres.

Each centre holds several rooms. Each room sits somewhere on its own staircase. Some rooms sit just below a step edge, where each new child is nearly pure profit. Some rooms sit just above a step edge, where the last child forced a full wage.

When national occupancy is high, most rooms sit in the good zone. Fees pour in against rosters that are already paid for. When national occupancy falls, thousands of rooms slide into the bad zone together. Each room keeps its wages and loses its fees.

Remember G8's 2025 numbers from Chapter 1: average occupancy of 65.8%. Think of that as thousands of rooms, and a large share of them half full. Each of those rooms carried full-room wages under the ratio law. Each of them also carried its share of rent, insurance, and the director's salary.

Wages are the largest cost in childcare, larger than rent. The company cannot cut them smoothly when children leave. That single fact converts a modest fall in occupancy into a severe fall in profit. Chapter 3 measures exactly how severe.

### A note on the other direction

The staircase punishes falling occupancy. But read the same staircase upward, and it promises something.

A room with 5 babies and 2 educators is a room with 3 empty, pre-staffed places. Babies 6, 7, and 8 would each bring about $140 of daily profit and no new wage. The costs are already paid. Recovery, when it comes, is nearly pure profit.

This is why investors watch half-empty childcare companies at all. The same machine that destroys profit on the way down can rebuild it, step by step, on the way up. Each returned child lands on a step that is already paid for. The whole question is whether the children come back. Nobody can command them to come back. The company can only make its centres worth choosing, and wait. That question — how to see the children coming before the company reports them — is the engine of Part IV.

### What the staircase means for you as an analyst

Take three habits from this chapter.

First, never assume that costs follow revenue down. In a staircase business, they do not. When you see "occupancy fell 5 points", expect profit to fall much more.

Second, ask where the rooms sit on their staircases. A company at 90% occupancy and a company at 65% occupancy are different animals. The first earns almost a full fee on each new child. The second still carries wages for children who left.

Third, respect the reasons operators keep staff. Retention, ratings, and shortage are real forces. A company that guards its educators in a downturn may protect its recovery. A company that cuts to the legal bone may save this year and lose the next three. Chapter 20 shows G8 making exactly this choice.

The staircase is not a flaw in the childcare business. It is the childcare business. The ratio law that creates the cost steps is the same law that makes parents trust centres with their children. No trust, no customers. The steps are the price of the product.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** Staff costs move in whole-wage steps under the ratio law, so revenue slides smoothly down while costs hold — half-full rooms carry full-room wages.

---


## Chapter 3 — Operating Leverage: Small Dips, Big Crashes {#ch03}

*Chapters 1 and 2 built a machine with heavy fixed costs and staircase wages. This chapter measures what that machine does to profit.*

### The question this chapter answers

In Chapter 1, Sunny Corner lost 10% of its occupancy, and profit fell 70%. That looked like a strange, extreme result. It was not extreme. It was normal for this kind of business.

This chapter gives the effect a name, a formula, and a real example. The name is **operating leverage**. In plain words: when costs are fixed, small changes in revenue cause large changes in profit. The effect works in both directions, down and up.

### Break-even: the line between two worlds

Start with one question: how many children does Sunny Corner need before it stops losing money?

The occupancy level where profit is exactly zero is the **break-even** point. Below it, every day ends in loss. Above it, every day ends in profit.

We can find it with two numbers from Chapter 1's cost sheet.

**Number one: the fixed costs.** Rent $1,000, wages $4,500, other $500. Total: **$6,000 each day**. (We treat wages as fixed here. Chapter 2 showed that this is nearly true, and we keep the toy model simple.)

**Number two: what each child contributes.** Each child brings a $150 fee and causes $10 of food and supplies. So each child contributes $150 − $10 = **$140 each day** toward the fixed costs. This figure — fee minus variable cost — is called the **contribution** of one customer.

Now the break-even question becomes simple division. How many $140 contributions cover $6,000 of fixed costs?

> $6,000 ÷ $140 ≈ 43 children

Sunny Corner has 50 places. So its break-even occupancy is 43 ÷ 50 = **86%**.

Stop and look at that number. The centre must fill 86% of its places just to earn zero. Every child up to number 43 works to pay the rent and the wages. Only children 44 to 50 create profit.

### Seven children of profit

Here is the whole business of Sunny Corner in one sentence: children 1 to 43 pay for the machine, and children 44 to 50 pay the owner.

The profit of the entire centre rides on 7 children out of 50. Now you can see why small dips cause big crashes. The dip does not need to touch the 43. It only needs to touch the 7.

Watch the profit line move as occupancy falls, using the same numbers as always:

| Children | Occupancy | Profit each day | Change from full |
|---|---|---|---|
| 50 | 100% | +$1,000 | — |
| 47 | 94% | +$580 | −42% |
| 45 | 90% | +$300 | −70% |
| 43 | 86% | ≈$0 | −100% |
| 40 | 80% | −$400 | loss |
| 33 | 66% | −$1,380 | deep loss |
| 25 | 50% | −$2,500 | severe loss |

Read the first three rows slowly. A 6% fall in occupancy cut profit by 42%. A 10% fall cut profit by 70%. A 14% fall erased profit completely.

Then read the bottom rows. At 66% occupancy — close to G8's real 2025 average — the toy centre loses $1,380 each day. The machine did not slow down. It reversed.

### Where the 86% came from — and why real centres vary

A quick honesty note on that 86%. It came from our toy cost sheet, and toy numbers were chosen for round arithmetic, not realism. Real centres break even across a wide range, commonly somewhere between 60% and 80% occupancy, dependent on rent, wages, and fees. A centre with cheap rent in a full suburb breaks even low. A centre with new-building rent in an oversupplied suburb breaks even high.

The exact number matters less than the structure. Every centre has a break-even line. Every centre's profit lives entirely in the children above that line. And no operator fully controls where the line sits, because rent is contracted and wages follow the ratio law and the award.

Keep the toy's lesson, not the toy's number: the profitable children are the last few, and they are always the first to go.

### The lever, named

Why call this "leverage"? A lever is a bar that turns a small push into a large force. Operating leverage turns a small change in revenue into a large change in profit. The fixed costs are the pivot.

You can put a rough number on the lever. Compare the percentage fall in profit with the percentage fall in revenue.

At Sunny Corner, from 50 to 45 children: revenue fell 10%, profit fell 70%. The lever multiplied the move by 7.

There is no magic constant here. The multiplier depends on how close the business runs to break-even. Far above break-even, the lever is mild. Near break-even, the lever is savage. Below break-even, losses deepen with every lost child. The closer to the line, the wilder the swings.

That rule is worth keeping: **the same company gets more dangerous, and more explosive, as it approaches its break-even point** — dangerous on the way down, explosive on the way up.

### The real machine again: G8 in 2025

Now check the toy model against reality.

From Chapter 1: G8's 2025 revenue was $948.2 million, down 7.2% on the year before. Average occupancy fell almost 5 percentage points to 65.8%.

If costs followed revenue, profit would also have fallen about 7%. It did not. G8's underlying operating profit fell 18.9%, to $93.3 million.

A 7.2% revenue fall became an 18.9% profit fall. The lever multiplied the move by about 2.6. The multiplier is smaller than Sunny Corner's 7, and the reasons are instructive. G8 is a mixture of hundreds of centres. Some run full and profitable, and some run near-empty. The mixture softens the average. G8 also cut costs where it could: it closed weak centres and trimmed support office spending. Real companies fight the lever. But the lever still won, by a factor of 2.6.

One warning about words. Companies report several kinds of "profit", under names like "underlying" and "statutory". The differences matter, and Chapter 29 untangles them. In 2025, G8's statutory result was a large loss, mostly from a one-time accounting write-down. That write-down is a separate story for Part III. The 18.9% fall in underlying operating profit is the number that shows the lever at work.

### The moving line

One more layer makes the real world harder than the toy. The break-even line itself moves.

Break-even came from two numbers: fixed costs, and contribution for each child. Change either number, and the line shifts under the company's feet.

Watch what a wage rise does. Say Sunny Corner's wage bill rises 10%, from $4,500 to $4,950 each day. Fixed costs are now $6,450. Break-even becomes $6,450 ÷ $140 ≈ 46 children, or 92% occupancy. The centre changed nothing, and its safe zone shrank from 7 children to 4.

Now watch a fee rise. Say the fee climbs from $150 to $160, and variable costs stay at $10. Contribution becomes $150. Break-even becomes $6,450 ÷ $150 = 43 children again. The fee rise repaired the damage of the wage rise.

This tug-of-war runs through the whole childcare sector, every year. Wages push break-even up. Fees pull it back down. When wages rise faster than fees, every centre in the country needs more children just to stand still. Chapter 27 turns this squeeze into a signal you can watch.

For G8 in 2025, both forces were at work at once. Wages rose under a new pay deal. Fee increases were capped under the same deal. And occupancy — the only number that could rescue the arithmetic — fell. The lever, the staircase, and the moving line all pushed the same direction. The 18.9% profit fall was the sum of all three.

You can estimate a real company's position without inside information. The annual report gives revenue and profit. Profit near zero means the company sits close to break-even, where the lever is savage. A profit margin under $10 in each $100, as G8 had in 2025, means the line is close. Treat every forecast about such a company — including your own — as fragile.

### Fragile — and stressful — by design

Investors often call fixed-cost businesses "fragile". Now you can see exactly what they mean. The profit of the whole enterprise lives in a thin slice at the top of the occupancy range. Small shocks to that slice produce violent results.

This fragility has a human cost too. Managers of high-leverage businesses live close to the line. A flu season, a new rival across the road, a bad news story — each small event moves the profit needle hard. Forecasts miss. Promises break. Chapter 18 shows what happens when a leveraged company misses its own forecast twice in three months.

But remember the second edge, from Chapter 2. Leverage has no opinion about direction. If Sunny Corner's occupancy climbs back from 86% to 100%, profit climbs from zero to $1,000 each day. Fourteen points of occupancy create the centre's entire profit. The same lever that crushed profit on the way down multiplies it on the way up. Businesses like this reward the analyst who can judge which direction occupancy will move next — and punish everyone else.

That is why this book exists. The entire second half is a toolkit for one task: reading the direction of occupancy before the company reports it.

### What to do with this chapter

Three habits again, for the analyst you are becoming.

First, when you study any business, find the fixed costs and estimate the break-even point. You do not need perfect numbers. Even a rough break-even tells you whether the business lives near the line.

Second, never read a revenue fall at face value. Ask what the lever will do to it. A headline that says "revenue down 7%" can mean "profit down 19%", or worse, once the fixed costs bite.

Third, remember that leverage cuts both ways, and that the market often forgets this. A leveraged company near break-even is priced with fear. If occupancy turns up, the recovery in profit can be just as violent as the crash was. Whether the price already expects that recovery is a different question. It needs Chapter 4.

Because so far, we have only studied the machine's profit. We have not asked the question that investors actually face every day: what is this machine worth? What price should you pay for a share of it? And why does the price sometimes fall much further than the profit did? That is Chapter 4 — the Price Formula.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** With fixed costs, profit lives in the last few children above break-even, so a small occupancy dip crushes profit — and a small recovery multiplies it.

---


## Chapter 4 — The Price Formula {#ch04}

*Chapter 3 showed how occupancy moves profit. This chapter shows how profit moves the share price — and why the price moves further.*

### What a share is

A company can be divided into equal pieces. Each piece is a **share**. If you own a share, you own that fraction of the whole business: its centres, its profits, and its problems.

Say a toy company called KidCo is divided into 10 million shares. If you own 1 million of them, you own 10% of KidCo. You own 10% of every dollar of profit it makes.

Shares of a listed company trade on a stock exchange. The **share price** is simply the price of the last trade. Nothing more mystical than that. But what makes buyers and sellers agree on a number? This chapter builds that number from parts you already know.

### The formula

Here is the central formula of this chapter. We will call it **the Price Formula**, and we will use it for the rest of the book:

> **Share price = (profit ÷ shares) × confidence**

Take the parts one at a time.

**Profit ÷ shares.** This is the profit that belongs to one share. Call it **profit per share**. If KidCo earns $10 million and has 10 million shares, each share earns $1. Simple division. Investors call this figure "earnings per share", and you will see that phrase in every company report.

**Confidence.** No sensible person pays $1 for a share that earns $1 this year. A share pays year after year. The real question is: how many years of profit will you pay for, today, in advance? That number of years is the confidence figure. Investors call it **the multiple**. If buyers pay $15 for each $1 of yearly profit, the multiple is 15. In market language, this is a "price-to-earnings ratio of 15". Same thing: 15 years of today's profit, paid upfront.

So KidCo: $1 of profit per share × a multiple of 15 = a share price of $15.

### What the multiple really measures

Why would anyone pay 15 years of profit upfront? Because they expect the profit to grow, or at least to continue. Why would anyone pay only 5 years? Because they doubt the profit will survive.

The multiple is a measure of belief about the future:

- A high multiple (say 20 to 30) says: "This profit will grow. I pay extra for the growth."
- A middle multiple (say 10 to 15) says: "This profit looks stable. I pay a fair number of years."
- A low multiple (say 4 to 8) says: "This profit may shrink or vanish. I only pay for a few years, because the years after that are in doubt."

Notice something important. The multiple is not set by any authority. No formula produces it. It is the crowd's mood about the future, expressed as a number of years. It can change without anything happening inside the company. A news story, a rival's collapse, a change in interest rates — each can move the multiple on its own.

### What feeds confidence

Three forces feed the multiple, and each one returns later in this book.

**The alternative.** Money always has somewhere else to go. If a bank pays 1% interest, then $1 of yearly company profit looks precious, and the crowd pays many years for it. If the bank pays 6%, the same $1 looks ordinary. High interest rates pull multiples down across the whole market, and low rates push them up. This force alone can move a share price while the company sleeps. Chapter 12 shows this tide moving the entire childcare sector.

**The growth story.** A profit that grows is worth more years than a profit that stands still. Pay 20 years upfront for a profit that doubles on the way, and you really paid 10 years of the future profit. This is why fast-growing companies carry high multiples — and why the multiple collapses when the growth story dies. Chapter 16 shows exactly that collapse.

**The trust in management.** Numbers reach investors through the company's own reports and forecasts. A management team that hits its forecasts earns a calm, steady multiple. A team that misses teaches the crowd to discount everything it says. Trust is slow to build and quick to lose, and the multiple records it.

Rates, growth, trust. When you see a multiple move, one of these three usually moved first.

That gives us the map of every possible crash.

### The three ways a price can crash

Look at the formula again: price = (profit ÷ shares) × confidence. A price can only fall if one of its three parts moves against you:

1. **Profit falls.** The machine from Chapters 1–3 delivers less. Each share earns less.
2. **Confidence falls.** The profit is unchanged, but the crowd pays fewer years for it. The multiple shrinks.
3. **Shares increase.** The company creates new shares. The same profit divides across more shares, so each share earns less. (This one is strange enough to deserve its own chapter — Chapter 5.)

The worst crashes combine two or three at once. And here is the trap for beginners: the second cause — a fall in confidence — needs no bad event inside the company at all. A company can report the same profit as last year and lose half its market value, because the crowd stopped believing in the years ahead. Part III shows this exact event in G8's history.

### Why the price falls further than the profit

Now connect Chapter 3 to this chapter, because the two levers stack.

Chapter 3 showed the first lever: a small occupancy dip becomes a large profit fall. The Price Formula holds the second lever: a large profit fall usually shrinks confidence too. The crowd does not just register the lower profit. The crowd asks: "What else don't we know? Can we trust the next forecast?" It pays fewer years for a profit stream it trusts less.

So the two levers multiply:

> Small occupancy dip → large profit fall → larger price fall.

Watch this with toy numbers. KidCo earns $1 per share on a multiple of 15. Price: $15. Now occupancy dips, and operating leverage turns the dip into a 20% profit fall. Profit per share: $0.80. If the multiple held at 15, the price would be $12 — down 20%.

But the multiple does not hold. The crowd trims its confidence from 15 years to 12. New price: $0.80 × 12 = $9.60. The price fell 36% on a 20% profit fall. No panic, no scandal. Just both levers moving the same direction at once.

### A real morning: 4 November 2025

Here is the double lever in G8's real history — recent history.

Through 2025, G8 had guided investors to expect operating profit of about $115 million. On 4 November 2025, the company issued a trading update. Families under cost-of-living pressure were making fewer enquiries. Occupancy was running 6.6 percentage points below the prior year. The new profit guidance: $91 to $98 million.

Take the midpoint, about $94.5 million. The forecast fell roughly 18%. By late morning of the same day, the share price had fallen 13.7% — and this was a price that had already fallen for months.

Both levers are visible in that morning. Occupancy slipped a few points, and the leverage of Chapters 2 and 3 turned the slip into an 18% forecast cut. Then the second lever engaged: a company that cuts its own forecast teaches the crowd to trust its forecasts less. Some of that morning's fall was arithmetic on the new profit number. The rest was confidence leaving the multiple.

Keep this event in mind. It returns in Chapter 6 with a different question: what did the company see first, before occupancy fell? The answer — enquiries — opens the door to everything in Part IV. A person who watched the right early signal did not need to wait for the company's announcement. The announcement only confirmed, at 13.7% cost, what the signal had already said.

### The whole company, priced

One more piece of language, because news reports use it constantly.

Multiply the share price by the number of shares, and you get the market's price for the entire company. This is the **market value** (market reports say "market capitalisation"). KidCo at $15 with 10 million shares has a market value of $150 million.

When a headline says a company "lost $200 million in value today", no money left any vault. The market value fell because the price of one share fell. The same machine stands in the same suburbs with the same children. What changed was the crowd's answer to one question: what is the whole thing worth per slice, today?

Market value is also the honest way to compare a company with its own past. A share price of $2 today against $5 years ago tells you nothing by itself, because the number of shares may have changed between the two dates. Market value at least compares whole company with whole company. Chapter 22 uses this tool on G8's full history, with results that surprise most people.

### Reading the formula backwards

The Price Formula also works in reverse, and this reverse reading is one of the most useful habits in investing.

You always know the price — the market publishes it every minute. Reports give you profit per share. Divide, and you get the multiple the crowd is paying today. The multiple tells you what the crowd currently believes, in years.

Try the logic on any company. Price $15, earnings $1: the crowd pays 15 years — it believes the profit is safe. Price $4, earnings $1: the crowd pays 4 years — it believes the profit is dying. Neither belief is automatically correct. The crowd is sometimes right, sometimes wrong. But now you know exactly what belief you would buy, or bet against, at today's price.

This is the honest way to use the formula. Not "the price fell, so the share is cheap". Instead: "the price implies this belief about future profit — do my facts support a different belief?" The whole of Part IV builds the facts. Part V builds the discipline to act on them, and only on them.

One warning before we move on. Beginners look at a price that fell from $15 to $5 and feel that $15 is the "real" value, waiting to return. It is not. The old price was old profit multiplied by old confidence — two numbers that no longer exist. And there is a third number that may have changed too: the count of shares itself. When a wounded company creates millions of new shares, old prices stop meaning anything at all. That is Chapter 5 — the Pizza Principle.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** A share price is profit per share multiplied by confidence, so a price can crash three ways — profit falls, confidence falls, or the share count rises.

---


## Chapter 5 — The Pizza Principle {#ch05}

*Chapter 4 left one crash route unexplored: the share count itself. This chapter walks it.*

### More slices, same pizza

Picture a pizza cut into 8 slices. You hold 1 slice. You hold one eighth of the pizza.

Now someone cuts the same pizza into 16 slices, and you still hold 1 slice. Nobody took anything from your hand. But you now hold one sixteenth of the pizza. Your slice is half the meal it was.

Shares work the same way. The company is the pizza. The shares are the slices. When a company creates new shares, every old share becomes a smaller fraction of the same company. The word for this is **dilution**. We will call the whole idea **the Pizza Principle**, and it will follow us through the rest of the book.

One difference from a real pizza matters, and it is fair to state it early. When a company sells new slices, buyers pay money for them, and that money enters the company. The pizza gets a little bigger as it is cut into more slices. Whether the old owners win or lose depends on one thing: the price of the new slices. Hold that thought. It decides everything in this chapter.

### Why a company creates new shares

A company sells new shares for one reason: it wants cash that it does not have. This is called a **capital raising** — the company raises money by selling freshly created slices of itself.

Sometimes the reason is opportunity. A growing company sells new shares to buy new centres. Chapter 15 shows G8 in exactly that mode, in its early years.

Sometimes the reason is survival. A wounded company burns cash each month, and its bank grows nervous. It must find money or die. So it prints new slices and sells them for whatever buyers will pay. And buyers, who can see the wound, will not pay much.

That last sentence is the cruel mechanism of this chapter: **the companies that most need to sell new shares get the worst prices for them**. Strong companies raise money cheaply, in small slices. Desperate companies raise money expensively, in huge slices. Dilution hits hardest exactly when shareholders are already bleeding.

### The toy raising

Watch the mechanism with toy numbers.

KidCo, from Chapter 4, earns $10 million with 10 million shares. Profit per share: $1. The market's confidence multiple is 15. Share price: $15.

Now a crisis strikes. Profit falls to $5 million. Confidence falls to a multiple of 8. The new price: ($5m ÷ 10m) × 8 = $4. The share has fallen 73% already, through the two levers of Chapters 3 and 4.

The crisis also burns cash, and KidCo needs $20 million to survive. It must sell new shares at about the market price — say $4. To raise $20 million at $4, it must create **5 million new shares**.

Count the slices. The pizza now has 15 million slices instead of 10 million. Your 1 million shares were 10% of KidCo. They are now 6.7%.

Now run the future. Suppose the crisis passes, and profit recovers fully, to the old $10 million. Confidence recovers fully too, back to a multiple of 15. Everything is healed. What is the share price?

> ($10m ÷ 15m shares) × 15 = $10.

Not $15. Ten dollars. The company fully recovered. The old shareholder who bought at $15 is still down 33%, forever, unless profit grows beyond its old peak. The extra 5 million slices exist permanently. Full recovery of the company is not full recovery of the shareholder.

Sit with that result. It is one of the least understood facts in investing, and it is pure arithmetic.

### How a company survives while its owners lose 80%

Put the whole toy story in one line of arithmetic, because it answers a question that puzzles many beginners: how can a company be alive and well while its long-term shareholders sit on ruinous losses?

Follow one shareholder through the KidCo story. She bought at $15. The crisis took the price to $4 — a 73% loss. Then the raising added 5 million slices at $4. Then the company healed completely: old profit, old confidence. Her share now trades at $10. From $15 to $10 is a 33% permanent loss — after a full recovery.

Now run a harsher, still realistic script. Suppose the crisis forces KidCo to raise twice, and the share count reaches 30 million. Full recovery of profit and confidence then prices the share at ($10m ÷ 30m) × 15 = $5. The company: fully healed, cash-rich, growing. The original shareholder: down 67% from $15, permanently. If profit only half recovers, she is down more than 80%.

Nothing dishonest happened in that script. No fraud, no collapse, no delisting. The company survived precisely by selling pieces of its future, and the pieces came out of existing owners' fractions. **Company survival and shareholder recovery are two different events.** News reports cover the first. Your broker account records the second.

### Who gets offered the new slices

One practical detail completes the picture, because it decides who bears the dilution.

In a **placement**, the company sells new shares directly to large institutions, fast and quietly. Ordinary shareholders find out afterwards. They were diluted without the chance to buy their share of the new slices.

In an **entitlement offer**, every existing shareholder is offered new shares in proportion to their holding. A holder with 1% of the company may buy 1% of the new shares. A holder who takes up the offer keeps their fraction of the pizza. A holder who declines, or who lacks the cash, is diluted like everyone else.

G8's 2020 raising used both: a $227 million placement to institutions, then a $75 million entitlement offer to everyone else. Remember the take-up figure: about one third. Two thirds of the offered slices went unclaimed by the shareholders they were meant for, and passed to underwriters. In a panic, many small holders cannot or will not defend their fraction. The arithmetic of dilution lands on exactly the people least placed to resist it.

### The broken ruler

Here is the same result from a different angle. It gives this book one of its most important tools.

A person looks at KidCo after the recovery and says: "This share was $15 once. It is $10 today. It must be cheap." The person is measuring today's share against an old price.

But the old price belonged to a different object. A share of KidCo in the old days was one ten-millionth of the company. A share today is one fifteen-millionth. The name on the share is the same. The thing itself is smaller.

Measuring with old prices is measuring with a **broken ruler**. The ruler's markings changed when the share count changed. Any measurement against a pre-raising price is a measurement of nothing.

The rule that follows is short: **after any capital raising, throw away every old price**. Value the company fresh: today's profit, today's share count, today's confidence. The Price Formula still works perfectly. It just must be fed today's numbers. This rule is the Broken Ruler, and Chapter 22 applies it to G8's entire price history — including the famous $5.

### Autumn 2020: the real raising

Now the real event, the largest dilution in G8's history.

In early 2020, COVID-19 closed Australia. Parents kept children home. For a period, the government made childcare free and paid operators reduced amounts. Revenue collapsed while rents and core rosters ran on. The Profit Machine of Chapter 1, running near empty, burned cash exactly as the toy model predicts.

G8 moved fast. On 9 April 2020, it announced a capital raising of $301 million. The price of the new shares: **80 cents each**. Two years earlier the shares had traded above $2, and six years earlier above $5. Institutions bought $227 million of new shares within days. Ordinary shareholders were offered $75 million more, and took up only about a third of it — a measure of the fear at the time. Underwriters took the rest, and the raising completed in May 2020.

Count the slices. At 80 cents, $301 million bought about **376 million new shares**. G8 had roughly 460 million shares before. It had roughly 840 million after (sources vary a little on the exact totals; Chapter 19 pins down the precise counts). The share count did not creep. It nearly **doubled** in one month.

Run the Pizza Principle on that. Every future dollar of G8 profit would now divide across nearly twice as many slices. For the price of one share to return to any pre-2020 level, the company would need to earn roughly **twice** its old profit. Not recover — double.

That is why this book keeps insisting on the Broken Ruler. After May 2020, every G8 price chart that starts before 2020 shows two different companies wearing one name. The $5 of 2014 was the price of a slice from a 10-slice pizza. Today's price buys a slice from a 20-slice pizza. The comparison is not pessimistic or optimistic. It is simply void.

### Was the raising wrong?

A fair question: did G8's directors make a mistake in 2020?

The honest answer needs both sides. Selling 376 million slices at 80 cents was brutally expensive for existing owners. But the alternative was worse. A company that runs out of cash does not get to negotiate. It fails, or its bank takes control, and shareholders can lose everything. The directors sold a large piece of the pizza to guarantee there would still be a pizza.

The lesson for the analyst is not "raisings are evil". It is: **the time to think about dilution is before it happens**. A company with thin cash, heavy fixed costs, and falling occupancy is a raising waiting for a date. The shareholder who sees that combination early can decide with open eyes. The shareholder who ignores it wakes up one April morning owning half as much company. Chapter 32 builds the watch-list for exactly this risk.

### The full formula, assembled

With this chapter, the Price Formula is complete. All three parts can now move:

> **Share price = (profit ÷ shares) × confidence**

- Profit: driven by occupancy through the staircase and the lever (Chapters 1–3).
- Confidence: driven by rates, growth, and trust (Chapter 4).
- Shares: driven by capital raisings, at prices set by desperation or strength (this chapter).

Every G8 crash in Part III — and there are five — is some combination of these three parts moving. You now hold the complete toolkit to read each one.

One question remains before we leave the foundations. Everything so far reacts to occupancy. Profit follows it, confidence follows profit, raisings follow cash burn. But what does occupancy itself follow? Is there anything upstream of it — anything that moves first? That question is Chapter 6, and its answer shapes the rest of the book.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** New shares cut the same company into more slices, so after any capital raising, old share prices are broken rulers — measure only with today's count.

---


## Chapter 6 — Lagging vs Leading {#ch06}

*Chapters 1–5 showed everything that follows occupancy. This chapter asks the better question: what does occupancy follow?*

### The chain

Nothing in business happens without a cause, and the causes stand in a line. Here is the line that runs through every childcare company. Read it slowly, left to right:

> **Trust → searches → enquiries → tours → enrolments → occupancy → revenue → profit → price**

Walk through it as one family.

A mother returns to work in four months. She trusts childcare in general, and she has heard nothing bad about the local centre. So she searches online for "childcare near me" (**searches**). She calls two centres to ask about fees and places (**enquiries**). She visits one centre on a Tuesday morning and watches the rooms (**tours**). She signs the forms (**enrolment**). Three months later, her daughter starts, and the centre's **occupancy** ticks up by one child. The fees flow into **revenue** over the following months. The year's revenue settles into **profit**. And months after that, the reported profit feeds the share **price** through the formula of Chapter 4.

Now read the chain with a clock in your hand. From the first search to the first fee can take three to six months. From the first fee to a full-year profit report can take another six to twelve. From trust to price, the chain can run a year and a half.

That clock is the whole point of this chapter.

### History and signal

Split the chain in the middle, at occupancy.

Everything from occupancy rightward — revenue, profit, price — is the **downstream** end. By the time these numbers reach you, the events that caused them are months old. Investors call such numbers **lagging indicators**. They are true, precise, audited — and old. Watching them is driving by the **rear-view mirror**: a perfect view of where you have been.

Everything from occupancy leftward — enrolments, tours, enquiries, searches, trust — is the **upstream** end. These move first. They are the future arriving in small, countable events: one phone call, one Tuesday tour. Investors call these **leading indicators**. They are noisy, unaudited, and scattered — and early. They are the view through the windscreen.

Here is the asymmetry that makes analysis worth doing. The downstream numbers are easy to get and worthless for foresight, because everyone sees them at the same moment, after the fact. The upstream signals are hard to get and precious, because they move months before the reports. Easy and late, or hard and early. The market pays for early.

### The toy timeline

Run a toy shock through the chain to see the clock tick.

Say a frightening news story about a childcare centre — any centre, anywhere — breaks in January. Watch the chain carry it:

- **January:** Trust dips. Some parents planning care simply pause. Searches fall. Phones ring less. Nothing visible happens at any centre.
- **February–March:** Tours thin out. Enrolment forms that would have been signed are not signed. Occupancy has not moved yet, because enrolled children keep attending.
- **April–June:** The missing enrolments start to bite. Children leave for school or family reasons, as they always do, but fewer new children replace them. Occupancy drifts down, month by month.
- **August:** The half-year report shows the fallen occupancy and the profit damage, magnified by the lever of Chapter 3. The share price falls hard in one day.

Now place two observers in this story. Observer A reads company reports. She learns of the problem in August, together with everyone else, at full price. Observer B tracked searches and enquiries. He saw the pause in February — six months early. Same public world. Different ends of the chain.

Observer B did not predict anything. This distinction matters, and Chapter 23 builds a philosophy on it. He simply watched the upstream end of a chain that takes months to flow. The future he "saw" had already happened — in phone calls.

### 4 November 2025, read again

Chapter 4 told the story of G8's November 2025 downgrade as a price event. Now reread it as a chain event, because the company's own words map onto the chain exactly.

In its 4 November update, G8 named the cause of its problem: **weaker enquiry volumes**, as families under cost-of-living pressure held back. Enquiries sit far upstream — third link in the chain. The company then reported the downstream result: occupancy at 68.3%, down 6.6 points on the prior year. Then guidance fell from about $115 million to $91–98 million, and the price fell 13.7% by late morning.

Look at the order inside that single announcement. Enquiries moved first. Occupancy followed. Profit guidance followed occupancy. Price followed guidance — within minutes of the announcement, months after the enquiries. The chain ran in exact textbook order, in public, with dates attached.

Now ask the Observer B question. The enquiry weakness built over months before 4 November. Some of its traces were public the whole time: search interest in childcare brands, web traffic, review activity, the sector's quarterly demand data. A person watching those traces did not know G8's internal enquiry numbers. But that person could suspect the direction — before the 13.7% morning made it official. Part IV is a manual for building exactly that watching apparatus, dial by dial.

### One complication: the crowd watches too

Honesty requires one complication before the picture sets.

The chain says that price moves last. That is true of the mechanical link: price reacts to reports on the day they appear. But the price is set by a crowd, and parts of the crowd also watch upstream. Fund managers call centres. Analysts track hiring and fees. Their trades push the price early, before any report.

So in practice the price is a mixture. Part of it reacts late, to official news. Part of it drifts early, as informed watchers act on upstream signals. This is why share prices often sag for months before a bad announcement, then still fall further on the day. The early part was the watchers. The final drop was everyone else.

G8's 4 November fall showed both parts. The price had already weakened for months — some of the crowd smelled the enquiry problem. The 13.7% morning was the rest of the crowd, arriving at once.

The lesson is not "the market already knows everything". The lesson is a race. Upstream information leaks into the price at the speed of the fastest watchers. Your edge is not secret information. Your edge is watching public upstream signals earlier and more carefully than the average holder of the stock. In a small, unloved company, that average is surprisingly beatable.

### The chain runs upward too

Everything above described bad news flowing down the chain. The same chain carries recovery, in the same order, with the same delays.

When trust returns, searches recover first. Phones ring weeks later. Tours fill after that. Enrolments follow tours, and occupancy climbs only months after the phones first warmed up. The company's reported numbers heal last of all — and the staircase of Chapter 2 means profit heals fast once occupancy moves, because returning children land on wages already paid.

This gives the upstream watcher two jobs, not one. In good times, watch for the first silent link to break. In bad times, watch for the first link to mend. A wounded company whose enquiries have quietly turned up is a different investment from one whose enquiries still fall — even if their occupancy numbers look identical today. Occupancy cannot tell the two apart. The upstream links can.

Mark this asymmetry too: at the bottom, most of the crowd has stopped watching at all. The stock is boring, the news is stale, the holders are exhausted. The first upstream improvements arrive into a silence. That silence is where patient watchers are paid.

### Why everyone does not simply watch upstream

If upstream signals are so valuable, why does the whole market not watch them? Four honest reasons.

**Upstream is noisy.** Searches and enquiries wobble for meaningless reasons: school holidays, weather, a viral video. Any single week of data is nearly worthless. Only patterns over months mean anything, and patterns take patience to collect.

**Upstream is unofficial.** No auditor checks a search-volume chart. You can misread it, and nobody will correct you. Downstream numbers are checked; upstream numbers are yours alone.

**Upstream is indirect.** You never see "trust". You see shadows of it: fewer searches, slower reviews, quieter phones. Connecting shadow to cause takes judgment, and judgment can be wrong. Chapter 24 is an entire chapter about one such mistake — made by this book's own author.

**Upstream requires humility.** The downstream watcher is never embarrassed; he reports what officially happened. The upstream watcher makes claims before confirmation, and is sometimes publicly early, which looks identical to being wrong — until it does not.

This book chooses the upstream trade: noise, doubt, and earliness over comfort, certainty, and lateness. That choice needs tools and discipline. The tools are Part IV. The discipline is Part V.

A first taste of the discipline belongs here, because it names a distinction the whole book turns on. There are two ways to wait on a wounded company. One way watches the upstream links and waits for them to turn — and accepts the answer, whichever way it points. The other way watches the price and waits for it to "come back", because it was higher once. The first is **patience with signals**. The second is **patience with hope**. They feel identical from inside. Only the first one is analysis. Chapter 33 returns to this line and makes it a rule.

### The foundations, complete

Stack the six chapters of Part I into one tower, bottom to top:

1. A childcare centre is a fixed-cost machine; only occupancy feeds it (Chapter 1).
2. The ratio law makes costs fall in steps, slower than revenue (Chapter 2).
3. So small occupancy dips become large profit swings (Chapter 3).
4. Price = profit per share × confidence, so price swings harder still (Chapter 4).
5. Crashes force new shares, which dilute owners and break old rulers (Chapter 5).
6. And occupancy itself is the *last* link of a long chain — everything that matters happens upstream, months earlier (this chapter).

One machine, two levers, one pizza, one chain. That is the complete foundation. Every later chapter applies these six ideas to real ground. If any floor of the tower feels loose, reread its chapter now, because the book will stand on it without further warning.

Next, Part II leaves the single company and studies the playing field: who builds centres, who fills them, who pays, and why the whole sector swings between feast and famine on a timetable of its own.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** Occupancy, revenue, profit, and price are the old end of a chain that starts with trust and enquiries — watch the upstream links, because they move months first.

---



# PART II — THE PLAYING FIELD: AUSTRALIAN CHILDCARE {#part2}


## Chapter 7 — The Pie: Demand {#ch07}

*Part I built the machine. Part II studies the field the machine stands on — starting with the families who feed it.*

### From one centre to the whole country

Part I watched occupancy move and traced the consequences. Part II asks the bigger question: what sets occupancy for the whole sector?

The answer has two halves. Demand: how much childcare Australian families want. Supply: how many places the sector offers. Occupancy is simply the first divided by the second. This chapter measures the demand half. We will call it **the pie** — the total amount of care that families want to buy. The next chapter counts the seats at the table.

### How to measure a pie

"Demand for childcare" sounds vague. Make it concrete with two numbers.

The first number is **children in care** — how many children attend subsidised care at all. In the December quarter of 2025, about 1.44 million Australian children attended subsidised childcare.

The second number is **hours per child**. A child who attends two days a week is half the demand of a child who attends four. In the same quarter, the average child attended about 27.6 hours each week.

Multiply the two, and you get the size of the pie: total hours of care wanted each week. This book prefers a friendlier unit: **child-days**, one child attending for one 10-hour day. Divide total weekly hours by 10, and the pie is about 3.9 to 4.0 million child-days each week (≈). Keep that unit. The book's own gauge in Part IV is built from it.

Now the interesting part: this pie has been growing for decades — while its most obvious ingredient has not.

### The strange case of the missing babies

Common sense says childcare demand should follow births. More babies, more demand. Watch the actual numbers.

Australia registered about 303,000 births in 2010 (≈). Births peaked at 315,147 in 2018. Then they fell: 286,998 in 2023, a low, and 292,318 in 2024. The fertility rate in 2024 was 1.481 births per woman — the lowest ever recorded in Australia, and far below the 2.1 that keeps a population steady without migration.

So the baby supply is roughly flat across fifteen years, and lately falling. Yet the childcare pie grew hugely across the same years. Measured in child-days, demand rose from about 3.1 million each week in 2014 to about 4.0 million in 2024–25 (≈) — growth of roughly 28%, against a flat baby line.

A pie that grows 28% without extra babies needs explaining. Three forces did it, and each one matters to an analyst, because each one has its own future.

### Force one: more of the children go to care

The share of Australian children aged 0 to 12 who attend subsidised care rose from about 30.7% in 2016 to about 35.0% in 2025 (≈). This share is called **participation**.

Behind that dry percentage sit real household changes. More mothers return to paid work, and return earlier. Households need two incomes more than they did. And the informal option shrank: the **grandma shift**. A generation ago, grandparents were the default carers. Today's grandparents live further away, work longer themselves, or retire later. Families who would have used grandma now use a centre.

Participation is powerful because it works on the whole stock of children at once. Births change the pie one year-group at a time. Participation moves every year-group together.

### Force two: each child attends more

The second force is hours. A child in care in the December 2025 quarter averaged about 27.6 hours a week. Hours per child have crept upward over the years, and each extra hour per child is a pure addition to the pie without a single new child.

The forces behind hours are the same household forces: longer working weeks for parents, both parents working, and — as the next chapter of forces shows — subsidy rules that make extra days cheaper.

### Force three: the culture flipped

The third force has no number, and it may be the strongest. Childcare rebranded.

A generation ago, most Australians called it "child minding" — a service you bought reluctantly, because you had to work. Today the sector, the government, and most parents call it **early childhood education**. The research behind the shift is real: the early years matter for development. But notice what the rebrand does to demand. Minding is a cost you avoid if you can. Education is an investment you feel guilty withholding. Families who do not strictly need care now buy it because they believe it is good for the child.

This cultural force lifted both other forces. It pushed participation up, and it pushed hours up. And it changed the customer's mindset in a way that matters for crashes: a parent buying "education" compares centres on quality, not just price and distance. Chapter 17 shows what that did to old centres when shiny new rivals opened next door.

### The quiet sixth force: migration

Honesty requires one more dial, smaller but real. Births are not the only source of children. Australia runs high migration, and migrants are disproportionately young adults — the exact group that has children and needs two incomes. Migration adds children directly, and it adds future parents constantly.

This force matters most at the local level. A new outer-suburban growth corridor can fill with young migrant families in five years, creating a local pie where none existed. An analyst reading only national births would miss it entirely. Keep migration as a background dial: slow nationally, sometimes decisive locally, and — like the subsidy — settable by government policy on an announcement day.

### Reading the pie in practice

Where does an ordinary person actually read this pie? One source dominates, and it is free.

Every quarter, the federal Department of Education publishes the Child Care Subsidy data: how many children attended subsidised care, their average weekly hours, and average fees, by care type and by region. Every demand number in this chapter traces to that series or to the ABS. The data arrives with a lag of a few months — remember Chapter 6's warning about lags — but it is the sector's demand ledger, published for anyone.

Practise the two-step calculation once, because Part IV will automate it. Take the December 2025 quarter: about 1.44 million children × about 27.6 average hours = roughly 39.7 million hours of care each week. Divide by a 10-hour day: about 3.97 million child-days each week (≈). That single number is the national pie, computed from two public figures. Do it each quarter, and you hold a demand series the newspaper never prints.

The same reports break the figures down by state and region, and the regional cut is where analysis sharpens. A flat national pie can hide a growing Queensland and a shrinking Victoria; an operator concentrated in the wrong state lives in a different market from the national average. Chapter 25 builds the local version of the pie properly. For now, note the principle: the national pie sets the mood, but every operator eats from specific regional pies, and the data lets you weigh those separately.

One habit completes the practice: decompose every change. If the pie rises 2%, ask — more children, or more hours per child? A children-led rise is participation or births at work: broad and durable. An hours-led rise often traces to a subsidy rule change: real, but policy-dependent. The December 2025 quarter's decline was children-led — the more structural kind. Same habit in reverse.

### The pie is policy-fed

One more feature of this pie, and it is the strangest one: the government pays for most of it.

Australian childcare fees are heavily subsidised. For most families, the government pays a large share of each hour's fee, directly to the centre. (The exact machinery is Chapter 9.) That means the true price a family pays is set as much in the federal budget as at the centre's front desk.

The consequence: **demand for childcare answers to politics**. A generous budget makes care cheaper and the pie grows. A tightened rule makes care dearer and the pie shrinks. In 2023 and again in January 2026, the rules turned generous — Chapter 9 tells that story. No other ingredient of the pie can change as fast as a subsidy rule can.

For an analyst, this cuts both ways. Policy support makes childcare demand unusually stable — recessions barely dent a subsidised essential. But it also concentrates risk: one budget decision can move the whole national pie, in either direction, on a single announced date.

### The first crack in the pie

For years, every force above pushed one direction: up. In the December 2025 quarter, something new happened. The number of children in subsidised care fell 0.7% from the year before — the first decline in the modern data.

A single quarter is a single quarter, and Chapter 6 taught you not to lean on one data point. But the composition of the dip matters. Births had been weak for years, so the smallest age groups were thinning. Cost-of-living pressure squeezed family budgets, and G8 itself had told the market in November 2025 that enquiries were weakening. And a trust wound — Part III's subject — was pushing some Melbourne families away from long day care entirely.

Whatever the mix, the lesson for this chapter is structural. The pie's growth is not a law of nature. It is the sum of births, participation, hours, culture, and policy — five separate dials, each of which can turn down. An analyst who watches "childcare demand" as one blob sees nothing. An analyst who watches the five dials separately sees which force is moving, and how long it might last.

### What the pie means for the machine

Connect this chapter back to Part I with three short links.

First: the pie is national, but every centre eats locally. A centre in a suburb of young families can starve while the national pie grows. The local pie — the families within a few kilometres — is the one that fills a given machine. Chapter 8 returns to this with a metaphor the book will keep.

Second: the pie moves slowly, with one exception. Births, participation, hours, and culture drift over years. Policy moves on announcement days. If you see the pie jump, look for the policy.

Third: a growing pie hides sins. When demand rises every year, even a badly run centre fills, and even an oversupplied suburb eventually grows into its seats. When the pie flattens — as it just has — every sin surfaces at once. Part III is, among other things, the story of what a flattening pie did to the biggest operator in the country.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** Childcare demand is children × hours, fed by participation, culture, and government subsidy far more than by births — and each of those dials can turn down.

---


## Chapter 8 — The Seats: Supply {#ch08}

*Chapter 7 measured how much care families want. This chapter counts the seats on offer — and meets the strange fact that nobody controls how many get built.*

### Counting the seats

The supply side of childcare has three layers, from big to small.

The first layer is **centres** — the buildings. The kind that matters for this book is called **centre-based day care** in official data: the ordinary long-day childcare centre. Australia had roughly 6,300 of them in 2013 (≈). By December 2024 it had 9,571. By December 2025, 9,786 — still growing at 2.2% in the latest year.

The second layer is **places**. Each centre holds a licensed number of places, exactly like Sunny Corner's 50. Places are the true unit of supply. A year in which centres grow 2% but the new centres are large can add 4% to places.

The centre-versus-places distinction hides a trend worth naming: centres are getting bigger. The typical new build of the recent boom holds 90 to 130 places, against the 40 to 75 of older stock — developers spread fixed land and construction costs across more fees, and the ratio law scales smoothly enough to allow it. So each year's 2% growth in centres has quietly meant more than 2% growth in places, and each new fork in a suburb is a bigger fork than the ones already at the table. When you count supply anywhere in this book, count places where you can, centres only when you must.

The third layer is **licences** (formally, service approvals). Nobody may operate a centre without one. The regulator in each state approves services under the national framework. A licence application in the pipeline today is a centre operating next year — which makes licence data a leading indicator of supply, a fact Part IV will use.

Sit those numbers next to Chapter 7's pie. From 2013 to 2025, seats grew over 50%. The pie, measured in child-days, grew about 28% from 2014. Seats grew faster than the pie for a decade. Remember the definition: occupancy is the pie divided by the seats. The national result was arithmetic destiny: sector occupancy fell — 81.8% in December 2023 to 76.1% in December 2025 on one industry measure, with more history in Part III.

### Forks and pies

Now shrink the picture to one suburb, because childcare is bought locally. A parent will drive perhaps ten minutes. The market that matters to any centre is the families within a couple of kilometres.

Picture the suburb's demand as one pie of fixed size: so many local children, needing so many child-days each week. Every centre in the suburb is a fork in that pie.

One centre, whole pie. A second centre opens: two forks, and unless the pie grows, each fork now lifts a smaller share. A third opens, then a fourth. The pie did not change. Only the number of forks did.

We will call this **Forks and Pies**, and it is the book's model for local competition. Its cruelty is its simplicity. A new centre does not need to be better than yours to hurt you. It only needs to exist. Its fork goes into the same pie, and your slice shrinks by arithmetic, not by any failing of your own.

Now recall the machine from Part I. Sunny Corner breaks even at 86% occupancy in the toy model — real centres commonly between 60% and 80%.
