A new rival that pulls away one child in five can push a healthy centre straight through its break-even line. In a fixed-cost business, Forks and Pies is not a nuisance. It is the difference between profit and loss.

One real example, fresh from the data. In Western Australia, approved places grew about 17.6% in two years to late 2025, while the number of children in care stayed broadly flat. Occupancy fell from 82.3% to 70.5%. Nothing was wrong with WA's centres. There were simply more forks in the same pie.

### A toy suburb

Run Forks and Pies with numbers once, because the arithmetic is the tool you will reuse in Part IV.

Invent the suburb of Millbrook. Its local pie: 1,000 child-days each week — say 250 local children averaging four days. Millbrook has three centres of 70 places each: 210 seats, or 1,050 seat-days each week. Occupancy: 1,000 ÷ 1,050 ≈ **95%**. Every centre is comfortably above break-even. Operators prosper; waiting lists form. Note the irony: those waiting lists are exactly the signal that summons the developer.

Now a developer opens a fourth 70-place centre. Seat-days: 1,400. The pie is unchanged at 1,000. New suburb-wide occupancy: 1,000 ÷ 1,400 ≈ **71%**.

Spread evenly, every centre in Millbrook — including the three that did nothing — fell from 95% to 71%. Check that against Part I: Sunny Corner's toy break-even was 86%, and real centres commonly sit between 60% and 80%. One building, approved on grounds that had nothing to do with need, moved every incumbent from "comfortably profitable" to "fighting at the line". And the pain is permanent until the pie grows: at 3% pie growth a year, Millbrook needs about twelve years to return to 95% — or seven to return to the mid-80s.

The spread is rarely even, of course. The new centre is purpose-built and shiny; it may fill faster by pulling harder on the oldest incumbent — a dynamic Chapter 17 will name the Beauty Contest. But the suburb-level arithmetic binds regardless: the average must fall. Individual excellence redistributes the pain; it cannot repeal it.

### Who decides to build a centre?

Here is the question this chapter exists to answer, and its answer explains half of the sector's history: who controls the number of forks?

Walk through everyone with a hand on the tap.

**The developer** decides to build when the project's numbers work: land price, construction cost, and the rent a childcare tenant will sign for. Notice what is missing from that list — the suburb's occupancy. The developer sells or leases the finished building and moves on. Full rooms are the operator's problem.

**The council** approves the development application. Councils assess traffic, noise, parking, and planning rules. Councils do not assess whether the suburb needs another centre. "The suburb already has six centres" is not a ground for refusal.

**The regulator** grants the service approval. The regulator's questions are about safety and quality: the building, the outdoor space, the qualified staff. The regulator does not ask whether local supply is adequate. That is not its job.

**The subsidy system** pays wherever the parent enrols. The government funds demand; with minor exceptions it does not plan supply. A new centre in an oversupplied suburb receives subsidy flows exactly as an undersupplied one does.

**The operator** — the only party who feels occupancy — often does not choose either. Chains sign leases on buildings that developers have already built. Chapter 11 shows the trap in that choice: refuse the lease and a rival takes the building anyway; the fork goes into the pie either way.

Now add it up. Four parties can each say no to a centre, but each for their own narrow reason. **No party anywhere in the chain says no because the suburb is full.** Australia has no planning gate on childcare supply. The tap has many handles and no owner.

### What an ownerless tap does

An ownerless tap produces a specific pattern, and you have already seen its results in the data above.

When conditions favour building — cheap land finance, hungry property investors, a subsidy boost lifting demand — everyone builds at once. Each developer's project makes sense alone. Nobody's spreadsheet contains the other developers' projects. The suburb gets four new centres in two years, and every operator's occupancy falls, including the incumbents who built nothing.

When conditions turn — construction costs jump, interest rates rise, investors cool — the pipeline freezes just as bluntly. Not because anyone judged that supply was adequate, but because the property arithmetic broke. The 2.2% growth of 2025 is well below the sector's boom-year pace, and the reasons are construction costs and money costs, not any assessment of need. Chapters 12 and 13 give this rhythm its proper name and its timetable.

For now, fix the structural point. In most industries, somebody's judgment eventually restrains supply. Here, supply answers to property mathematics and stops answering to occupancy entirely. The people who feel the pain of oversupply — operators — are not the people deciding to create it.

### Seats are sticky

One more property of childcare supply completes the picture: seats, once built, do not leave.

A purpose-built childcare centre is worth little as anything else. The building has child-height toilets, sleep rooms, fenced yards, and a floor plan no office wants. If its operator fails, the cheapest path for the landlord is to find another operator — at a lower rent if necessary. The seats stay in the suburb. The fork stays in the pie.

Contrast that with the fruit stall of Chapter 1, which simply stops trading, or with industries where a failed factory is scrapped. Childcare oversupply does not self-correct through exits, or corrects only slowly and partially. The sector's great collapse — Chapter 14's ghost story — removed a company and barely removed a seat.

The stickiness has a second face: conversion in, never out. Houses and offices convert into childcare centres readily — the renovation is routine, and boom years saw plenty of it. Childcare centres convert back into almost nothing. The tap, in other words, has a one-way valve fitted at the drain.

For the analyst, this stickiness sets the timetable of recovery. When a suburb is oversupplied, the fix is not "weak centres close and balance returns". The fix is years of pie growth slowly filling the extra seats. Count the extra seats, estimate the pie's growth rate, and you can estimate the years. That calculation — supply seen years ahead — is one of the most reliable tools in Part IV, precisely because seats move so slowly and so visibly.

### The field, half-mapped

Two chapters into Part II, the field looks like this. Demand: a slow-moving pie, fed by five dials, of which policy is the fastest. Supply: seats that anyone can add, nobody restrains, and almost nobody removes.

What happens when the fast dial of demand — the subsidy — meets the ownerless tap of supply? That collision has a rhythm, and the rhythm has burned childcare investors twice in twenty years. It needs two chapters: first the subsidy machinery itself, then the sword with two edges.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** Every new centre is another fork in a fixed local pie, and no one in the approval chain — developer, council, regulator, or subsidy — ever says no because a suburb is full.

---


## Chapter 9 — The Subsidy Engine {#ch09}

*Chapter 7 said the pie is policy-fed. This chapter opens the feeding machine and traces one dollar through it.*

### The strangest customer in the room

Ask who a childcare centre's customer is, and the obvious answer is the parent. But follow the money, and a stranger appears. For a typical Australian family, the government pays most of the fee — and pays it straight to the centre.

This chapter traces how a dollar travels from the federal budget to a centre's bank account, and what that journey does to demand. We will call the whole apparatus **the Subsidy Engine**. You cannot understand any Australian childcare company without it. Roughly speaking, the taxpayer is the sector's majority customer.

### One dollar's journey

The subsidy's current form is the **Child Care Subsidy**, or CCS, which started in July 2018. Here is the journey, step by step, with a toy family.

**Step one: the budget.** Parliament allocates billions each year to the CCS. The money exists before any child enrols. It flows only when parents enrol children — the subsidy funds demand, not buildings.

**Step two: the family's percentage.** Each family gets a CCS percentage, set mainly by household income. Lower incomes get the highest rates — up to 90% since July 2023. Higher incomes get less, tapering down as income rises. Say our toy family, the Nguyens, qualify for 70%.

**Step three: the capped hourly fee.** The subsidy applies to the centre's hourly fee — but only up to a ceiling, the **hourly rate cap**. In late 2025 the cap for centre-based care was $14.63 per hour. If a centre charges below the cap, the percentage applies to the actual fee. If it charges above, the percentage applies to the cap, and the family pays every cent above it. The average centre-based fee in late 2025 was about $14.40 (≈) — just under the cap. That closeness is not an accident; the cap acts as a magnet for fees, a point Chapter 27 turns into a signal.

**Step four: the money splits.** Say the Nguyens' centre charges $14.00 an hour, and their child attends 30 hours a week: $420. The government pays 70% — $294 — directly to the centre. The Nguyens pay the remaining $126. That remainder is called the **gap fee**, and the gap fee is the only price the family ever feels.

**Step five: demand responds.** The Nguyens never weigh $420 against their budget. They weigh $126. Childcare that "costs" $37,000 a year in headline terms feels like $11,000. At that felt price, a third day for their younger child looks affordable. The pie of Chapter 7 grows a slice.

Read the journey again and notice its shape. A budget decision in Canberra changes a percentage; the percentage changes the gap fee; the gap fee changes the number of days families buy. **The federal budget is wired directly to the demand dial** — with no valve anywhere on the supply side.

### Three announcements that moved the pie

The engine has been rebuilt twice and re-tuned once in a decade. Each date matters to our story, and each shows the engine's power.

**July 2018 — the CCS itself.** The CCS replaced a messier pair of older payments. Its design sharpened the link between income, activity, and subsidy, and its arrival lifted effective support for most working families. The sector treated it as a demand event, and Part III shows operators — G8 included — counting on it to fill seats. It arrived, note well, while a building boom was already flooding the other side of the equation.

**July 2023 — Cheaper Child Care.** The maximum CCS rate rose to 90%, and rates rose across most income levels. The felt price of care dropped noticeably in a single month. Demand did what a price cut always does. For operators, 2023–24 brought the tailwind of fuller rooms — and, as Chapter 10 will warn, a tailwind of exactly the kind that summons builders.

**5 January 2026 — the 3 Day Guarantee.** The old system tied subsidised hours to a parental **activity test**: broadly, the less you worked or studied, the fewer subsidised hours you got. From January 2026 the test was abolished. Every eligible family is now guaranteed subsidised care for three days a week — 72 hours a fortnight — regardless of work status, with more for some families. Families who previously qualified for one day, or none, can now afford three. The engine reached, deliberately, for the families the old rules excluded.

Three dates, one pattern: the pie jumped on announcement days, by government choice, in a way births and culture never can. When Chapter 7 said policy is the fastest dial, this is what it meant.

### The Nguyens, before and after

Watch one family cross a rule change, because it shows the engine moving a household — and a sector — in one step.

It is 2025. Mrs Nguyen's sister, Linh, is not in paid work; she cares for her mother and studies part-time. Under the old activity test, her limited recognised "activity" entitles her child to few subsidised hours — in practice, about one day a week. Extra days would cost the full unsubsidised fee, roughly $140 a day. On one income, that is prohibitive. Her son attends one day.

Now it is 5 January 2026. The activity test is gone. Linh's family is guaranteed 72 subsidised hours a fortnight — three days a week — at her high subsidy rate, since the household income is low. Her out-of-pocket cost for a day falls to roughly $15–20 (≈). She books the extra days: good for her son, and space to study.

Multiply Linh by the hundreds of thousands of families the old test constrained, and you see what an announcement day does. No new child was born; no parent changed jobs. A rule moved, and the pie grew — concentrated, note, among lower-income families, and in the suburbs where they live. The engine does not just resize demand. It redraws its map. An analyst tracking a specific operator must ask each time: does the new demand land where this operator's seats are?

### How big is the engine, really?

One number puts the whole chapter in scale. The federal government budgets well over $13 billion a year for the Child Care Subsidy (≈, and rising with each reform). Set that against a sector whose largest operator — G8, with about 4% of the nation's centres — collects under $1 billion of annual revenue. The subsidy line in the federal budget is an order of magnitude larger than the biggest company in the industry it feeds.

For most families the CCS covers half to nearly all of the fee, so a majority of a typical centre's revenue arrives from the Commonwealth, routed via parents' entitlements. Hence this chapter's opening claim, now cashed out: the taxpayer is the sector's majority customer. When you analyse an Australian childcare operator, you are analysing a business whose dominant revenue source is a single government program. That is a strength — no customer is more creditworthy — and a concentration risk that belongs in every thesis, because programs have parliaments, and parliaments have budgets.

### The engine's fine print

Three details of the machinery matter enough for an analyst to hold onto.

**The cap disciplines fees — up to a point.** Because subsidy stops at $14.63, every dollar a centre charges above the cap lands raw on the family. Centres therefore cluster near the cap. But the cap itself is indexed upward over time, and each rise gives the whole sector permission to move. Watch also the special deal of 2024–25: the government funded a 15% wage rise for educators — 10% from December 2024, 5% more from December 2025 — and in exchange, participating operators accepted caps on fee growth of 4.4% in the first year and 4.2% in the second. Wages up by right, fees up by permission slip: the squeeze of Chapter 3's "moving line", now written into policy.

**The engine pays only for attendance.** No child, no subsidy. This is why the engine funds demand and not supply — and why an empty new centre in an oversupplied suburb gets nothing until it wins children. The engine never rescues an operator directly. It rescues the *price* the parent faces.

**The engine can run in reverse.** Everything given on an announcement day can be tightened on one. Means tests can bite harder; caps can lag fees; a future budget under pressure can trim percentages. The sector plans as if the engine only ever turns one way. History says engines answer to parliaments, and parliaments change.

### Why the government keeps feeding the engine

A machine this expensive survives because it serves powerful purposes, and an analyst should know them, because they signal how durable the spending is.

The economic purpose is **workforce participation**. Every subsidised place lets a parent — usually a mother — work more hours. Treasury counts that as growth. The social purpose is **early education**: the evidence that quality early learning pays off, especially for disadvantaged children, is the stated ground for the 3 Day Guarantee. The political purpose is the bluntest: childcare costs are a top-tier household issue, and promising cheaper care wins votes across the spectrum.

Three purposes, all durable. This is why the book treats the Subsidy Engine as structurally safe in a way few subsidies are — while refusing to treat any single setting of it as permanent. The engine will very likely persist. Its dials will certainly move.

The practical habit follows: put the two annual budget nights — federal budget in May, mid-year update in December — on your analytical calendar, alongside election campaigns. Those are the nights the engine's settings can change. Chapter 28 files them under reform announcements: scheduled moments when the fastest dial of demand is on the table.

### The engine and the machine

Close the loop back to Part I.

For the Profit Machine, the Subsidy Engine is the great stabiliser of revenue. Demand for a subsidised essential barely flinches in recessions; the taxpayer stands behind two-thirds of the fee line. Banks lend to childcare landlords, and REITs sign 15-year leases, precisely because the tenant's customer is, in effect, the Commonwealth. Chapter 11 introduces the players who price that comfort.

But you have now seen both halves of Part II's field. The engine pumps demand, on announcement days, to everyone equally. The tap of supply — Chapter 8's ownerless tap — answers to none of it directly, but responds eventually, through property mathematics, to the fuller rooms the engine creates.

Pump demand fast. Let supply answer slowly, massively, and without restraint. Do it on a national scale, twice a decade. What could go wrong is the next chapter — the sword with two edges.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** The subsidy engine wires the federal budget straight to the demand dial — it moves the whole pie on announcement days, and it can move it either way.

---


## Chapter 10 — The Double-Edged Sword {#ch10}

*Chapter 9 showed the engine that pumps demand. This chapter shows what the pump does, two to four years later, to the people it helped.*

### The sword, named

Put Chapters 8 and 9 side by side and a mechanism appears.

A subsidy boost lands. Gap fees fall. Families buy more days. Rooms fill across the country. Every operator's occupancy climbs, and — through the staircase and the lever of Part I — profits jump. That is the first edge of the sword, and it cuts in the operators' favour.

Then the second edge swings. Fuller rooms and better profits change the property arithmetic of Chapter 8. Developers' spreadsheets light up. Investors bid for childcare buildings. Councils receive applications, regulators receive approvals, and two to four years after the subsidy boost, the new centres open — all at once, wherever the boom maths worked. Forks flood into pies. Occupancy falls back, and can fall below where it started.

We will call this **the Double-Edged Sword**: every subsidy that lifts demand also, with a delay, summons the supply that destroys the lift. The first edge arrives by announcement. The second arrives by construction schedule.

### Why the two edges never meet

The sword exists because the two sides of the market answer to different masters and run on different clocks. This is the deepest structural fact in Part II, so take it slowly.

**Demand answers to budgets.** Chapter 9 traced the wiring: percentage → gap fee → days bought. When the subsidy moves, demand moves within months. The response is fast, national, and simultaneous.

**Supply answers to property maths.** Chapter 8 traced that wiring too: land price, build cost, achievable rent, and the investor's required return. Occupancy appears nowhere in it. When fuller rooms lift achievable rents, the property maths improves — but the response takes years. Land must be found, applications approved, buildings built. The response is slow, local, and staggered.

Now the key sentence: **no one stands between the two clocks.** The budget office that boosts demand does not model the construction wave it will trigger. The developer riding the wave does not model the budget cycle. Each side optimises its own arithmetic. The collision is nobody's plan and everybody's outcome.

In most markets, price would referee the collision — oversupply would cut prices, losses would stop entry. Childcare muffles that referee. Fees cluster at the subsidy cap rather than falling when rooms empty; the engine holds up the price side even in oversupply. And Chapter 8 showed that seats, once built, do not leave. So the collision resolves the only way left: through occupancy. Supply overshoots, and the whole sector's fullness — the very variable that decides profit in a fixed-cost business — absorbs the blow.

### The sweet spot

The sword's timing creates a rhythm an analyst can actually use, so mark its phases.

**Phase one — the announcement (year 0).** The subsidy boost lands. Demand jumps. Existing operators harvest the first edge: fuller rooms on unchanged costs, the staircase working in their favour. Profits and moods improve together.

**Phase two — the sweet spot (roughly years 0 to 2).** The new demand is real; the answering supply is still in councils and construction sites. These are the best years to own childcare seats. The trap is psychological: everyone in the sector extrapolates the sweet spot forward. Operators sign new leases, investors pay up for centres, and forecasts assume the fullness is permanent.

**Phase three — the flood (roughly years 2 to 4).** The construction wave lands. New centres open into the demand the subsidy created, and then keep opening past it, because projects started in the sweet spot cannot un-start. Occupancy sags nationally and craters in the suburbs where building was easiest.

**Phase four — the hangover (years 4 and beyond).** The pipeline empties, because the property maths broke. The sector waits for the pie's slow growth to fill the extra seats. How long the hangover lasts depends on how far supply overshot — and on when the next subsidy boost restarts the cycle.

The phases are approximate, and real history overlaps them. But the ordering is structural: announcement, sweet spot, flood, hangover. It follows from the two clocks, and the two clocks are not going away.

### Millbrook rides the sword

Run one full cycle through Chapter 8's toy suburb, with dates, so the phases become concrete.

**Year 0.** Millbrook stands at its crowded equilibrium: four centres, 1,400 seat-days, a 1,000 child-day pie, occupancy 71%. Operators scrape along near break-even. Then a subsidy boost lands. Gap fees fall; local families add days; the pie swells 15% to 1,150. Occupancy: 82%.

**Years 0–2, the sweet spot.** At 82%, every centre is clear of the line. Profits return. The operators breathe; one renovates. But watch the other spreadsheets. The achievable rent on a Millbrook childcare building has risen with its occupancy, and two developers, working separately, option sites on opposite sides of the suburb. Each spreadsheet clears its hurdle. Neither contains the other.

**Year 3, the flood.** Both centres open within months of each other: 140 new places, seat-days now 1,960. The pie, its subsidy jump digested, has crept to about 1,200. Occupancy: 61% — below where the suburb started, below every centre's break-even. The subsidy boost has ended, three years on, with every operator in Millbrook worse off than before it — and six forks in the pie instead of four, permanently.

**Years 4 and beyond, the hangover.** No further centre is proposed; the auction market has cooled. At 3% pie growth, Millbrook needs about sixteen years to see 82% again. The seats wait. The landlords collect. The operators endure.

Toy numbers, but no step exaggerated: Chapter 8's Western Australia ran this exact script at state scale, with places up 17.6% in two years into a flat pie. When the book says the sweet spot is the dangerous time, Millbrook's year 1 is the picture to hold: profits at their best, and both rival sites already optioned.

### The sword in the recent data

You have already seen the sword's edges in this book's verified numbers. Line three of them up.

July 2023: Cheaper Child Care lifts the maximum subsidy to 90% — a demand boost, phase one. Through 2023–24, rooms fill: national centre-based occupancy stood at 81.8% in December 2023 — a sweet spot reading.

Then the second edge. Centres nationally grew to 9,786 by December 2025, and in the friendliest states the growth ran hottest — Western Australia added about 17.6% to its approved places in two years. Into flat local demand, that was pure new forks. WA occupancy fell from 82.3% to 70.5%; nationally, 76.1% by December 2025. Phase three, on schedule, roughly two years after the boost.

And the next swing is already wound up. The 3 Day Guarantee of January 2026 is another demand boost — phase one again. The construction response to *it* will land around 2028, into whatever the sector then looks like. The sword does not care that the sector is wounded. The clocks just run.

One honesty note: 2023–25 was not a clean laboratory. Cost-of-living pressure squeezed demand from below, a trust crisis (Part III) hit one operator's demand directly, and construction costs slowed the supply response compared with earlier cycles. The sword explains the shape of the data, not every wiggle in it. Chapter 24 has more to say about the danger of explaining every wiggle.

### Why nobody waits out the sweet spot

If the phases are this legible, why does the sector never simply sit still through phase two? Because the sweet spot pressures every player to act, and the pressures are personal.

The chain executive faces the growth imperative of Chapter 11: the market pays a growth multiple, rivals are signing the developers' leases, and a CEO who banks the sweet-spot profits while shrinking ambition is a CEO explaining a sagging share price to the board. The independent owner sees the strongest trading of her life and the highest price her centre will ever fetch — and as often as not, doubles down instead, adding a second site at boom-era rent. The analyst, meanwhile, is professionally punished for standing aside during the two best earning years of the cycle. "Early" and "wrong" are indistinguishable in a quarterly world.

So the sweet spot converts its own prosperity into commitments — leases, builds, forecasts — that mature in phase three. Understanding the sword does not exempt anyone from its incentives. It only offers the reader, who answers to no board and no quarterly ranking, the one privilege the professionals lack: permission to date the announcement, watch the paperwork, and wait.

### Who the sword cuts

Notice who bleeds in phase three, because it is not who caused the flood.

The developer who built the new centres was paid on completion — Chapter 11 shows why his profit never depended on a single enrolment. The landlord holds a 15-year lease. The council collected its rates, the bank its interest. The subsidy kept flowing to wherever parents enrolled.

The blood comes from operators — all of them. Incumbents who built nothing lose occupancy to the new forks. And the worst-cut operator is the big incumbent with old buildings: it has the most seats exposed, in the most suburbs, and the new rivals are purpose-built and shiny. Hold that thought through Part III, because between 2016 and 2018 G8 Education was exactly that operator, standing in exactly that phase of the cycle.

Shareholders bleed one step behind the operators, through the levers of Part I: occupancy → profit (Chapter 3) → confidence (Chapter 4). And the cycle's cruellest trick is that shareholders who arrive in the sweet spot — when profits look best and forecasts look safest — are buying at precisely the point the second edge is being forged.

### Using the sword instead of bleeding on it

Turn the mechanism into analytical habits — three of them.

First, **date every demand boost, and start a clock.** A subsidy announcement is not just good news for operators. It is the starting gun of a supply cycle. Write down the date; expect the flood in years two to four; treat sweet-spot profits as the cycle's peak until proven otherwise.

Second, **watch the supply clock directly.** The beauty of the second edge is that it is visible years ahead — in council applications, service-approval pipelines, and construction data. Chapter 26 builds this into a dashboard dial. Demand shocks surprise; supply floods announce themselves in paperwork.

Third, **judge every childcare profit number against the cycle's phase.** The same 80% occupancy means one thing in a hangover with an empty pipeline, and the opposite thing in a sweet spot with a full one. A number without its phase is half a fact.

The sword is the playing field's master rhythm. But rhythms play through people, and the field is crowded with them — developers, landlords, banks, councils, parents — each acting on private arithmetic. Time to meet them properly.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** Every subsidy boost summons, two to four years later, the supply that destroys it — demand answers to budgets, supply answers to property maths, and no one referees the collision.

---


## Chapter 11 — Meet the Players: How Each Side Thinks {#ch11}

*Chapter 10 showed a cycle that nobody plans. This chapter meets the nine players whose separate, sensible choices add up to it.*

### Why motives matter

An analyst who knows what each player optimises can predict behaviour without inside information. None of the nine players below is villainous. Each acts rationally on the numbers in front of them. The sector's strangeness — supply that ignores occupancy, seats that never leave — is just their nine rationalities added together. Meet them in the order the money moves.

### The developer

The developer buys land, wins approval, and builds the centre. His profit arrives when he sells or leases the finished building. **A developer's profit never depends on a single child enrolling.** By opening day, he has been paid.

What he optimises: the spread between total project cost and the building's value once a childcare tenant signs. The value rises with the rent on the lease, so his incentive is to secure the longest lease at the highest rent any operator will sign. Whether that operator can fill the rooms afterwards is, structurally, not his problem. When Chapter 8 said supply answers to property maths, this is whose maths it meant.

### The landlord and the childcare REITs

The finished centre is usually sold to an investor who wants rent, not childcare. At the top of this class sit the listed childcare landlords — **Arena REIT** and **Charter Hall Social Infrastructure REIT** — each owning hundreds of centres. (A REIT, a real estate investment trust, is a listed company that owns buildings and passes rent to shareholders.)

What the landlord optimises: long, secure, rising rent. Childcare leases commonly run around 15 years (≈), with rent that steps up automatically each year. The tenant's fees are subsidy-backed, so the rent is, at one remove, taxpayer-backed — the most secure retail-sized tenant in property. Notice what this player structurally cannot care about: the operator's occupancy, so long as the rent clears. If an operator fails, the landlord's playbook is Chapter 8's: install the next operator, cheaper if needed. The seats stay.

For the analyst, the REITs are also a gift: they publish. Their reports disclose rents, lease lengths, vacancy, and re-leasing terms — a public window into the sector's property spine. Chapter 26 taps it.

### The bank

Behind the developer and the landlord stands a lender. What the bank optimises: secure interest. A childcare building offers what banks love — a long lease, a physical asset, and a tenant whose income is government-subsidised. So credit flows readily to childcare property, in good times almost eagerly.

The bank's blind spot is the same as the landlord's, one step further removed: it lends against the lease, not against the suburb's occupancy. In the boom phase of the cycle, bank credit is the fuel line feeding the ownerless tap.

### The mum-and-dad property investor

Below the REITs sits a large retail class: ordinary investors who buy a single childcare centre at auction, often with retirement savings. What they optimise: **yield** — annual rent divided by purchase price — compared against the term deposit rate they left behind.

When interest rates fall, this player's hunger sharpens (Chapter 12 is entirely about this), and auction prices for childcare buildings climb. Every strong auction result feeds back to the developer: build more, the exit is hot. The retail investor never sees an occupancy figure in the auction brochure. She sees "15-year lease to national operator, government-backed sector". She is the demand side of the property market whose supply side floods the childcare market.

### The operator

At last, the player who actually runs the Profit Machine — and the only one whose income depends on occupancy. Operators split into **chains** (G8 and its peers, plus the non-profit giant Goodstart) and **independents** (single-centre owners, the majority of the market).

Scale the two camps first, because outsiders always overestimate the chains. G8's roughly 395 centres are about 4% of the nation's 9,786 — and it is the largest listed operator. Goodstart's centres bring the two giants together to little more than a tenth of the market. The majority of Australian childcare is still owned in ones and twos: a director-owner, a family company, a community committee. This fragmentation matters analytically. It means no operator sets market prices; it means the lease-signing decisions that flood a suburb are made by hundreds of small hands as well as a few large ones; and it means a listed chain's occupancy can diverge from the sector's, in either direction, for reasons of its own portfolio.

The chain optimises portfolio profit; the independent optimises one centre she often works in herself. Both live inside every constraint of Part I: ratio law, staircase costs, leverage. But the chain carries one structural burden the independent escapes: it must keep growing to justify its stock-market multiple (Chapter 4), and growth means signing more of the developers' leases.

Here is **the lease trap**, promised in Chapter 8. A developer offers a chain a new building two kilometres from the chain's existing centre. Accept, and the chain's new centre cannibalises its own — one more fork in its own pie. Refuse, and the developer leases it to a rival, and the fork lands anyway, in someone else's colours. The rational chain often accepts, reasoning that self-cannibalisation at least keeps the revenue in-house. Multiply that logic across every chain at once, and the flood of Chapter 10 builds itself — each operator defensively signing the leases that collectively drown them all.

### The council

The council approves the development application. It optimises compliance and local politics: traffic, parking, noise, heritage. Childcare scores well on all of them — quiet, daytime-only, employment-generating, and popular with young-family voters. Approvals are therefore comparatively fast, and market need is not a criterion. The council is a gate that stands open.

### The government

The Commonwealth runs the Subsidy Engine, and Chapter 9 gave its three motives: workforce participation, early education, votes. Add the fourth face it shows operators: **the safety mandate**. Through the national framework, government sets ratios, qualifications, and quality ratings — the very rules that create Staircase Costs. The government is thus simultaneously the sector's paymaster, its regulator, and its most important customer's sponsor. It is not, remember, the planner of its supply. Nobody is.

### The educator

The educator does the actual work, at award-anchored wages historically low for its responsibility. What she optimises, the sector learned the hard way: pay matters, but so do conditions, respect, and stability. For years the sector ran a chronic shortage — qualified educators could pick their employer, and centres competed for them like customers.

The educator is where the ratio law becomes a market force. A centre cannot legally open rooms it cannot staff. In the shortage years, occupancy was sometimes capped not by demand but by hiring. The 15% wage deal of 2024–25 (Chapter 9) was the government leaning directly on this player's scarcity — and Chapter 29 will use job ads and vacancy rates as a dial on the whole machine.

### The parent

Finally, the only real customer — the only player whose choice creates revenue. Everyone else in this chapter is paid *because* a parent chose a centre.

What the parent optimises is worth stating carefully, because it is not price. Surveys and behaviour agree on the ranking: safety and trust first, absolutely and non-negotiably; then location and hours; then quality as the parent perceives it — ratings, staff warmth, the feel of a tour; then price, dulled by the Subsidy Engine to a gap fee. A centre can be cheap, close, and excellent, and lose the child in one moment if trust breaks. The parent's veto is total, immediate, and beyond appeal.

Note also what the parent is not: informed the way the other players are. She tours twice, reads some reviews, and decides on an hour's evidence and a feeling. This is rational — she has a job and a baby, not a research department — but it means her vote moves on impressions, ratings, and word of mouth, and it can move in herds.

Part I called occupancy the last link before the money. This chapter ends by renaming it: occupancy is the running total of parents' votes, and the parent votes on trust first. When Part III reaches 2025, you will see what happens to the whole edifice — developers, landlords, banks, REITs, chains — when trust itself is what breaks.

### Using the board: three predictions

A motive map earns its keep by predicting behaviour. Test it three times, on situations this book will actually reach.

**Prediction one: a struggling operator asks its landlord for rent relief.** Read the landlord's motive — long secure rising rent — and the answer writes itself. Meaningful rent cuts would mark down the value of every comparable lease in the portfolio; far better to hold terms, offer at most a deferral, and if the operator fails, re-let the building to the next chain at a modest discount. Prediction: rent relief will be rare, late, and shallow, even amid operator distress. Keep this in mind when Part III shows G8 negotiating with hundreds of landlords at once.

**Prediction two: a safety scandal hits the sector.** Read the government's motives — participation, education, votes, and the safety mandate. A scandal threatens the votes and the mandate at once, so the response will be visible, fast, and regulatory: inquiries, tightened rules, new compliance duties on operators. The demand side — the subsidy — will if anything be defended harder, because the program is popular. Prediction: scandals raise operators' costs and scrutiny while leaving the engine running. Chapter 21 tests this against 2025's reality.

**Prediction three: money gets cheap again.** Read the developer, the bank, and the yield-hungry investor together, and the tap reopens — regardless of current occupancy, because occupancy appears in none of their arithmetic. Prediction: the next cheap-money era will seed the next oversupply, even if the sector enters it wounded. The only genuine restraint would be a player changing its rulebook — a council weighing saturation, a lender demanding occupancy data. Watch for those changes; do not assume them.

The method generalises. For any sector event, ask player by player: what does this do to the number each one optimises? The board's response, summed, is your forecast — no insider access required.

### The board, assembled

Nine players. Line up what each one watches: the developer, his margin; the landlord, her lease; the bank, its security; the investor, her yield; the operator, its occupancy; the council, its rulebook; the government, its three purposes; the educator, her offer; the parent, her child.

Only one of the nine watches occupancy. That asymmetry *is* the childcare sector. Now, one force moves every player on this board at once — the price of money itself.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** Nine rational players build the childcare cycle, and only one of them — the operator — is paid according to whether the rooms are full.

---


## Chapter 12 — Interest Rates: The Tide Under Everything {#ch12}

*Chapter 11 met nine players, each with private arithmetic. This chapter shows the one number that sits inside all nine spreadsheets.*

### The price of money

An **interest rate** is the price of money: what you pay each year to borrow it, or earn to park it. In Australia the anchor is the **cash rate**, set by the Reserve Bank (the RBA). Every other rate — mortgages, business loans, term deposits — moves in loose formation with it.

Most people meet interest rates through a mortgage. This chapter's claim is larger: **the cash rate is the tide under the whole childcare sector**. When it moves, every player of Chapter 11 tilts, all in the same direction, at the same time. A childcare centre, seen from the property market, is secretly an interest-rate instrument.

To see why, follow one number through three spreadsheets.

### The yield spreadsheet

Start with the mum-and-dad investor at auction, because her arithmetic is the simplest and moves first.

She has $2 million. A term deposit pays her the going rate risk-free. A childcare centre offers rent of $130,000 a year — a **yield** of 6.5% on her $2 million.

Set the term deposit at 5%. The centre pays 1.5% more, for the trouble of owning a building. Interesting, not compelling. Now let the RBA cut until deposits pay 1%. Nothing at the centre changed — same rent, same lease — but the comparison transformed. The centre now pays six times the deposit. She bids. So does everyone like her.

Here is the mechanism in one line: **when rates fall, buyers accept lower yields, so prices of rent-paying assets rise.** If bidding pushes the centre's price to $2.6 million, the same $130,000 rent is a yield of 5% — still handsome against 1% deposits. The rent did not grow. The tide lifted the price.

Every player upstream of her reads that auction result. The developer of Chapter 11 sees his exit price rising; projects that failed at a 6.5% exit yield succeed at 5%. The bank sees strong collateral and lends into it. The landlord REITs see their portfolios revalue upward and their borrowing costs fall, and they expand. Cheap money does not merely encourage childcare construction. It re-prices the entire chain until construction is the obvious choice.

### The revaluation, in numbers

Before moving on, price the tide's effect exactly once, because the size surprises people.

The auction logic compresses to one formula: **price = rent ÷ required yield**. The centre paying $130,000 rent, priced by buyers demanding 6.5%: $130,000 ÷ 0.065 = $2.0 million. The same centre when the crowd accepts 5%: $130,000 ÷ 0.05 = **$2.6 million** — a 30% price rise from a 1.5-point yield shift, with not one extra child in the building. Push to the cheap-money extreme, 4%: $3.25 million. From 6.5% to 4% — the kind of drift Australian childcare yields actually made across the 2010s (≈) — the identical building gains more than 60%.

Now reverse it. The investor who paid $3.25 million at the bottom of the tide, facing buyers who once again demand 6.5%, holds an asset the market prices at $2.0 million — a 38% loss with the rent still paid in full, every month, without fail. Nothing failed except the era.

This single division explains most of what puzzles outsiders about childcare property: why auction prices soared while operators struggled, why developers built into visible oversupply, and why the same building can be a triumph and a disaster for two successive owners who both collected identical rent. The denominator moved. In any leased-asset market, the denominator is the tide.

### The tide goes out

Now run the machinery in reverse, because reverse is where our story goes.

Rates rise. Deposits pay 5% again. The auction buyer demands a higher yield, so she offers less for the same rent — asset prices fall. The developer's exit price sags while his other line items — construction costs and his own borrowing — climb. Project spreadsheets that sang at 0.1% go silent at 4%. The bank tightens. The pipeline freezes.

Notice three properties of this reversal, because they explain the sector's history.

It is **indiscriminate**. The tide does not study suburbs. It lifts every project in the boom and strands every project in the tightening, regardless of local need. The ownerless tap of Chapter 8 is opened and closed by a hand that is not even looking at childcare.

It is **delayed**. Buildings started in the boom still open during the tightening — the flood of Chapter 10 arrives at the worst possible moment, into a sector whose financing has already turned.

And it reaches **the share market too**. Recall Chapter 4: the multiple is fed by the alternative — what money earns elsewhere. Rising rates compress the multiples of listed operators at the same time as the property tide strands their landlords. The same tide moves the price of the building and the price of the shares.

### The same tide in the share market

Before the history, close the third loop — the one running through Chapter 4 — with toy numbers, because it moves the asset this book's reader actually buys.

Recall the Price Formula: share price = profit per share × the multiple, and the multiple's first feeder is the alternative return on money. Make that concrete. When deposits pay 1%, a dollar of steady company profit is precious; the crowd might pay 18 years for it. When deposits pay 5%, the same dollar competes with an effortless alternative; the crowd might pay 12.

Now put a toy operator, KidCo again, in both worlds. Same company, same $1 of profit per share, same rooms, same children. At the bottom of the tide: $1 × 18 = $18. At the top: $1 × 12 = $12. A 33% share-price fall with no change whatsoever in the business. Layer this onto what rates simultaneously do to the operator's world — stranding its landlords, freezing rival supply (a help), pinching mortgage-stressed parents (a hurt) — and you see why disentangling "what did rates do" from "what did the business do" is a standing task in Part V's valuation work.

The practical habit: whenever you compare a company's multiple with its own past — "GEM traded at 20 times in 2014, only 8 now" — check the tide first. Part of every multiple belongs to the era's interest rates, not to the company. Comparing multiples across rate regimes without adjusting is a subtler cousin of the Broken Ruler: measuring with an instrument that was itself recalibrated between readings.

### The Australian tide, 2011–2026

Now put real dates on the tide, because Part III will hang G8's whole history on them. The figures are the RBA's cash rate, rounded (≈).

**2011: about 4.75%.** Ordinary money. Childcare property is a niche.

**2012–2016: cut after cut, to 1.5%.** The tide starts rising. Yield hunger builds. Childcare — long lease, subsidised tenant — emerges as a retail investor darling.

**2016–2019: held at 1.5%, then cut again.** These are the years the flood of centres built on cheap money lands on the sector (and, as Part III will show, on G8's doorstep). Note the overlap with the CCS launch of July 2018: demand boost and cheap-money supply boom, colliding.

**2020–2021: emergency. 0.1%.** COVID crashes rates to almost zero. Money is nearly free. Even as the sector's operators bleed (Chapter 19's story), the property machine gorges: this is the peak of the 2015–2021 cheap-money era that built the modern oversupply.

**May 2022 – November 2023: the fastest tightening in a generation, 0.1% to 4.35%.** The tide goes out in eighteen months. Construction costs, already inflated, meet dear money. The pipeline freezes — Chapter 8's 2.2% supply growth of 2025, against the boom years, is this freeze in the data.

**2025–2026: partial relief, cuts toward about 3.6% (≈).** Money cheaper than the peak, still far from free. The pipeline thaws slowly, if at all; construction costs remain high. Where the tide goes next is unknown — to this book and to everyone.

Read that timeline against the Double-Edged Sword and the two rhythms lock together: the subsidy engine pumped demand in 2018, 2023, and 2026, while the money tide governed how violently supply answered. The 2018 flood was a cheap-money flood — massive. The response to 2023's boost ran into dear money — muted. The response to the 3 Day Guarantee will be set by rates nobody yet knows.

### The instrument, read honestly

"A childcare centre is secretly an interest-rate instrument." Cash it out precisely.

The building's price moves inversely with rates, like a bond, because it is valued off its rent yield. The supply of new competitors moves with the availability of cheap projects. The operator's stock-market multiple moves with the alternative return on money. Three exposures, one tide.

But the *demand* for childcare barely notices rates — care is bought from wages and subsidy, not borrowed money. (Rates pinch families at the edges through mortgages, as the cost-of-living squeeze of 2025 showed, but the effect is second-order.) So the tide moves the sector's supply and its prices while leaving its demand nearly alone. That asymmetry is analytical gold: **rate moves forecast supply conditions years ahead without much muddying the demand you are tracking.** When money got dear in 2022, an analyst could pencil in the 2024–26 pipeline freeze — a tailwind for incumbent occupancy — before a single construction statistic confirmed it. Chapter 26 will make the cash rate a standing dial on the dashboard.

The watching itself is light work. The RBA announces its cash rate decision on a published schedule, with a statement explaining its lean; the market's own pricing of future rates is published daily; and construction cost indices round out the picture of whether stranded pipelines can refloat. Ten minutes a month keeps the tide dial current — Chapter 26 slots it into the dashboard beside the supply paperwork it governs.

One caution completes the honesty. The tide explains conditions, not outcomes. Cheap money made the 2016–18 flood possible; it did not force any particular company to drown in it. Part III is about a company, and companies make choices inside the tide. To understand the rhythm those choices ride on, we need one more idea — the oldest one in investing. It has carried this book's logic for two chapters without being named. Time to name it.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** Interest rates are the tide under every player at once — they price the buildings, summon or freeze the supply of rivals, and set the multiple on the shares, all without asking whether the rooms are full.

---


## Chapter 13 — The Capital Cycle {#ch13}

*Chapters 10 and 12 each showed a rhythm. This chapter names the master rhythm beneath both — and the childcare twist that makes it crueller here.*

### The oldest story in business

Strip away childcare's particulars, and the pattern of the last three chapters is one investors have watched for two centuries, in shipping, mining, hotels, semiconductors, and lobster boats. It is called **the capital cycle**, and it runs in four beats.

**Beat one: high returns.** Some industry earns unusually good profits — because demand jumped, or supply is short, or both.

**Beat two: capital arrives.** Money is attracted to high returns the way water runs downhill. New entrants build capacity; incumbents expand; banks fund everyone. Each project is judged against today's fat returns.

**Beat three: returns collapse.** All that capacity opens, mostly together, because everyone decided in the same conditions. Supply now exceeds demand. Prices or utilisation — in our sector, occupancy — fall, and the fat returns that attracted the money are destroyed by the money's arrival.

**Beat four: capital flees.** Losses stop new building. Weak capacity closes or changes hands. Supply stops growing while demand grinds upward, and scarcity slowly returns — preparing beat one again.

The cycle's engine is a simple mismatch you have already met twice: **decisions are made on today's returns, but capacity arrives years later, into a different world.** Chapter 10's sword is the capital cycle with a subsidy as beat one. Chapter 12's tide is the capital cycle's financing valve. One rhythm, three costumes.

The rhythm is ancient and universal, which is what makes it trustworthy. Shipowners have ordered fleets at freight-rate peaks, and taken delivery into gluts, for two hundred years — a ship takes three years to build, the same lag as a childcare pipeline. Miners approve new pits at price peaks that the pits' own output later destroys. Semiconductor makers add billion-dollar factories in unison and crash their own chip prices on schedule. Investors who specialise in this pattern have built entire disciplines on it, and Appendix E lists the classic books. Childcare's version is gentler than shipping's — subsidised demand cushions the troughs — but the clockwork is identical, and a reader who has seen it in one industry will never again mistake it for bad luck in another.

### Why the cycle keeps happening to intelligent people

Nobody is fooled twice, surely? The cycle survives because at every beat, each participant is behaving rationally — the same disaggregated rationality as Chapter 11's board.

In beat two, the developer's project genuinely clears its hurdle at current rents. The bank's loan is genuinely secure against the current lease. The retail investor's yield genuinely beats her deposit. Nobody's spreadsheet has a row for "everyone else's simultaneous identical decision" — the one variable that will destroy them all. Economists call it a fallacy of composition: what is sensible for each is fatal for all.

And in beat three, exit is slow for equally rational reasons: sunk costs whisper "hold on", leases bind for years, and hope is cheaper than a write-off. So capacity leaves far more slowly than it arrived. The cycle is not a failure of intelligence. It is what intelligence does when it is distributed and delayed.

For the analyst, this yields the central rule of capital-cycle investing, and it will govern Part V: **judge an industry by the behaviour of its supply side, not by the strength of its demand.** Demand forecasts seduce; supply behaviour tells the truth. An industry where capital is flooding in is dangerous at any level of demand. An industry where capital is fleeing is interesting at almost any level of gloom. The question is never "will people need childcare?" — they will. The question is always "what is the money doing?"

### The band and its walls

Run the four beats over and over, and returns trace a wave: above normal in scarcity, below normal in glut, crossing "fair" on the way through. The wave stays inside a rough **band**, and the band's walls are the interesting part.

The ceiling: returns cannot stay exceptional, because exceptional returns summon capital, and in childcare Chapter 8 showed the door is wide open — no planning gate, fast approvals, willing lenders. High occupancy in this sector is self-erasing: 90% national occupancy would trigger a building boom that ends it.

The floor: returns cannot stay terrible forever, because losses repel capital and demand keeps growing underneath — the pie of Chapter 7, fed by participation, culture, and the Subsidy Engine. Empty seats stop multiplying while the pie rises to meet them. Terrible occupancy is also self-erasing, just far more slowly.

Why "far more slowly"? Because the walls are not symmetric, and here childcare adds its twist.

### The sticky exit

In most industries, beat four does real demolition. Failed mines close; old ships are scrapped; excess capacity physically leaves. The floor rises to meet you.

Childcare's exit is sticky, for reasons this book has already assembled. The seats outlive the operators: a purpose-built centre is worth little as anything else, so when an operator fails, the landlord — Chapter 11's rationalist — installs the next operator at a discounted rent rather than demolish. The building's ownership changes; its forks stay in the pie. The subsidy floor props the weak: an engine that pays wherever parents enrol keeps marginal centres alive that a harsher market would cull. And the leases bind: a chain wanting to shrink must buy its way out of 15-year commitments — Chapter 19 will price that pain in G8's real accounts.

So in childcare, beat four removes companies but barely removes capacity. The cycle's lower turn — the recovery of returns — must come almost entirely from demand growth grinding up to fill the seats, because the seats will not retire to meet it. That can take years longer than in a normal industry, and it sets the analyst's timetable: when you find an oversupplied childcare market, count the excess seats, divide by the pie's growth rate, and you have the shape of the wait. The fix is a slow flood of children, not a quick cull of centres.

### Reading the supply side: five telltales

"Judge the supply side" is a rule; here is its childcare toolkit. Five telltales, each observable from public sources, each mapped to a beat of the cycle.

**One: the pace of new approvals.** Service-approval and development-application pipelines (Chapter 26 gives the sources) are beat two made visible. Accelerating approvals into already-soft occupancy is the loudest warning the sector ever gives.

**Two: the price of existing centres.** When childcare buildings sell at auction on ever-skinnier yields, capital is flooding in — beat two. When sales stall and yields widen, the tide has turned — beat four beginning.

**Three: the behaviour of the biggest operators.** Chains announcing ambitious rollouts and bidding for portfolios marks late beat two; chains closing, selling, and "optimising networks" marks beat three ending. The incumbents' own actions are a supply signal — and Part III will grade G8's actions against exactly this test.

**Four: the tone of the landlords.** REIT reports boasting of development pipelines signal expansion; REITs emphasising "asset recycling" and tenant diversification signal caution. Landlords read the cycle for a living; their published posture is a free second opinion.

**Five: the entry of amateurs.** The surest late-cycle marker in any industry: when capital arrives from people who know nothing about the operations — seminar-fed investors, syndicates, "set and forget" auction buyers — beat three is near. Scarce knowledge no longer restrains the money.

None of the five requires forecasting demand. That is the point. The demand side of childcare invites elegant, unfalsifiable stories about working families and educational culture. The supply side leaves paperwork. Trust the paperwork.

### A worked wait: counting the years

The sticky exit makes one calculation the analyst's workhorse, so run it once in full. A market holds 1,400 seat-days of supply against a 1,150 child-day pie: occupancy 82%, roughly the sector's recent national peak. A flood adds 20% more seats: 1,680. Occupancy falls to 68%. How long until 82% returns, if seats stop growing and the pie compounds at 3% a year? The pie must reach 82% of 1,680, which is about 1,378 — growth of 20% from 1,150. At 3% a year, that is a touch over six years. At 2% growth, nine years. If seats keep growing at even 1%, stretch every answer further.

Six to nine quiet years, from one visible act of overbuilding — that is the arithmetic of the hangover, and it is why Part III's middle chapters cover half a decade of a company waiting for its markets to refill. When you hear "occupancy will recover", the only serious response is this division: excess seats over pie growth. Everything else is mood.

### Where the cycle stands as this book is written

Apply the four beats to the verified record, and date the needle.

The cheap-money years through 2021, plus the subsidy boosts of 2018 and 2023, ran an extended beat two — the sector added over 3,000 centres from 2013. Beat three is in the recent numbers: national occupancy down from 81.8% to 76.1% in two years, WA at 70.5%, operators' returns compressed. And beat four has begun on the entry side: dear money and construction costs have cut supply growth to 2.2%, the pipeline visibly tightening.

But mark the asymmetry: entry has slowed; exit, as always here, barely happens. Seats grew even in 2025. The cycle's floor must therefore be built by the pie — and the pie just printed its first-ever quarterly decline in children, while the January 2026 subsidy boost pumps in the other direction. Beat four with a sticky exit, a wobbling pie, and a fresh demand stimulus: that is the field on which Part III's company stands today. The reader now holds every tool needed to judge it.

Hold the needle's position lightly, though. Cycles are read confidently only in hindsight; in real time, every beat is contested by someone with a spreadsheet. The discipline is to re-run this dating exercise each quarter as the telltales update, not to file the conclusion and stop looking.

One tool remains for Part II, and it is a story rather than a mechanism. The capital cycle in this chapter is abstract; its beats deserve a face. Australian childcare supplies the perfect one: a company that rode beat two harder than anyone in the sector's history, and met beat three in 2008 with $1.8 billion of debt. The sector survived it. The company did not. The difference between those two outcomes is the last lesson of the playing field.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** High returns summon the capacity that destroys them, and low returns repel it — but in childcare the seats never leave, so the cycle's floor is rebuilt slowly, by demand alone.

---


## Chapter 14 — Ghost Story: ABC Learning, 2008 {#ch14}

*Chapter 13 promised the capital cycle a face. Here it is — the company whose ghost has haunted every Australian childcare investor since.*

### The rise

In the early 2000s, a Brisbane company set out to do something no one had done in childcare anywhere: consolidate a cottage industry of single-centre operators into one giant. ABC Learning Centres bought childcare centres the way other companies bought stationery — dozens at a time, year after year, first across Australia, then overseas.

The strategy had a name this book will need again in the very next chapter: a **roll-up**. Buy many small businesses cheaply, staple them together, and let the stock market value the assembled whole at a far richer multiple than the parts cost. By November 2005 ABC ran 697 centres across Australia and New Zealand, with plans for 950 within the year, and it kept going — into the United States and Britain, toward more than 2,000 centres worldwide. At its peak the market valued ABC at about $4.1 billion, and its founder was a national business celebrity.

Read that rise with Part II's tools and it is beat two of the capital cycle in its purest form. Childcare returns looked strong and subsidy-backed; capital poured toward them — through ABC's shares and, above all, through debt. Banks lent ABC the purchase price of an industry. Each acquisition was rational on its own spreadsheet. The pile of spreadsheets was the risk no spreadsheet contained.

### The machinery of the fall

What broke ABC was not an empty sector. It was the machine's own wiring, and every wire is one you have already studied.

**Leverage on leverage.** Part I showed operating leverage: fixed costs that turn small occupancy dips into large profit swings. ABC stacked financial leverage on top — about $1.8 billion of debt whose interest was a fixed cost too. Two levers, one direction. When trading softened, profit did not dip; it halved. In the second half of 2007, ABC reported profit down 42%, and the shares fell 43% in a day. The Price Formula of Chapter 4 then did its double-lever work: profit down, confidence collapsing, multiple compressing — the crash that needs no single disaster.

**Opacity.** As doubts grew, analysts found they could not tell how ABC's actual centres actually traded — earnings leaned on developer fees, related-party deals, and accounting that resisted reading. Confidence, once questioned, had nothing solid to stand on. In August 2008 the company could not even release its accounts, and its shares were suspended, never to trade again.

**The margin call at the top.** ABC's founders had borrowed personally against their own shares. As the price fell, their lenders sold them out — forced selling that crushed the price further, destroying confidence faster. In November 2008, unable to service its debt, ABC entered receivership: the point where a company's lenders take the keys. Shareholders — including thousands of ordinary Australians — were wiped out entirely.

Note what is absent from this list: parents. Demand for childcare barely moved in 2008. Australian children kept attending. ABC did not die of an empty pie. It died of debt, opacity, and a stock-market machine thrown into reverse — with a global financial crisis as the weather.

### The sector that would not die

Now the half of the story that makes this a Part II chapter rather than a cautionary footnote: what happened to the seats.

ABC's centres held roughly a fifth of the nation's long day care places. Its collapse threatened mass closures in the middle of a financial crisis — so the federal government injected $22 million to keep the doors open while receivers sorted the estate. Then the estate changed hands. In December 2009, a consortium of charities — Mission Australia, the Benevolent Society, the Brotherhood of St Laurence, Social Ventures Australia — bought 570 of the centres and rebranded them **Goodstart Early Learning**, today the country's largest operator. Other centres went to independents. The company was formally wound up in 2010.

Count what actually exited: a brand, a head office, a share register, a founder's fortune. Count what stayed: the buildings, the educators, the children, the subsidy flows, and nearly all the seats. Within two years the sector functioned as if ABC had never existed — same rooms, new letterheads. Chapter 13's sticky exit, demonstrated at national scale: **the collapse of the sector's largest company removed almost no capacity.**

And so the sector recovered without the company. Demand kept grinding upward through the 2010s — participation, culture, subsidy. The seats found operators. The industry's returns healed on schedule. ABC's shareholders received nothing, ever.

### What an analyst could have seen

The most useful question a ghost story can answer: was the death visible in advance, from public information? For ABC, yes — on at least four dials, all of a kind this book will build in Part IV. Judge each with Part I's tools.

**The debt dial.** ABC's borrowings were disclosed in every annual report, and they grew with every acquisition round. Chapter 3 taught that operating leverage alone makes profit fragile; a reader who added the interest bill to the fixed-cost line could compute that a modest trading dip would swallow the profit entirely. No inside information — just the published balance sheet, read against the staircase.

**The complexity dial.** Analysts who tried to reconcile ABC's earnings with its centre count found earnings increasingly dependent on development fees and related-party arrangements rather than on children attending rooms. When a company's profit cannot be traced to its product, the profit deserves a discount, not a premium. The market awarded the premium anyway — until August 2008, when the accounts could not be produced at all.

**The founder-loan dial.** The founders' margin loans against their own shares were disclosed under market rules. The implication was mechanical: any large price fall would trigger forced selling, converting bad news into catastrophe automatically. A reader of substantial-holder notices — Chapter 29's territory — knew the trapdoor existed years before it opened.

**The pace dial.** Centres purchased per year, against the integration capacity of one head office: hundreds annually, across four countries, in businesses whose quality lives in local staff. Chapter 2's logic said each centre's value walks out the door when educators churn. Rolling up faster than you can staff is a claim that the staircase does not exist. It does.

Four dials, all public, all flashing for at least two years before receivership. The lesson is not that ABC's end was precisely predictable — timing never is. The lesson is that "nobody could have known" is false comfort. The knowable was published. What failed was the reading, and the willingness to act against a $4 billion consensus. Part IV exists to systematise the reading; Part V, the acting.

### The lesson on the tombstone

Carve the lesson precisely, because it will hang over every remaining chapter of this book:

> **Mean reversion saves industries, not companies.**

"Childcare will recover" and "this childcare company will recover" are entirely different sentences. The first is close to a law: the pie grows, seats fill, the band of Chapter 13 asserts itself. The second depends on whether the specific company — its debt, its leases, its share count, its trust — survives long enough, intact enough, for its shareholders to collect. ABC proved the two sentences can point in opposite directions at once: the industry mean-reverted beautifully, over the corpse of its biggest company, to the benefit of a buyer who paid cents on the boom-era dollar.

For the analyst, the ghost dictates a discipline. Whenever you catch yourself arguing "demand is assured, therefore the shares are safe", stop: that was true of ABC and worthless. Sector safety reaches shareholders only through a company-shaped filter — the balance sheet, the lease stack, the share count, the trust of parents and lenders. Part I built the tools for that filter; Part V will force every thesis through it. And the Pizza Principle adds the modern refinement: since 2008, wounded companies more often survive by massive dilution than die in receivership — the shareholder loses most of the stake either way, just with extra steps. Survival of the name is not recovery of the owner.

### Why the market loved it anyway: the roll-up's magic trick

One puzzle remains, and solving it now will save Part III a chapter of bewilderment: why did the stock market fund ABC's spree so eagerly, for so long? Because a roll-up performs a genuine magic trick with the Price Formula, and the trick works — until it stops.

Watch the trick in toy form. A single childcare centre, bought privately, costs about 4 years of its profit — small businesses sell cheap, because they depend on their owner and cannot be easily resold. A listed company, though, trades on the multiple of Chapter 4 — say 20 years of profit, if the market believes in its growth.

Now let ListedCo buy a centre earning $1 million, paying the private price: $4 million. The moment that $1 million of profit enters ListedCo's accounts, the market values it at ListedCo's multiple: 20 × $1 million = $20 million. Sixteen million dollars of market value, conjured by moving a profit stream from a private ledger to a listed one. Issue new shares at the high multiple, buy more centres at 4 times, repeat. Each purchase makes the growth look faster, which defends the high multiple, which funds the next purchase.

Nothing in the trick is illegal, and part of the gain is even real — a chain has better bargaining power and spreads head-office costs. But see what the structure requires: an endless supply of cheap centres, a market that never re-examines the multiple, and an integration machine that never chokes. Remove any one, and the wheel reverses with the same force it spun forward: the multiple compresses, the currency (shares) weakens, acquisitions stop, and growth — which was mostly acquisitions — vanishes. ABC removed all three at once, with debt as the accelerant.

Hold the trick's anatomy firmly. Two years after ABC's receivership, a new company began performing it again, in the same sector, on the same stage — buying centres at almost exactly 4 times profit.

### The ghost at G8's christening

One more fact makes this chapter personal to our running example. When ABC fell, it left more than a lesson — it left a vacuum. A fragmented sector, seats cheap to buy, banks and investors chastened but a subsidy engine intact, demand grinding upward. Into that vacuum, from 2010, stepped a small Gold Coast company with a familiar idea: buy centres cheaply, many at a time, and let the market value the whole above the parts.

It would do it with less debt than ABC — the ghost taught that much — and with shares instead. Its name was G8 Education. Whether it had learned the rest of the tombstone is the story of Part III, and the reader now owns every tool required to judge it: the machine, the staircase, the two levers, the pizza, the chain, the pie, the seats, the engine, the sword, the players, the tide, the cycle, and the ghost.

The playing field is mapped. Enter the player.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** ABC's collapse removed a company and almost no seats — mean reversion saves industries, not companies, and "the sector will recover" is never the same claim as "this company's shareholders will."

---



# PART III — THE RUNNING EXAMPLE: G8 EDUCATION, A FULL HISTORY {#part3}


## Chapter 15 — The Roll-Up Machine (2010–2014) {#ch15}

*Part II ended with a magic trick and a new performer. Part III follows that performer for sixteen years. Here is the opening act.*

### A small company with a big idea

In 2010, two years after ABC Learning's corpse was carried off the stage, a small Gold Coast company began buying childcare centres. It had been assembled from modest beginnings — early learning brands merged into a listed shell — and renamed **G8 Education**. The name was ambitious for its size. So was the plan.

The plan was Chapter 14's magic trick, performed with discipline this time. Australia had over 4,000 long day care centres in fragmented hands: retiring founders, small family companies, operators with two or three sites. Such businesses sold privately for about **4 times their yearly operating profit** — cheap, for all the reasons small private businesses are cheap. A listed company, once the market believed in its growth, would be valued at a far richer multiple. Buy at 4, be valued at 15 or 20. The gap between those numbers was the business model.

And G8, watching ABC's fresh grave, added a rule: do it without ABC's debt mountain. Fund the buying mainly with **new shares** — the Pizza Principle run deliberately, in its benign direction. Sell new slices at the market's high multiple; spend the cash on centres at the private market's low multiple. Each purchase added more profit per new slice than the slice cost. Dilution, used this way, enriched existing holders instead of robbing them. The machine only required one condition: that the market's multiple stay high.

Hold that condition. The whole chapter hangs on it, and so does the next.

### The treadmill, by the numbers

Watch the machine run with the real figures of its biggest year.

Through 2013 the shares climbed as acquisitions landed: $3.00 by September 2013, about $4.35 by March 2014 — a 45% rise in six months. The rising price was not a bystander; it was fuel. A higher price meant each new share raised more cash, which bought more centres, which grew profit per share, which justified the price. In February 2014, G8 bought **63 centres in one transaction for $104.7 million** — still near its target 4 times operating profit. In the first half of 2014 alone, the company added 114 centres. It announced a **$150 million share raising** in March 2014 and issued **$50 million of corporate notes** — a bond-like IOU paying fixed interest — to keep the war chest full. By late 2014, the share price had passed **five dollars** (≈), and G8 held about 5% of the national market, with management talking of 15%.

Now apply Part I's toolkit, because every gear is one you own.

The Price Formula: profit per share was growing at spectacular rates — but look inside the growth. Almost none of it came from rooms getting fuller. It came from *adding rooms*, purchased with new slices. The multiple, meanwhile, was priced for the growth to continue. G8 traded not on what its centres earned, but on what its next hundred acquisitions would earn.

The Pizza Principle: the share count swelled with every raising — and shareholders cheered, correctly, because each new slice bought more profit than it cost. The arithmetic of virtuous dilution is real. Its dependence on a high multiple is equally real, and nobody cheers arithmetic's second half.

### The accretion sum, once, slowly

The roll-up's engine deserves one careful toy calculation, because "buy at 4, be valued at 20" hides the per-share arithmetic that made shareholders cheer each dilution.

RollCo has 100 million shares, earns $50 million, and trades at 20 times: profit per share 50 cents, price $10. It now buys a group of centres earning $10 million a year, paying the private price of 4 times: $40 million. It funds the purchase by issuing 4 million new shares at $10.

Count what changed. Profit: $60 million. Shares: 104 million. New profit per share: 57.7 cents — up 15% from 50 cents, though the share count grew. Each old holder's slice got thinner and *richer*, because the new slices brought in profit worth five times what they cost. At an unchanged 20-times multiple, the price moves toward $11.54. That 15% jump, repeatable with every acquisition round, is **accretion** — the technical name for the magic trick's per-share payoff.

Now stress the same sum the two ways that matter. Raise the purchase price to 6 times profit and accretion thins toward 9%. Let the market's multiple slip from 20 to 10, and the same $40 million purchase needs 8 million new shares — accretion halves again, and below roughly the purchase multiple, it turns negative: each deal now *dilutes* profit per share, and the machine runs backward. The trick's profitability is just the spread between two multiples, one of which the company pays and the other of which the crowd grants. The company controls neither forever.

That reversal arithmetic — benign dilution flipping malign as the multiple compresses — is the single most useful sum in this chapter. Keep it loaded; Chapter 16 fires it.

### The cracks in the discipline

Two details from 2014, both public at the time, deserve the reader's cold eye.

First, **the price of meals rose**. In March 2014, G8 announced the acquisition of Sterling Early Education for **$228 million — about 5.8 times operating profit**, not 4. Contemporary observers noted the departure from discipline. It is the roll-up's oldest symptom: cheap targets run out, because the roll-up itself has been buying them for years, and rival buyers have noticed the trick working.
