The magic gap — buy at 4, be valued at 20 — narrows meal by meal, precisely when the market's forecasts assume it forever.

Second, **the machine's appetite became a fixed cost of its own**. A roll-up priced for growth must keep acquiring merely to stand still against expectations. G8's investment capacity — $65 to $100 million a year in 2013 — was lifted to $100 to $150 million by early 2014. Ever-bigger meals, needed ever-faster, at rising prices, funded by a share price that assumed the meals would stay cheap. Chapter 14's checklist murmurs: an endless supply of cheap centres, a market that never re-examines the multiple, an integration machine that never chokes.

And integration was the quietest risk of all. Buying a centre transfers a lease and a licence. It does not transfer the director's loyalty, the educators' morale, or the parents' trust — the things Chapter 2 said actually fill rooms. G8 was absorbing a hundred-plus centres a year with a small head office. Each centre arrived with its own brand — the company would come to hold about 21 of them — its own culture, and its own staircase of costs. The roll-up's spreadsheet recorded the profit acquired. No line recorded whether it would stay.

### What the machine was not doing

Now step back onto Part II's playing field, because the most important facts of 2010–2014 are things G8's machine was *not* watching.

It was not watching the tide. These were the years the cash rate slid from 4.75% toward 2.5%, and Chapter 12's machinery engaged: yield-hungry investors, eager banks, developers whose spreadsheets suddenly sang. The same cheap money that funded G8's raisings was quietly funding something else — a construction pipeline of brand-new rival centres, aimed at the very suburbs where childcare looked profitable. And childcare looked most profitable exactly where G8's occupancy was best.

It was not watching the tap. Nobody was — Chapter 8's lesson. G8's acquisition model priced each centre on its current earnings, in its current suburb, with its current competition. The forks that would arrive within two kilometres by 2017 appeared on no completion statement.

And it was not watching the ghost's fourth dial. ABC died, in part, of buying faster than it could integrate. G8's pace — 114 centres in six months — was ABC's pace with better manners and less debt. The debt lesson had been learned. The pace lesson had not.

Two smaller choices from the era complete the picture, each with a long tail. The **notes** — $50 million of fixed-interest IOUs, with more to follow — reintroduced a sliver of ABC's fixed-cost financing under politer terms: modest beside the equity funding, but interest is a staircase that never steps down. And the **brands**: G8 deliberately kept the trading names of what it bought — eventually about 21 of them — reasoning that parents trust the local name on the fence, not a corporate parent. Sensible marketing, with two consequences the reader should file: it left the company's quality controls stitched across 21 identities, and it meant, decades later, that most parents at a G8 centre did not know they were at one. Both consequences return, with force, in 2025.

None of this is hindsight smugness; contemporary sceptics said much of it, and the machine ran anyway, because for four years every quarter proved the sceptics poorer. That is how beat two of every capital cycle feels from inside: the doubters sound like broken clocks, right up until the clock strikes.

### The machine at full speed

Freeze the frame at late 2014, because this is the summit, and the book will measure every later altitude against it. Note, too, what the summit paid its climbers: rising dividends, funded comfortably by profits that the raisings kept swelling — the roll-up at full song rewards everyone visibly, which is precisely what makes its assumptions so hard to question aloud.

Share price: above $5 (≈). Centres: roughly 400, assembled in four years. Market position: the largest listed childcare operator in the country, on its way — said management — to three times the share. The multiple: rich, and resting on three stacked assumptions — that cheap centres remained plentiful, that acquired profits would persist under G8's roof, and that the suburbs those profits lived in would stay as uncrowded as the day each centre was bought.

Three assumptions. Part II gave you the tools to test each one, and none of the tests needed private information. The supply of cheap targets was visibly thinning — G8's own Sterling price proved it. The integration question was visible in the acquisition pace. And the coming crowding was visible, years ahead, in the cheap-money construction pipeline — Chapter 13's paperwork, already filed with councils across the country.

The machine did not know it yet, but it had already eaten its best meals. What broke first was none of the three assumptions above. It was the thing the whole model stood on — the one condition flagged at this chapter's start. The multiple.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** A roll-up buys profit cheaply and is valued richly on the difference — a machine that works only while the multiple stays high, the targets stay cheap, and nobody asks whether the bought profits will stay.

---


## Chapter 16 — Crash One (2014–15): Belief Dies First {#ch16}

*Chapter 15 froze the frame at the summit. This chapter watches the first fall — the strangest of the five, because almost nothing happened.*

### A crash with no disaster

Search the record of 2014 and 2015 for G8's catastrophe and you will not find one. No profit collapse — profits, swollen by the acquisitions, kept growing. No scandal, no default, no empty rooms making headlines. Yet from its 2014 peak above $5, the share price sagged, slid, and stumbled until, by late 2015, roughly 40% of the company's market value had evaporated (≈).

For a beginner, this is bewildering: how does a stock fall 40% while the business reports growth? Nothing in ordinary life prepares you for it — houses do not halve while the rent rises; a shop's value does not evaporate while its tills ring. For a reader of Chapter 4, it is the textbook case. Recite the Price Formula: price = profit per share × confidence. Two parts, either of which can crash alone. In 2014–15, profit per share held. **The multiple halved.** The crowd did not learn that G8 was earning less. It stopped believing the earnings meant what the price had assumed they meant.

This chapter is about how that happens — how belief dies with no disaster on any single day — because it will happen again in this history, and because it is the crash beginners are least prepared for.

### What the crowd re-examined

Recall the roll-up's condition: the market must keep paying a growth multiple. Through 2014, one by one, the props under that multiple wobbled.

**The meals got dearer, publicly.** Sterling at 5.8 times operating profit was a number anyone could divide. If future centres cost 6 times instead of 4, the magic gap per acquisition shrank by half. A growth multiple priced for the old gap had to be re-priced for the new one — not because anything failed, but because the arithmetic of the *next* deal changed.

**The growth's quality came into question.** Analysts began separating G8's growth into bought and organic — asking Chapter 7's question at company scale: are the rooms themselves getting fuller? Strip out acquisitions, and underlying occupancy was unremarkable. A dollar of profit from a fuller room persists; a dollar from a purchased room persists only if the room stays full. The market had been paying one multiple for two different kinds of dollar, and it started paying two.

**The treadmill's logic turned visible.** More acquisitions needed more raisings; more raisings needed a high share price; the high share price needed belief in more acquisitions. Circular structures are stable while everyone faces inward. The first sustained price weakness broke the circle's spell: a lower price made raisings dilutive rather than virtuous — the Pizza Principle shifting from benign to malign in the same accounts — which slowed the treadmill, which undermined the growth story, which lowered the price. The machine that ran on its own reflection now reversed on it.

Notice what all three re-examinations share: **every input was old information.** The Sterling multiple, the organic occupancy, the treadmill structure — all had been public for months or years. No new fact arrived. The crowd simply looked again, with different eyes. That is why the fall had no anniversary, no single black day: belief does not break on schedule. It erodes, holder by holder, each sale nudging the price, each lower price licensing the next doubter.

### The toy version, for certainty

Run the crash in toy numbers once, so the "no disaster" arithmetic is beyond doubt.

RollCo, from Chapter 15: $60 million of profit, 104 million shares, profit per share 57.7 cents, multiple 20, price about $11.50. Over eighteen months, profit *grows* to $70 million — acquisitions still landing — and the share count drifts to 115 million with the raisings that funded them. Profit per share: 60.9 cents. The business, on its own report card, improved.

But the crowd, re-examining the story, trims the years it will pay from 20 toward 10. New price: 60.9 cents × 10 = $6.09. The stock has fallen 47% while profit per share *rose* 6%. No result missed, no scandal broke; the multiple simply travelled from faith to doubt. That is Crash One in four lines of arithmetic — and it is why this book insisted, back in Chapter 4, that you learn to read the multiple as a belief with a number on it.

### Holding through it: the shareholder's experience

Before the Affinity episode, stand for a moment where a 2014 shareholder stood, because belief-crashes are uniquely disorienting to live through, and the disorientation itself teaches.

In a profit crash, the holder has an event to analyse: results fell, here is why, decide. In a belief crash there is nothing to hold onto — every results day *beats* the gloom, management sounds baffled and wounded, and the price falls anyway, month after month. The holder's inner monologue becomes the dangerous one: "the market is irrational; the numbers are fine; I'll buy more." Sometimes that monologue is right. But notice what it assumes: that the old multiple was correct and the new one is a mood. Chapter 4 taught the honest reframe — the market is not denying the profits; it is repricing the *durability* of the profits. The question for the holder is never "are the results still good?" It is "were the props under the old multiple sound, and what has changed about them?" In G8's case the props — cheap targets, virtuous raisings, unexamined integration — were visibly weakening. The falling price was not noise fighting the numbers. It was the crowd, slowly, doing better analysis than the results announcements invited.

The practical takeaway for Part V: when you own a high-multiple story, write down the props on the day you buy. In a belief-crash, you will re-read that list with clearer eyes than any chart offers.

### The Affinity misfire

Into this sliding belief, in July 2015, G8 fired its boldest shot: a **hostile takeover bid** for Affinity Education, a smaller listed childcare roll-up — an offer made over the target board's objection, directly to its shareholders. And the currency offered was not cash but **scrip**: G8's own shares.

Read the bid with this chapter's eyes and its logic is poignant. The treadmill needed a very large meal; the private market's cheap targets were exhausted; and Affinity's own share price had slumped, making it the last cheap bulk on the shelf. Paying in scrip meant the machine could eat without cash — provided the market still valued G8's slices richly. The bid was, in effect, a bet that the old multiple was still alive.

It was not. The contest turned hostile and messy. Affinity resisted; the offer's value, denominated in G8's sagging shares, wobbled with them. In October 2015 the **Takeovers Panel** — the referee of Australian takeover conduct — declared aspects of G8's conduct "unacceptable circumstances", relating to how a parcel of Affinity shares had been assembled, and ordered G8 to sell shares back. By the end, G8 walked away with costs, a public rebuke, and no Affinity.

Scrip bids carry a signal beginners should learn to read, independent of this one's fate. A company that offers its *own shares* as payment is making an implicit statement about their value: you spend the currency you consider dear and hoard the one you consider cheap. Management teams that believe their stock is undervalued pay cash; teams that pay in scrip at depressed prices are either out of cash options or quietly comfortable with the exchange rate. Neither reading flattered G8 in 2015 — and the market, which performs this decoding instinctively, marked the signal accordingly. The general habit: whenever a deal is announced, note *what the acquirer chose to pay with*. The currency choice is a disclosure no announcement drafts carefully enough to hide.

The failed bid mattered less for what it cost than for what it confirmed. To a crowd already re-examining the story, it read as the machine straining: the disciplined 4-times buyer of 2011 now lunging, hostile and rebuked, at a listed rival priced in its own weakening paper. Confidence, already leaking, found another hole.

### The anatomy of a belief-crash, for your files

Crash One is the purest specimen this history will offer of a **multiple compression** — so file its anatomy before moving on.

*It needs no trigger.* A belief-crash can start from a question as mild as "what exactly am I paying twenty years for?" asked by enough holders in the same season.

*It is self-catalysing.* Each doubter's sale is the next doubter's evidence. Chapter 6 said part of the price is set by watchers acting early; in a belief-crash, the watchers *are* the event.

*It is immune to good results.* G8 kept reporting growth through the slide. Results answer the question "what did you earn?" The crash was answering "what are those earnings worth?" — a question results cannot reach. Management, baffled, points at the profits; the price ignores them. Both are behaving correctly.

*And it is the multiple's honest work, not a malfunction.* The 20-times G8 of 2014 priced three fragile assumptions as near-certainties. The 10-times G8 of late 2015 priced them as coin-flips. The second price was not madness replacing sanity; it was doubt replacing faith, in a company whose value had always been mostly faith.

For the analyst, one practical rule falls out. **When a stock's value rests on a story, track the story's props, not the stock's results.** The props — acquisition multiples, organic occupancy, the raising treadmill — were all measurable, and all deteriorated before or without any earnings miss. Chapter 29 will formalise prop-tracking; Crash One is why it exists.

### The fork in the road

Close the frame at the end of 2015. The multiple has halved; the treadmill has stalled; the Affinity door is shut. What remains is what the machine actually bought: roughly 470 centres (≈), assembled at speed, wearing 21 brands, filled to unremarkable levels, in suburbs chosen by four years of acquisition opportunism.

From here, G8's future had to come from the rooms themselves — from occupancy, the variable the roll-up years had treated as an assumption. And so the story's centre of gravity shifts from the share market to the suburbs: to the pie, the forks, and the flood of purpose-built rivals that Part II's cheap-money tide had been quietly financing all along.

The multiple died first. The rooms were next.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** A stock can crash 40% on unchanged profits, because the multiple is belief — and belief, once re-examined, erodes holder by holder with no disaster required.

---


## Chapter 17 — The Flood (2016–18): Crash Two {#ch17}

*Crash One killed the multiple. Crash Two came for the thing the multiple had been resting on — the rooms.*

### The water arrives

Chapter 12 dated the tide: from 2012 the cash rate slid, and by 2016 it sat at 1.5% and stayed. Chapter 10 set the sword's timetable: supply answers a profitable sector two to four years after the property maths turns. Do the sum. The childcare building projects conceived in the yield-hungry years of 2013–2015 — when G8's own prosperity was advertising the sector's returns — would open their doors in 2016, 2017, and 2018.

They did. Centre numbers nationally climbed through the mid-2010s at a pace the sector had never seen, on the way from roughly 6,300 (2013) toward 9,500-plus. And the new centres did not scatter randomly. Developers build where the demographics scream childcare — young families, dual incomes, commuter suburbs. Which is to say: they built where the existing centres already were, because the same screaming demographics had put them there. Forks and Pies, executed nationally. The pie kept growing — Chapter 7's forces were all running — but in suburb after suburb, the forks grew faster.

For G8 this was not bad luck. It was a targeting algorithm. The roll-up had spent 2010–2014 buying centres *because* they sat in profitable suburbs; the flood built new centres *because* suburbs were profitable. The machine had, with four years' diligence, assembled a portfolio of exactly the locations the flood would strike.

### The beauty contest

Now walk into one of those suburbs and watch the contest a parent actually judges.

On one corner: a G8 centre. Acquired in 2012, built perhaps in 1998. A converted house or an ageing purpose-built box; small rooms, a tired yard, parking on the street. Adequate, loved by its families, profitable for years precisely because nothing newer existed nearby.

On the other corner, opening Monday: the rival. Purpose-built last year to current standards and current tastes — big light-filled rooms, architect-drawn outdoor "learning environments", a café-grade foyer, basement parking, fees pitched keenly because its owner needs to fill 100 places from zero (a **greenfield** centre, in the trade's word for a brand-new site).

Chapter 11 described the judge: a parent deciding on two tours and a feeling, voting on trust and impressions. Put the old box and the new palace before that judge, at similar gap fees. This is **the Beauty Contest**, and the book gives it a name because it decides *where* the flood's damage lands. Suburb-level arithmetic (Millbrook's) says average occupancy must fall when forks arrive. The Beauty Contest says the fall is not average at all: it concentrates on the oldest, tiredest fleet in the suburb. New entrants skim the newly deciding families; incumbents keep their enrolled children — for a while, until each child ages out to school and is not replaced.

G8's fleet, assembled by buying whatever profitable centres came to market across a decade of vintages, skewed old. In the Beauty Contest years, the roll-up's bargain-hunting revealed its hidden cost: a portfolio of yesterday's buildings, entered against tomorrow's, in a contest judged on appearances.

### A word the reader must own: like-for-like

Before the numbers, one piece of reporting language becomes essential here, because it is the honest lens on any acquisitive company.

**Like-for-like** (companies also say "same-centre") measures only the centres owned in *both* periods being compared — this year versus last year, identical fleet. It strips out the flattery of acquisition: a roll-up's headline revenue can grow forever simply by buying, while every centre it owns quietly empties. Like-for-like occupancy asks the only question the flood cares about: are *these* rooms, the ones we already had, fuller or emptier than a year ago?

The habit to build: whenever an acquisitive company reports, skip the headline and find the like-for-like line. If the company stops publishing it, or redefines it, treat the silence as data. Through the flood years, G8's headline numbers still wore the roll-up's make-up; the like-for-like line told the truth this chapter now turns to.

### The numbers of the grind

Crash Two, unlike Crash One, shows up plainly in the operating numbers — not as a plunge but as a grind, quarter after quarter, which is how oversupply always arrives.

By 2018, G8's like-for-like occupancy — same centres, year on year, the honest measure that strips out acquisitions — was **74%**, down another 1.9 percentage points in the year. Set 74% against Part I's machinery. Sunny Corner's toy break-even sat at 86%; real centres commonly break even between 60% and 80%. At 74%, a substantial fraction of G8's rooms were operating below their line — full-room wages, Chapter 2, for quarter-empty rooms — while the rest earned thinned margins.

The lever did its work. For full-year 2018, revenue actually *rose* 7.7% to $858.2 million — acquisitions and fee increases still adding — but underlying operating profit fell **12.7%** to $136.3 million. Rising revenue, falling profit: the signature of a fixed-cost machine whose fullness is draining. When the results landed in February 2019, the shares fell 13% in the day, the worst performer on the market.

Management's commentary through this period is worth a beginner's attention for its tone: conditions were "challenging"; supply growth was "moderating"; improvement was expected as the pipeline slowed. All true, none false — and yet the grind continued, because Chapter 13's arithmetic does not care about tone. The seats already built stood in the suburbs. The only exits were sticky. The hangover had to be served in full, at the pace of the pie.

### The discounting spiral

Oversupply does one more thing to a suburb that the occupancy line only partly shows: it puts the fee under siege.

A greenfield opening at zero occupancy has a desperate arithmetic — Chapter 1's machine with *no* children — and its rational play is to buy occupancy fast: introductory discounts, waived enrolment fees, a free first week (offers of this kind are standard practice in crowded suburbs). Incumbents must then choose between matching — cutting the contribution line of Chapter 3's break-even sum — or holding price and losing the Beauty Contest's marginal families faster. Either answer thins profit; the choice is only which lever bleeds.

Recall from Chapter 3 what a small fee cut does at 74% occupancy: with contribution near $140 a day, a $10 discount removes 7% of every enrolled child's contribution to save children at the margin. The staircase makes matching expensive; the contest makes not-matching expensive. This pincer — volume or price, lose either way — is why oversupply damages profits *more* than the occupancy fall alone predicts, and why Chapter 25's gauge will track fees alongside fullness. A suburb where "first month free" banners bloom is a suburb telling you its seat arithmetic, in fabric, from the fence line.

### What the dashboard would have shown

Run this crash through the book's instruments, because Crash Two is the one a diligent outsider could have seen furthest ahead.

The supply paperwork led by *years*. Council development applications for the 2016–18 openings were filed from 2013; service approvals accumulated visibly; construction data boomed. Chapter 26's dials, had anyone assembled them, were flashing while G8's shares still traded near their peak. This is the flood's defining property: unlike a trust shock or a policy surprise, **oversupply files its plans in advance, with the council, in public.**

The tide dial agreed: 1.5% cash rates said the building would continue until the property maths broke, and the maths showed no sign of breaking.

Meanwhile the *demand* dials were fine — the pie grew every year of the flood. An analyst watching only demand (children, participation, subsidy) saw a healthy sector and missed everything. An analyst watching the CCS's July 2018 arrival might even have called it a rescue — demand boost, fuller rooms. It helped; it was also, per Chapter 10, the next cycle's first edge, and the flood simply outweighed it. The lesson generalises: **in an oversupply crash, demand data reassures you all the way down.** Only the supply side told the truth, and it told it three years early.

### The incumbent's defence menu

What could an operator actually *do*, standing in the flood? List the menu, because G8 eventually ordered from all of it, and the menu's limits are the point.

**Refurbish** — narrow the Beauty Contest gap; costly, slow, and the rival stays newer. **Discount** — defend volume by bleeding contribution; the spiral above. **Differentiate** — quality ratings, programs, staff continuity; real, but it moves parents in years, not quarters. **Close or sell** the condemned — the only move that removes a fork, and only from your own hand, at the cost of writing off the roll-up's purchase price. **Endure** — protect cash, hold staff, and wait for the pie, which is what the arithmetic mostly imposes regardless.

Notice what is *not* on the menu: any action that stops the rivals from opening. The flood's causes — the tide, the tap, the developers' spreadsheets — sit entirely outside an operator's reach. This is the strategic meaning of Chapter 11's asymmetry: the player paid on occupancy holds none of the levers that set supply. An incumbent in an oversupply crash is not choosing between recovery plans. It is choosing the least expensive way to wait.

### The crash that teaches the sector

Crash Two carried none of Crash One's mystery. Profit fell, so the price fell — the Price Formula's plainest clause, compounded by a multiple that had already lost its faith and now lost its patience. From the 2014 peak above $5 to the post-results $3.16 of early 2019, the two crashes together had removed roughly 40% of the price (≈) — and remember the Broken Ruler: the share count had kept growing through the period, so the company's lost market value was larger still.

But Crash Two is the one that teaches the *sector's* nature, and its lesson should be filed in Part II's terms. Nothing about 2016–18 required anyone's mistake. The subsidy engine ran; the pie grew; parents were served better than ever — shinier centres, keener fees, more choice. The flood was the capital cycle delivering its ordinary harvest to the players Chapter 11 said would bleed: operators, and above all the biggest incumbent with the oldest fleet. From the parents' side of the counter, the crash years were a golden age. Both facts are the same fact. An investor who confuses "the sector is thriving" with "the operators are thriving" has not yet understood the game; Crash Two is the tuition.

By late 2018, though, the paperwork dials were finally turning the other way. Supply growth was moderating; the pipeline was thinning; G8 itself would soon disclose that competitor openings within two kilometres of its centres had slowed to a crawl. The hangover's arithmetic was, at last, starting to run in the incumbent's favour. What the company needed now was simply time, and no surprises.

It got 2019.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** Oversupply is the crash that announces itself years ahead in public paperwork — and its damage lands hardest on the oldest fleet in the suburb, chosen by parents judging a beauty contest.

---


## Chapter 18 — Crash Three (2019): The Broken Promise {#ch18}

*Crash Two was a grind the whole sector shared. Crash Three was smaller, sharper, and entirely about one thing: a promise.*

### The setting: a company doing the right things

Enter 2019 with the picture fair and complete, because this crash punishes a company that was, by most operating measures, improving.

The flood was receding: national supply growth was moderating, and within two kilometres of G8's own centres — the only radius that matters, as Forks and Pies taught — competitor growth for 2019 would come in at just **1.0%, against 4.2% nationally**. G8 was pruning at last: it sold **25 Western Australian centres** — the state whose oversupply Part II documented — and closed **16 more as leases expired**, the first serious use of the exit door. It was building a new fleet for the Beauty Contest: a **44-centre, $155 million greenfield pipeline**, each new centre opening near-empty by design (recall Chapter 8: a greenfield is a bet that spends occupancy today for buildings that win contests tomorrow). Like-for-like occupancy rose for the first time since 2015. Staff turnover hit record lows.

In August 2019, with its half-year results, management put a number on the recovery: full-year operating profit **guidance** of **$140 to $145 million**. The shares, which had rallied more than 50% off their post-results lows earlier in the year, carried a market once again leaning toward belief. Recall Chapter 4's definition: guidance is a company's public forecast of itself — management's promise, in a number.

### The promise breaks

Through the September quarter, two small things went wrong. The seasonal occupancy build — childcare fills as the year progresses — ran shy of plan. And wages overran forecast by **$3 million** in the quarter: Staircase Costs in their pettiest, most characteristic form, rosters stepping down more slowly than soft enrolments walked.

In November 2019, G8 issued the correction: full-year operating profit now expected at **$131 to $134 million**. Do the beginner's arithmetic: against the August midpoint, a cut of roughly **7%**.

The market's response: the shares fell **17.8% in a week**, touching $2.13, a one-year low. A 7% profit miss; an 18% price fall. And this was the *second* revision in three months — August's guidance had itself trimmed expectations set earlier. Two downgrades, one quarter apart, from a management team asking the market to believe in a turnaround.

### Why companies promise at all

A fair question interrupts here: if precise forecasts are hostages, why give them? Three forces make guidance nearly compulsory, and knowing them helps you read it.

First, **the law leans on companies to speak.** Australian listed companies operate under continuous disclosure rules: when management becomes aware that results will differ materially from what the market expects, it must promptly say so. This is why downgrades arrive as sudden, dated announcements rather than gentle drift — the obligation crystallises, the statement must be made, and the correction lands in one lump on one morning. The rule is good for market honesty; it also guarantees that guidance failures are *events*, with all the price violence events attract.

Second, **the market punishes silence.** A company that declines to guide leaves analysts to guess, and the guesses scatter; managements generally prefer to anchor expectations than to be hostage to the wildest estimate. Guidance is expectation management — a hostage exchanged for control of the narrative.

Third, **turnarounds especially must promise.** A management asking the market to believe in recovery has one currency: forecasts, delivered. Each kept promise buys multiple points back; which is precisely why each broken one is so expensive. G8 in 2019 was spending exactly this currency when the staircase moved.

The reader's posture follows: treat guidance as information about *management's own visibility*, not as a fact about the future. A wide guidance range says visibility is poor; a narrow range from a high-leverage business says confidence exceeds structure; a history of hitting says the machine is being read well from inside. The guidance track record — Chapter 29 formalises it — is one of the few management-quality metrics an outsider can compute exactly.

### The multiple's revenge

Why does a 7% miss cost 18% of the price? A beginner suspects panic. Chapter 4 says: arithmetic. Both levers moved, and the second moved harder.

Lever one, profit: down 7%. If the multiple had stood still, the price falls 7%. The remaining 11 points came from lever two — and lever two is this chapter's subject. **A guidance miss does not just change this year's number; it changes the credibility of every future number.** The multiple is years of forecast profit paid upfront, and forecasts reach the crowd through management's mouth. A team that misses its own three-month-old promise — twice — forces every holder to re-discount not 2019's profit but the whole stream: 2020's, 2021's, all of it, now carrying a "management may be wrong again" tax. Call it **the multiple's revenge**: small miss, large re-rating, because the miss repriced the *promiser*, not the promise.

There is a mechanical accelerant too, worth knowing. Professional investors often hold turnaround stories on a leash — the thesis is "management says X; I hold while X holds." A downgrade snaps every such leash on the same morning. The selling is not hysteria; it is a hundred written theses each executing its own exit clause at once. Chapter 34 will teach you to write such clauses yourself; November 2019 shows what they look like when a crowd of them fires together.

File the general rule, because it recurs in this history and in every history: **in a fixed-cost business near its break-even line, guidance is a hostage to the staircase.** Chapter 3 showed the lever: at G8's occupancy levels, a one-point enrolment shortfall or a $3 million wage drift — trivia, at $850 million of revenue — swings reported profit by whole percentage points. Managements keep promising precise numbers because markets demand them; the machine keeps making precise numbers unpromisable. The analyst's edge is not predicting the miss. It is knowing which companies are *structurally likely* to miss, and refusing to pay a trust premium for their guidance.

### Anatomy of a downgrade day

Since this book's reader will live through downgrade days, walk through the mechanics of one — they follow a script, and knowing it converts panic into observation.

It begins before the market opens: the company lodges an announcement with the exchange, often after requesting a brief **trading halt** — a pause in trading while material news is released, so no one trades uninformed. The announcement's structure is standardised: the new guidance, the old guidance, and a paragraph of causes. Read the causes paragraph like an analyst, not a victim, sorting each cause into one of two bins.

**Environment misses** — occupancy softer, sector demand weak — say the world differed from forecast. **Execution misses** — wages over plan, rosters misjudged — say the company differed from its own controls. The bins price differently, and should. An environment miss reads on every rival too, and may already be in their prices. An execution miss is private information about this management's grip, and the multiple's revenge falls hardest on it. November 2019 contained both bins at once: a seasonal occupancy shortfall (environment) *and* a $3 million wage overrun (execution) — which is why the revenge was double-strength, and why "two downgrades in three months" mattered more than either alone. One miss is weather. Two, in sequence, from the same controls, is a pattern — and patterns are what multiples price.

Then the day unfolds: the price gaps down at the open as the leashed theses fire; analysts re-cut models by lunchtime; the close settles somewhere between arithmetic and fear. The next morning, commentary asks whether the reaction was "overdone" — which brings this chapter to the most instructive commentary of 2019.

### Morningstar's call, and the shock that deleted it

Now the coda that turns Crash Three from an anecdote into a lesson about analysis itself.

In the downgrade's aftermath, the research house Morningstar published a considered, reasoned view: the sell-off was **"excessive."** The profit cut was "reasonably small"; the problems — a soft occupancy season, a wage overrun — were temporary, not structural; supply relief was real and arriving. Their analyst valued the shares at $3.08 against a market price near $1.96, and forecast occupancy recovering from about 75% to about **80% within three years**.

Understand this clearly: **the analysis was good.** It weighed the supply data correctly — the two-kilometre disclosure was genuinely benign. It read the downgrade's causes correctly. It applied exactly the frameworks this book has been building, and on the information available in November 2019, this book would have reasoned much the same way. A patient reader of Parts I and II, shown the 2019 facts, should have found the bull case at least credible.

Occupancy did not reach 80% in three years. It did not reach 80% ever again, to the date this book was written. Within four months of the forecast, a coronavirus closed the country, parents withdrew their children, the government made childcare temporarily free under emergency rules, and every number in every childcare model on earth became waste paper. Chapter 19 tells that story. Here, take only the epistemology: the forecast was not refuted; it was **deleted** — by an event outside every model, unknowable in advance, that arrived anyway.

This is the humility that Part IV is built on, so state it precisely. Good analysis narrows the odds on the futures that resemble the present. It cannot price the future that doesn't. The practical consequences come later — position sizing in Chapter 34, scenario thinking in Chapter 31 — but the posture starts here: hold every thesis, including this book's, as a bet that the world stays inside its recent range. Sometimes it leaves.

### Three crashes down

Take stock, because the pattern across the crashes is now visible and the book will lean on it.

Crash One: profit fine, **multiple** died — belief re-examined. Crash Two: **profit** died — supply flood through the fixed-cost lever. Crash Three: profit merely stumbled, but **trust in guidance** died, and the multiple executed its revenge. Three crashes, three different clauses of the same Price Formula — and the third barely needed a business event at all, just a promise two sizes too precise for the machine that made it.

The shares ended 2019 near two dollars, less than half the broken ruler's $5. The company, by the operating evidence, was genuinely mending. And 2020 was six weeks away.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** A guidance miss reprices the promiser, not just the promise — in a fixed-cost business near break-even, precise forecasts are hostages, and the multiple takes its revenge on the second miss.

---


## Chapter 19 — Crash Four (2020): The Halved Slice {#ch19}

*Chapter 18 ended with a forecast deleted by history. This is the deletion — and the crash that did its damage quietly, through the share count.*

### The machine meets a pandemic

In the first months of 2020, COVID-19 reached Australia. By late March the country was closing: offices emptied, borders shut, and parents — some newly jobless, some newly working from kitchen tables, all newly frightened — began keeping their children home. Childcare attendance collapsed within weeks.

Run that through Part I, coldly. The Profit Machine's revenue line — children × fee — was failing at both terms: children vanishing, and fee collection collapsing with them. The cost line barely moved: rents contracted for years; core rosters held, both because the ratio law bound whoever remained and because sacking the workforce would destroy the recovery. Sunny Corner's arithmetic, nationwide: a machine built for 100 running at a fraction, burning cash daily. The government saw the same arithmetic — a sector-wide ABC Learning, everywhere at once — and in early April moved with startling speed: childcare became **temporarily free** to families, with operators paid flat emergency amounts pegged to pre-crisis revenue, and wage subsidies layered beneath. The package kept doors open. It also, deliberately, paid operators *less* than normal trading — survival rations, not profits.

The emergency package deserves a beginner's close look, because it reveals the sector's constitution under maximum stress. For roughly three months, families paid nothing; operators received flat payments pegged to a *pre-crisis* reference period — set at about half of former fee revenue (≈) — with the national wage subsidy scheme layered underneath for eligible staff. Study the design's logic with Chapter 11's player map: parents (voters) got free care; educators (scarce, essential) kept jobs; operators (systemically necessary) got survival, not profit; and the taxpayer bought the continuity of a system the workforce could not restart from cold. Every player's treatment matched their leverage exactly. The package proved the Subsidy Engine's implicit guarantee was real — childcare would not be allowed to collapse — while defining the guarantee's ceiling: *the system* is protected; *shareholder returns* are explicitly not. When normal subsidy trading resumed in the second half of 2020, that distinction was G8's whole story: alive, guaranteed, and poorer.

For G8, rations meant losses and cash burn with no visible end date. Recall the company's position entering the storm: two downgrades just behind it, occupancy in the mid-70s, a $155 million greenfield program mid-build, and Chapter 5's warning list — thin margins, heavy fixed costs, falling revenue — now reading like a diagnosis. The question was no longer profit. It was the balance sheet question: **how many months of cash?**

### The raising, replayed in full

You already know what happened next — Chapter 5 used it as its centrepiece — but now you can watch it with the full context of why, and with the crash-anatomy eye this Part has developed.

On **9 April 2020**, barely three weeks into the national emergency, G8 announced a **$301 million capital raising at 80 cents per share** — against a pre-COVID price above $2 and a five-year-old peak above $5. The structure: a **$227 million institutional placement**, done within days, and a **$75 million entitlement offer** to existing shareholders. Retail holders, offered the chance to defend their fraction of the pizza, took up about **one third** of it; underwriters absorbed the rest. By May it was complete: roughly **376 million new shares**, taking the count from about 460 million to about 840 million (≈, sources vary on exact totals). The slices nearly doubled; every pre-2020 share became, permanently, about half its former fraction of the company.

Why so much, so fast, so cheap? Because Chapter 5's cruel mechanism allows no better: the companies that most need money get the worst prices, and the *first* desperate raiser in a crisis gets a better price than the second. G8's directors, watching cash burn against an unknowable timeline, bought certainty of survival and paid for it with half the pizza. Dividends were suspended alongside. It was, by the standards of corporate triage, competent and decisive. It was also, for anyone holding since 2014, the moment the old company arithmetic ended forever.

### The arithmetic of desperation, priced

One toy comparison exposes what timing cost the shareholders, and why Chapter 32's early-warning list earns its keep.

Suppose G8 had raised the same $301 million a year earlier, in calmer weather, at $2.00 a share: about 150 million new shares, lifting the count from ≈460 to ≈610 million — dilution of about 25%. Raising at 80 cents instead required ≈376 million shares: dilution of about 45%. Same company, same cash in the door; the *only* variable was the price on the day of need. The difference — roughly 225 million extra permanent slices — is the fee the market charges for arriving desperate. Every future dollar of profit now splits those extra slices' way, forever.

This is the general law of capital raisings, worth engraving: **the cost of emergency money is set by the emergency, not the money.** A company that raises before it must sells few slices dear; one that raises because it must sells many slices cheap. And since managements almost never raise early — optimism is their job description — the analyst's task is to see the emergency forming while the market still prices calm. The watch-list signals were all public by March 2020: revenue in collapse, fixed costs contracted, cash finite, dividends doomed. The 80-cent print two weeks later was not news. It was the invoice.

### The quietest crash

Now the observation that gives this chapter its title and its place in the anatomy of crashes. Ask a chart where G8's disaster of 2020 shows, and the chart half-hides it. Prices crashed across the whole market that March, G8 among them; by year's end, with vaccines announced and attendance recovering, the shares had rallied off their lows, and a casual glance said "COVID dip, partly recovered — like everything else."

The chart is lying by omission. Recite the Price Formula's third clause: price = (profit ÷ **shares**) × confidence. The pre-2020 chart tracks slices of a 460-million-slice company; the post-2020 chart, slices of an 840-million-slice one. For the *price per slice* merely to return to its pre-COVID $2, the company now had to be worth nearly twice as much as before. For the old $5, it would need to earn, roughly, **double** its best-ever profit. The crash didn't happen on the price axis, where eyes look. It happened on the denominator, where they don't.

That is why this book calls Crash Four **the quietest one**, and it completes a taxonomy worth filing. Crash One was the multiple, visible as a sagging chart. Crash Two was profit, visible in falling results. Crash Three was trust, visible in one violent week. Crash Four was the share count — **the halved slice** — visible only to someone who reads the number of shares, which the chart never shows and the headlines never lead with. Beginners study price history; owners' outcomes are written in price *times* the history of the share count. The Broken Ruler is not a metaphor about memory. It is an instruction: after May 2020, every G8 price before it belongs to a different company.

### What the shareholder could have done

Because this book is a manual, not a wake, extract the actionable residue. Three decisions were live in April 2020, and each teaches.

**Before:** Chapter 32 will build the dilution watch-list — thin cash, fixed costs, revenue shock, dividends at risk — and 2020's G8 scores on every line *by late March*, two weeks before the announcement. The raising was not predictable in date; it was predictable in kind. A holder who saw it coming could choose eyes-open: sell, hold cash to participate, or accept dilution knowingly.

**During:** the entitlement offer was the one mercy — the right, per Chapter 5, to defend your fraction at 80 cents. Two thirds of retail holders, frightened or illiquid at the worst moment, let it lapse, and their fractions passed to underwriters at what proved a favourable price. The lesson is brutal but structural: **raisings transfer wealth from those who can't participate to those who can.** Deciding *in advance* whether you would participate in a crisis raising is part of owning any fragile-balance-sheet stock.

**After:** re-value from scratch — new count, new profit base, new confidence — as if encountering the company for the first time. Holders who instead anchored on "getting back to my price" began a wait that arithmetic had already declared unwinnable. Chapter 22 does the full sum; Chapter 33 deals with the hope.

One more accounting note belongs here for completeness. Alongside the raising, G8 carried the era's other new fixture: from 2019, accounting rules required companies to put their **leases on the balance sheet** — every future rent obligation capitalised as a liability, with a matching "right-of-use" asset. For a 400-centre tenant, that meant hundreds of millions of dollars of lease liabilities appearing overnight in the accounts (≈). Nothing about the business changed; the change was visibility. The rent obligations that Chapter 1 called the machine's fixed skeleton became, at last, a number on the balance sheet that any reader could weigh against cash and profit. Chapter 29 adds lease liabilities to the vital-signs dashboard for exactly this reason: they are the fixed-cost machine's commitments, printed.

### The strange gift in the wreckage

One more reading of 2020, through Part II's lens, because it sets up the rebuild.

The pandemic that halved G8's slices also did something no operator could have done: it slammed the supply tap. Construction froze; developers fled; the pipeline of rival centres — already thinning after the flood years — went quiet. And the crisis proved, at maximum stress, the sector's deepest strength: within months, government had demonstrated that childcare would not be allowed to fail — the Subsidy Engine's three purposes, revealed as a survival guarantee. Demand snapped back with reopening; the pie resumed its growth into a suddenly slower-growing forest of forks.

So G8 exited 2020 as a strange hybrid: shareholders halved, balance sheet secured, competition suppressed, sector guarantee proven. A company, in other words, with every excuse gone. The next four years would test what the machine could do with clear weather — and produce the most instructive non-recovery in this book.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** The deepest crash of 2020 never showed on the price chart — it happened in the share count, where a doubling of slices quietly rewrote what any future recovery could ever pay the old owners.

---


## Chapter 20 — The Rebuild (2021–2024) {#ch20}

*Chapter 19 left G8 with clear weather and no excuses. This chapter watches four years of genuine repair — and asks why the one number that mattered refused to follow.*

### The quality turn

The G8 that emerged from 2020 changed its definition of progress. The roll-up era had counted centres; the new management era counted almost everything else. Across 2021–2024 the company worked down a list that reads like the repair manual for every wound this Part has documented.

**Pruning the fleet.** The exits begun in 2019 — the WA sales, the lease-expiry closures — continued as policy: let go the centres that the Beauty Contest had condemned, keep the defensible ones, and stop feeding rent to hopeless rooms. The estate drifted down toward the roughly 400 centres of the mid-2020s, each closure a small admission that some of the roll-up's meals should never have been eaten — and each one, per Chapter 13, one of the sector's rare true exits.

**Arming for the contest.** The greenfield program delivered its purpose-built centres; older keepers got refurbishments. If parents judge on appearances, the fleet's appearance was, at last, being managed rather than inherited.

**The staff turn.** Here the rebuild was most serious, and most aligned with this book's mechanics. Chapter 2 said the staircase's cruellest cost is churn — educators leaving take enrolments with them; Chapter 11 said scarce educators pick their employers. G8 attacked both ends: pay above award (ahead of, then alongside, the sector's 15% funded deal), retention programs, career paths. By 2025 team retention reached **79%**, up 2 points in the year, and teacher vacancies fell by a quarter — numbers this book verified in Part I, and the kind that fill rooms two years later.

**The quality ledger.** Ratings under the national standard climbed until **95% of centres rated Meeting or Exceeding** — a public, parent-visible scoreboard. Alongside them, management began publishing **Net Promoter Scores**, and the measure deserves a beginner's minute because Chapter 28 will use it. NPS comes from one survey question — how likely are you to recommend us? — scored 0 to 10. Subtract the percentage of detractors (0–6) from promoters (9–10) and you get a number between −100 and +100. Its analytical value in childcare is specific: it polls *current* parents, the families whose word-of-mouth is next year's enquiries — an upstream link of Chapter 6's chain, measured quarterly. Record NPS during the rebuild meant existing families, who saw the rooms daily, rated them highly. Hold that fact; it matters enormously for interpreting what happened in 2025, when the trust of families *outside* the gates — who see only headlines — diverged violently from the trust of those within.

Grade the rebuild honestly: this was the right list, executed with evident sincerity. The company that had treated occupancy as an assumption now treated it as the product of a hundred small trust decisions. The machine was being maintained properly, arguably for the first time in its listed life.

### The number that would not come

And yet. Average occupancy across the rebuild years hovered stubbornly in the low-to-mid 70s (≈), grinding sideways while every input metric improved. By FY2024, group average occupancy stood at about **70.7%** — *lower* than the 74% of the crisis year 2018, and a full decade of measures away from the roll-up era's 80s. Four years of repair; no recovery in the one number the whole machine turns on. Why?

Line up the suspects with Part II's tools, because each explains a piece.

**The seats never left.** Chapter 13's sticky exit, still governing. The flood-era centres remained standing in G8's suburbs, now mature, rated, and full of word-of-mouth. Post-COVID construction stayed muted — the tide had turned dear — but muted growth still meant *growth*: the national count passed 9,500 centres. The hangover's arithmetic (excess seats ÷ pie growth) had always promised most of a decade, and it kept its promise.

**The pie wobbled.** Births had peaked in 2018 and slid to the 2023 low — the smallest cohorts arriving at exactly the baby-room end where ratios make occupancy most valuable. Participation and the 2023 subsidy boost pushed the other way. Net: a pie growing, but slower than the rebuild plan needed.

**The contest reset its bar.** The Beauty Contest is relative and undated: G8's refurbished 2005 centre now competed against rivals built in 2019. Improvement that merely narrows the gap does not win back the deciding parent; it only slows the losing.

**And the portfolio remembered.** Averages conceal: the rebuilt estate contained genuinely strong centres at 85%-plus — and a long tail of structurally challenged ones the pruning had not yet reached. The average's stubbornness was partly the tail's arithmetic, the roll-up's location choices still voting, a decade on.

### Capital discipline, learned

The rebuild's least visible repair was to the company's relationship with its own shares, and after Chapters 15–19 the reader can appreciate the reversal.

The roll-up era issued shares constantly — virtuously at first, then desperately in 2020. The rebuild era did the opposite: it **bought shares back**. A buyback is a raising in reverse: the company spends spare cash purchasing its own shares on-market and cancelling them, shrinking the count — un-slicing the pizza, a few slices at a time. From the post-2020 peak of about 840 million shares, buybacks worked the count down toward roughly 770 million by 2025 (≈). Modest against the 2020 dilution, but read the signalling with Chapter 16's currency logic inverted: a management buying its own shares is declaring them cheap with cash rather than words, and returning capital instead of empire-building with it. Dividends resumed on the same logic — modest, covered, resumable.

For the shareholder, the arithmetic is the Pizza Principle's gentle edge: every cancelled share raises profit per remaining share without the business improving at all. For the analyst, the rebuild-era capital pattern — prune centres, shrink count, pay small dividends, hoard resilience — was the strongest available evidence that the lessons of 2014 and 2020 had been institutionally absorbed. Which made the February 2026 decisions — dividend withheld, buyback paused — legible as exactly what they were: the same discipline, now running its defensive protocol.

### The lesson the rebuild teaches

Extract the general principle, because it is one of this book's most important and least intuitive.

**Operating excellence is necessary; it is not sufficient; and it is always late.** Retention, ratings, NPS — Chapter 6's chain places all of them upstream of occupancy, and they did move first, exactly as the chain predicts. But they move occupancy only through the slow valve of parental decisions, made a family at a time, against alternatives that are also improving, in suburbs where the seat arithmetic — set years earlier by other people's capital — still binds. A company can win every variable it controls and still wait years for the scoreboard, because the scoreboard is mostly written by variables it doesn't: seats, births, subsidies, tides.

For the analyst this resolves into a practical test that Part IV will formalise: when a rebuild story is pitched to you — and rebuilds are always pitched — separate the **controllables** (staff, quality, fleet: check the company's own metrics) from the **uncontrollables** (local seats, local pie: check the paperwork and the demographics). Fund the thesis only when *both* columns are turning. G8's 2021–24 controllables turned beautifully. The uncontrollables turned slowly, late, and partially. The occupancy line, which answers to both, split the difference into sideways.

### The rebuild test, in five questions

Compress the lesson into the checklist you will carry to every turnaround pitch, in any sector.

**One: are the controllables actually moving?** Not promised — printed. Retention, quality ratings, customer scores, in the company's own periodic numbers, trending across multiple periods. G8 2021–24: yes, clearly.

**Two: what do the uncontrollables say?** Local seat growth, local demand, the cycle's phase — the paperwork and demographics of Part II. G8 2021–24: slow improvement, from a deep hole, with the seat overhang intact.

**Three: what is the gap between the two clocks?** Controllables move in quarters; uncontrollables in years. Estimate the wait honestly — Chapter 13's division, excess seats over pie growth — and ask whether the balance sheet and your patience both cover it.

**Four: is the market paying for the rebuild already?** A turnaround priced at a recovery multiple offers no reward for being right and full punishment for being early. G8's unconvinced ≈$1.30 said the market was paying for little — the honest attraction of the setup.

**Five: what can delete the thesis?** Chapter 18's question, now standing. For a childcare rebuild the list was knowable: a demand shock, a policy reversal, a trust event. The rebuild years passed questions one through four respectably. Question five was waiting at the end of them.

Five questions, none requiring inside access. They will not make turnarounds safe — nothing does — but they separate rebuilds that are *working but unpaid* from rebuilds that are merely narrated. The distinction is worth most of what Part V has to teach.

### The quiet years' quiet dividends

Give the rebuild its due before the storm, because its work was not wasted — it was banked in forms the occupancy line doesn't show.

A company that retains educators enters any future recovery with rooms it can legally staff — no small thing in a shortage sector where rivals cap enrolments for want of qualified staff. A company with 95% quality ratings holds a public credential that cannot be improvised when trust is suddenly questioned. A company that has already pruned its worst leases faces the next crisis with fewer hopeless obligations. And a company that suspended dividends, then resumed modest ones, with debt rebuilt to manageable levels, holds the balance-sheet slack whose absence forced 2020's halving of the slice. By late 2024 the shares had drifted into the $1.20–$1.40 range (≈) — pricing, per the Price Formula, a modest profit stream at an unconvinced multiple: the market's verdict on a sound machine in a still-crowded field, promised nothing.

The rebuild, in short, had made G8 *resilient* — bought insurance against the crashes this Part had already catalogued: multiple, profit, promise, dilution. Four crashes, four hard-learned defences.

The fifth crash came for the one asset no operating metric measures, no rating certifies, and no balance sheet protects. It arrived on the first day of July 2025, and it attacked the only thing the parent — Chapter 11's absolute, veto-holding judge — had ever truly been buying.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** A rebuild can win every controllable — staff, quality, fleet — and still wait years on occupancy, because the scoreboard is mostly written by seats, births, and subsidies that no operator controls.

---


## Chapter 21 — The Wound (2025– ): The Trust Crisis {#ch21}

*Four crashes taught the machine to defend its multiple, its profits, its promises, and its balance sheet. The fifth attacked none of these.*

### A note on how this chapter is written

This chapter involves alleged crimes against children. The book states the public facts — charges, pleas, dates — soberly and without detail, because the details belong to a courtroom, the accused is entitled to a fair trial and is presumed innocent of what he has denied, and above all because families were harmed and this book will not make their suffering a spectacle. The book's subject is the business consequence: what happens to a childcare company when trust itself is wounded. That is the only question analysed here.

### The public facts

On **1 July 2025**, Victoria Police charges against a former childcare educator, Joshua Dale Brown, became public. The charges related to alleged offences against young children at Melbourne centres where he had worked — initially involving one centre in Point Cook operated under one of G8's brands, with later charges relating to further victims and centres in Melbourne's west.

The scale of the disclosure was what made it a national event. Brown had worked across **23 childcare services between January 2017 and May 2025**, for multiple operators, while holding a valid Working With Children Check throughout. Authorities contacted thousands of families whose children had attended those services during those years, and recommended precautionary health screening for many of them. For weeks, childcare safety led national news bulletins, parliamentary question times, and — the point for this chapter — dinner-table conversations in every suburb in the country.

The legal timeline, as at this book's writing: the charge sheet evolved as the investigation proceeded, standing at **153 charges relating to 12 alleged victims at four Melbourne centres**. On **29 May 2026**, Brown formally entered **not guilty pleas** to all charges — described by his barrister as a holding position while legal discussions continued, with counsel indicating he accepted a significant portion of the alleged offending. The matter was committed to the **County Court of Victoria**, with a directions hearing on **23 June 2026** and a trial expected but not yet fixed, likely in 2027. He remains in custody. Nothing further about the case belongs in this book.

### The chain, attacked at its source

Now the analysis. Recall Chapter 6's chain: **trust → searches → enquiries → tours → enrolments → occupancy → revenue → profit → price.** Every prior crash in this history entered the chain midstream — supply hit enrolments, COVID hit attendance, downgrades hit the price directly. The 2025 crisis was categorically different: it struck the first link, the one upstream of everything, for which no operating metric exists and from which every other number flows.

And it struck with a horrible precision. Chapter 11 established the parent as the only real customer, voting on trust *first*, absolutely, with a total and unappealable veto. Chapter 20 banked G8's rebuilt credentials — 95% quality ratings, record NPS, awarded staff. The events of July 2025 asked every parent a question those credentials could not answer: *the system of checks itself failed to prevent this — how do I know any centre is safe?* For some parents, no answer sufficed: enquiries paused, tours went unbooked, and some enrolled families left. Not all, not even most — but Part I taught you what a machine at 70% occupancy does with a few points of lost demand. The staircase holds the costs; the lever multiplies the loss.

Watch the chain transmit, link by link, in the verified record. **Enquiries first**: through late 2025, families under cost pressure and trust pressure enquired less — and on 4 November 2025, G8 told the market so, cutting guidance from about $115 million to $91–98 million on "weaker inquiry volumes", the shares falling 13.7% by late morning. **Occupancy next**: spot occupancy 68.3% (down 6.6 points) at that update; full-year 2025 average **65.8%** (down 4.9 points); and by mid-February 2026 — the fresh-year re-enrolment season, when the prior year's paused enquiries came due — spot occupancy of **54.4%**, down 7.5 points on a year earlier. **Then revenue**: $948.2 million, down 7.2%. **Then profit**: underlying operating profit $93.3 million, down 18.9% — the lever converting a 7% revenue fall as Chapter 3 promised. **Then the price**, down with each disclosure. The chain ran in its exact textbook order, over eight months, in public.

### Inside versus outside the gates

One split in the 2025 data illuminates how trust actually breaks, and Chapter 20's NPS explanation set it up.

Through the crisis, G8's measures of *enrolled* families — the parents inside the gates, who meet the educators daily — held up remarkably: retention of staff improved, quality ratings stood at records, and internal advocacy measures stayed strong. The catastrophic numbers were all *outside* the gates: enquiries, tours, new enrolments — the decisions of families who knew the company only through headlines. Spot occupancy's fall to 54.4% was concentrated in the failure to *replace* the natural outflow (children ageing into school, families moving) with new arrivals, compounded at the January re-enrolment season when leaving is easiest.

This split is the general anatomy of a trust crisis in any consumer business: existing customers, rich in first-hand information, discount the headlines; potential customers, holding nothing but the headlines, walk. The chain of Chapter 6 marks the fracture precisely — the wound sits between *trust* and *enquiries*, upstream of every relationship the company actually has. Which yields the watching instruction Part IV will formalise: in a trust crisis, the company's own customer metrics will look deceptively fine. Watch the strangers, not the family — search interest, enquiry commentary, tour volumes — because recovery, when it comes, must arrive through the same door the strangers stopped entering.

The government's response ran on schedule for Chapter 11's playbook: reviews and inquiries at state and federal level, momentum for tightened working-with-children checks, a national push on CCTV in centres, and accelerated reform of safety regulation (≈, details still evolving as this book was written) — visible, fast, regulatory, cost-raising, while the Subsidy Engine itself was defended and even expanded. Every operator inherited the compliance bill; the named operator inherited the headlines too.

### The statutory loss, decoded

The headline of the February 2026 results was grimmer still: a **statutory loss of $303.3 million**. A beginner needs this number decoded, because it mixes two different kinds of bad news.

Inside it sat a **$349.1 million goodwill impairment**. Plain words: when G8's roll-up bought centres above the value of their desks and cots, accounting recorded the excess as "goodwill" — the purchased expectation of future profits. An impairment is the auditor-supervised admission that some of those expectations are dead: a write-down of the balance sheet, not a cheque written this year. No cash left the company in that $349 million; the cash story was the milder (still painful) operating decline. But do not dismiss it as "merely accounting": it is the company formally conceding that a slab of what the roll-up era paid for — Chapter 15's meals — will never earn its keep. The 2010–2014 machine received its final invoice in 2026.

The cash-preserving responses followed the 2020 playbook, earlier and less desperately: **no final dividend** (a 2-cent interim had been paid in October 2025), the share **buyback paused**, and roughly **40 underperforming centres closed or suspended** (≈) in a cost reset. Chapter 32's dilution watch-list should now be open in your mind — thin margin, falling occupancy, dividends stopped — with one difference from 2020: this time the balance sheet entered the storm with the rebuild's banked slack, and about 770 million shares, not 460, would bear any future raising.

### What makes this crash different — and what to watch

File the fifth crash in the taxonomy: Crash One, the multiple; Two, profit via supply; Three, trust in guidance; Four, the share count; **Five, trust in the product itself** — demand attacked at its root. Its distinguishing features matter for analysis.

It is **unbounded by any prior model**: no occupancy series contains a national trust shock, so history-based forecasting fails exactly when needed — Chapter 18's humility, again. It **runs on the court calendar, not the business calendar**: directions hearings, a 2027 trial, verdicts and appeals — each a date when the story re-enters every news bulletin, and each public and knowable in advance (Chapter 28 makes the court list a dashboard dial). It **summons the regulator**: Chapter 11's player analysis predicted a safety scandal brings visible, fast regulation — and 2025–26 delivered reviews, tightened checks, and momentum for CCTV and reform, raising every operator's costs while defending the subsidy engine itself. And it is **asymmetric across the sector**: every operator suffered scrutiny, but the operator whose brand stood in the headlines suffered the Beauty Contest's cruellest round — parents choosing between two centres now weighed one with its name in the news.

Meanwhile, the professionals disagreed with each other in public — the sharpest such split in the company's history. In late February 2026, in the same week of the same falling price, the superannuation fund HostPlus **sold** its stake down from 6.33% to 5.29%, while the contrarian manager Allan Gray **bought** its holding up from 18.68% to 19.87%, and a director added shares personally. Smart money, disagreeing with itself at maximum volume — Chapter 29 will teach you to read such filings. The disagreement *is* the information: at 54.4% spot occupancy, with a trial pending, the range of honest opinion about this company had never been wider.

For the reader who will watch this crash unfold beyond the book's last page, the watchables are all public and dated: the County Court list for each hearing and eventually a verdict; the company's occupancy disclosures at results and trading updates, with the enquiry commentary that leads them; the reform announcements that will set the sector's new compliance costs; and the substantial-holder filings recording, fortnight by fortnight, which professionals are voting which way with money. Part IV assigns each of these a dial, a frequency, and a trigger.

That is where the history ends, because it is where the present begins — the book was written inside this crash, not after it. What no one knows yet: where trust's floor lies, what the 3 Day Guarantee's demand wave does against it, and what the court calendar holds. Part IV builds the instruments for exactly this kind of unknowable; Part V, the discipline for acting inside it. But first, one piece of unfinished arithmetic — a promise made in Chapter 5 about a famous number.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** The fifth crash attacked the chain's first link — trust in the product itself — and no operating metric, quality rating, or balance sheet can defend a childcare company from the parent's absolute veto.

---


## Chapter 22 — The $5 Illusion {#ch22}

*Five crashes are on the table. This chapter does the arithmetic that beginners skip — and buries the most expensive sentence in investing.*

### The sentence

Somewhere right now, an investor is looking at G8's chart, seeing today's price under a dollar (≈), seeing 2014's peak above five, and thinking the thought this chapter exists to kill: *"It was $5 once. Even halfway back would be a triple."*

The thought feels like analysis. It contains a price, a comparison, and an upside. It is not analysis. It is a memory wearing analysis's clothes, and Part I gave you the tool that exposes it: the Price Formula, price = (profit ÷ shares) × confidence. The $5 of 2014 was produced by a specific profit, a specific share count, and a specific confidence. To treat $5 as a destination, all three must be recoverable. Walk through each, with this Part's verified history in hand.

### The share count: the ruler, re-marked twice

Start with the denominator, because it alone is fatal.

The $5 shares of 2014 were slices of a company with roughly 300-something million slices (≈) — the roll-up years grew the count with every raising, which was the model working as designed. By 2019, the count stood near **460 million**. Then April–May 2020 added roughly **376 million more** at 80 cents — the halved slice of Chapter 19 — taking the count to about **840 million**. Buybacks in the rebuild years trimmed it back to roughly **770 million** by 2025–26.

So today's share is a claim on roughly **one seven-hundred-and-seventy-millionth** of the company; 2014's five-dollar share was a claim more than twice as large. For today's slice to deserve $5, the whole pizza must be worth more than *twice* what the 2014 market — at peak enthusiasm, peak multiple, peak belief — priced the entire company. "Back to $5" is not a recovery. It is a demand that the company become, per the market's judgment, more than double its best self ever. The ruler has been re-marked; measuring today's price against 2014's is measuring with the Broken Ruler, and every centimetre lies.

Do the full-company sums to make it vivid. 2014: ≈$5 × ≈330m shares ≈ **$1.6–1.7 billion** of market value (≈). Today: a sub-dollar price × ≈770m shares ≈ **$600–700 million** (≈). The company's whole-pizza value has fallen by roughly 60% — painful, but *far less* than the 80%-plus fall in the price per slice. The difference between those two falls **is** dilution's invoice, delivered to whoever held the old slices. Company survival and shareholder recovery, Chapter 5 said, are different events. Here they are, quantified, twelve years apart on one chart.

### The profit: what would "back" even mean?

Now the numerator. The 2014-era company earned its profits on peak-roll-up economics: acquisitions still accreting, occupancy in the 80s (≈), wages pre-squeeze, and about 21 brands' worth of purchased earnings still fresh. The 2026 company earns $93 million of underlying operating profit on 65.8% average occupancy — with the goodwill impairment formally certifying that a slab of the old purchased earnings is never coming back.

Could profit double from here? Part I's machinery says: in principle, yes — that is exactly what operating leverage promises. Chapter 2's staircase runs upward; returning children land on wages already paid; a genuine occupancy recovery from the mid-60s toward the low 80s would transform profit, plausibly by the required multiple. The bull case for today's G8 is precisely this lever, and this book has taught you to respect it.

But notice what the lever needs: **occupancy points that must be won from a field this Part has mapped** — 9,786 centres and rising, a pie that just printed its first decline in children, a Beauty Contest against younger fleets, and a trust wound running on a court calendar. The $5 illusion smuggles in the assumption that those points are G8's by right, merely mislaid. Part II said otherwise: nobody owns occupancy. It is contested, suburb by suburb, against rivals, demographics, and memory.

### The confidence: the years nobody will pay twice

Last, the multiple — and here the illusion is subtlest. The 2014 price paid roughly 20 years of profit (≈) for a story: endless cheap acquisitions, compounding forever. That multiple was not a market fact awaiting return; it was a *belief* that Crash One specifically, permanently re-examined. The 2026 market pays a single-digit-to-low-teens multiple (≈) for a story with five crashes in it, told by a management whose 2019 and 2025 guidance both broke, about a product whose safety made national news.

Confidence can rebuild — trust signals, court resolutions, delivered guidance can all lift the multiple, and Part IV watches each. But "the multiple returns to 2014" would require the market to forget what it learned at G8's own expense. Markets misprice; they rarely un-learn. An analyst may reasonably model the multiple improving. Modelling it *restored* is patience with hope wearing a spreadsheet.

### What honest $5 arithmetic looks like

To bury the illusion properly, replace it with the sum it pretends to be. What would today's G8 have to become for $5 to be *fair*, by this book's own formula?

Price = (profit ÷ shares) × multiple. Fix the knowns: 770 million shares. Grant a healthy-company multiple of 15 — generous for this history, defensible for a trusted, recovered operator. Solve for profit: $5 × 770m ÷ 15 ≈ **$257 million** of sustained per-year profit. Today's underlying operating profit: $93 million — and note the sums here mix profit definitions loosely, which only *understates* the challenge. Roughly, then: profit must nearly **triple**, and stay tripled, and be believed.

Could it? Sketch the bull's path with Part I's lever: recovery from 65.8% average occupancy to the low 80s across ≈400 centres, each returning child landing on paid-for wages, plus fee growth at the cap — a doubling of profit is within the lever's honest reach over some years. A tripling requires *more than recovery*: expansion, margin transformation, or a decade of compounding — all while 9,786 rivals contest every point. So the honest verdict on $5 is not "impossible"; it is "a specific, heroic, multi-year scenario, currently priced by the market as unlikely." That sentence, unlike the illusion, is analysis: it names what must happen, over what period, against what field. Chapter 31 will price scenarios exactly this way — and less heroic ones too, because the useful question was never "can it reach $5?" but "what is *this* company, with *these* slices, worth across the futures that might actually occur?"

Notice, finally, what the honest sum did that the illusion never does: it produced a research agenda. Tripling requires occupancy points — so go watch the occupancy signals. It requires a believed multiple — so go watch the trust dials and the guidance record. The illusion ends conversations ("it was $5 once"); the arithmetic starts them. That is the reliable way to tell memory from analysis in any stock you will ever study: analysis always hands you homework.

### The general lesson: prices are not rubber bands

Pull the three strands into the rule, because the illusion is universal — every crashed stock in every market has its own $5.

**An old share price is evidence of nothing except what old profit, old share counts, and old beliefs once multiplied to.** Prices are not rubber bands stretched from a "true" level, storing return energy.
