Like-for-like or nothing (17). Misses reprice the promiser (18). Emergency money is priced by the emergency (19). Controllables ≠ uncontrollables (20). The parent's veto is absolute (21). Value what exists (22). No dial without a cause; label the mirrors (24). Three scenarios, named disagreement (31). Dilution telegraphs in documents (32). Consequences, not verdicts; signals, not hope (33). Thesis and kills before dollars (34). Re-weight at regime changes only (35).

A reader comparing this sheet against professional research will notice what is missing: price targets, ratings, catalysts-by-quarter. The omissions are the method. Targets were replaced by scenario ranges (Ch 31), ratings by named disagreements with the price's weights, catalysts by a calendar of *scheduled evidence* — court dates, ledger prints, results days — none of which promises a price response, all of which promises information. The sheet competes on a different axis entirely: not sharper forecasts, but earlier, cheaper, better-organised observation.

### Standing the dashboard up: the first ninety days

A reader beginning from zero should build in this order — each stage usable before the next begins.

**Days 1–7: the spine.** Subscribe to the company's announcement feed; bookmark the ledger, the register, the RBA calendar; start the weekly heat-and-Trends log (twenty minutes establishes the habit before the infrastructure). **Days 8–30: the gauges.** Build the national SO sheet from the two downloads (one evening); pick the 30-centre sample; run the first call round to establish baselines — first readings are worthless as signals and priceless as baselines. **Days 31–60: the deep dials.** The register sweep for the sampled radii; council portal bookmarks; the guidance table built backward from five years of announcements (the single most instructive exercise in the book — Part III reconstructs itself in your own spreadsheet). **Days 61–90: the first sitting.** A full quarterly review, ending in the first scenario sheet and — if a thesis emerges — the written page and kills of Chapter 34. Total: perhaps six working days spread over a quarter, after which the standing cost is the three-days-a-quarter of Chapter 30.

**When a trigger fires**, the protocol is always the same four steps, in order: *verify* (against the source, not the headline — Chapter 24's discipline); *classify* (which scenario clause does this reading touch?); *re-weight* (per the written rules, logged); *check the thesis page* (kill, tranche, or hold — the page decides, not the day's adrenaline). Nothing in the protocol contains the word "immediately" except verification; the dashboard's entire architecture exists so that no decision ever needs to be made at the speed of news.

### Using the page

On tools: everything above runs in one spreadsheet workbook and a diary. Tabs: the SO gauge, the local sample, the guidance table, the heat/Trends log, the quarterly sitting log, the thesis page, this dashboard. No subscriptions are load-bearing (a news database helps dial one; the free search version suffices), no code is required, and the deliberate low-tech is a feature twice over: an apparatus you built entirely by hand is one you understand entirely (Chapter 25's principle), and one with no vendor to discontinue it. Automation is a fine luxury *after* a year of manual quarters — never before, because the manual labour is where the intuition Chapter 30 called compounding actually forms.

And the system's full inventory, for the avoidance of grandeur: **two pages and a workbook.** This dashboard page schedules the looking; the thesis page (Chapter 34) owns the deciding; the workbook holds the series and the logs. A reader flipping back through thirty-six chapters of machinery should land on how little survives compression — which is the point. Complexity lived in the *understanding*; the *operation* was always meant to fit beside the kettle.

Three notes complete the book's toolkit. **The sheet is a scheduler, not an oracle:** its cells say where to look and when to worry — every *decision* still routes through the written thesis, the scenario weights, and the kill criteria, which live on your one page of thesis, beside this one page of dials. Two pages, total. **The sheet is transferable:** re-title the dials and this same architecture — machine, chain, cycle, panel — audits a gym chain, a hotel REIT, an airline, any fixed-cost machine playing an occupancy game; the epilogue takes that walk. **And the sheet expires:** triggers were calibrated to this regime, this company, this wound. The dials' *logic* is durable; their thresholds are maintenance items, Chapter 25's log-tab rules applying to the dashboard itself.

The apparatus is complete: a machine understood, a field mapped, a history read, a panel built, a discipline installed — and all of it, finally, on a page an ordinary person can run in three days a quarter. Whether it is ever pointed at this particular stock again matters less than that it exists: the reader who assembled it can now audit a fixed-cost machine anywhere, which was the education the running example was always in service of. What remains is only to look up from this one company and notice how much of the world is playing the same game.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** The whole book fits on one page — every dial with its source, schedule, and pre-committed trigger — but the page schedules attention; only the written thesis, weights, and kill criteria ever make a decision.

---


# Epilogue — The Occupancy Game Is Everywhere {#epilogue}

*One company, thirty-six chapters. Now look up.*

You have spent this book inside one Australian childcare company, and it would be fair to wonder how much of the education travels. Here is the answer: nearly all of it — because the machine you now understand is one of the commonest structures in the economy, wearing different uniforms.

Walk through a day and count the machines. The **gym** you pass at dawn signed a ten-year lease and staffs its floor whether eleven members swipe in or eleven hundred; its profit lives in the last few hundred memberships above break-even, its crashes come when three rival gyms open in the same postcode on cheap money, and its "occupancy" is called utilisation. The **hotel** on the corner is a childcare centre with minibars: enormous fixed costs, revenue = rooms × rate, a beauty contest against every newer tower, a capital cycle that overbuilds in every boom, and an operator who controls service but not the six cranes on the skyline. The **airline** you fly is the machine at its most violent — a fleet of leased fixed costs moving at 900 kilometres an hour, profit balanced on the last five passengers per flight, "load factor" its word for occupancy, and a history of rescue-by-dilution so reliable it is almost a business model. The **cinema**, the **restaurant**, the **co-working floor**, the **university**, the **hospital ward**, the **freight railway**, the **data centre** — machines, all of them, paying for capacity whether the seats fill or not.

For every one of them, your toolkit applies with only the nouns changed. Find the fixed costs and the staircase (union rosters, lease stacks, minimum crews). Find break-even and ask how close the machine lives to it. Find the pie and the forks — the local demand and who is adding capacity into it — and remember that nobody anywhere owns the tap: hotel developers, gym franchisors, and airline fleet planners all run on property maths and borrowed optimism, not on the incumbents' utilisation. Date the demand boosts and start the two-to-four-year clock on the answering supply. Check the tide, because every leased-asset industry is secretly an interest-rate instrument. Ask what is knowable years ahead (aircraft orders are published; hotel pipelines are published; gym franchise sales are announced) and months ahead (bookings, searches, review velocity — every consumer machine leaks upstream signals now). Compute the vital signs before believing any recovery story, because fixed-cost machines are dilution's natural prey in every industry, and the tells are the same six everywhere. And when a crashed one tempts you with its old price, run the slice count first.

The deeper transfers are the disciplines, which never needed childcare at all. Mean reversion saves industries, not companies — true of airlines (the industry always recovers; the shareholders of particular airlines, rarely) exactly as it was true of ABC. Prices are consequences, not verdicts, in every market that trades. Effort is not evidence, whatever the sector under study. And the chain — trust, enquiries, commitments, utilisation, revenue, profit, price — runs through every business that must be chosen by a human being, which is every business. Somewhere upstream of every reported number, always, someone is deciding whether to walk through a door. The whole craft of this book is watching doors instead of reports.

A last word on the company that taught you all this. As the book closes, G8 Education's story is unresolved: a wounded machine, a court calendar, a demand wave, a frozen pipeline, professionals disagreeing at full volume. This book has deliberately not told you how the story ends, because it cannot know, and — the philosophy's last word — *neither can anyone else selling certainty about it*. What the book hopes instead is precise: that its reader can now watch the resolution with instruments rather than moods; that whatever the outcome, the reader will be able to say *which dial moved first*; and that the education survives the example, whichever way the example goes.

One reminder, made at the beginning and owed again at the end: this book is education, not financial advice. Nothing in it is a recommendation to buy or sell any security. Its numbers were gathered honestly, marked ≈ where uncertain, and will age; its methods were offered so you can gather your own. The author has confessed his own instrument's failure in these pages, which should calibrate your trust in everything else. Decisions, and their consequences, are yours — which was, in the end, the whole point of learning any of this.

The rooms are out there, filling and emptying. You know how to watch them now.

---

> **The one thing to remember:** Everywhere a business pays for capacity whether customers come or not — gyms, hotels, airlines, cinemas — the whole toolkit transfers with the nouns changed, because the occupancy game is everywhere, and now you know how to watch the doors.

---

## Appendix B — The SO Index: Spreadsheet Recipe and Formulas {#app-b}

The gauge of Chapter 25, as bare instructions. Reminder before use: **the SO index is a lagging depth gauge, not a forecaster** (Chapter 24's confession). Its faults are listed at the end, deliberately.

### Workbook layout

One workbook, four tabs: `NATIONAL`, `LOCAL`, `LOG`, `NOTES`.

### Tab 1 — NATIONAL

One row per quarter. Columns:

| Col | Name | Entry / formula |
|---|---|---|
| A | Quarter | e.g. `2025-Q4` |
| B | Children in subsidised care | From CCS quarterly report (all care types or CBDC-only — pick one, keep it forever; note choice in NOTES) |
| C | Average weekly hours per child | Same report |
| D | Weekly hours demanded | `=B×C` |
| E | Child-days per week | `=D/10` |
| F | CBDC services | From ACECQA register/snapshot, same quarter |
| G | Nominal capacity (child-days) | `=F×1000` |
| H | **SO index** | `=100×E/G` |
| I | YoY change | `=H−H[same quarter last year]` |
| J | Rule-change flag | Text: any policy/definition change this quarter (e.g. `2026-Q1: 3-Day Guarantee starts`) |

Reference values for checking your build: 2014 ≈ **46.8** (≈3.09m child-days; ≈6,600 services); 2024 ≈ **41.5** (≈3.97m; 9,571); 2025-Q4 ≈ **40.6** (≈3.97m; 9,786).

Reading rules: use **changes and YoY comparisons only** — the level is unit-dependent and means nothing absolutely; never compare raw values across a J-flagged boundary; the derivative (is the fall flattening?) is the most informative feature.

### Tab 2 — LOCAL (one block per sampled suburb)

For each of 20–40 sampled catchments around the operator's centres:

| Col | Name | Entry / formula |
|---|---|---|
| A | Suburb / centre ID | Your label |
| B | Approved places within 2 km | Sum from ACECQA register download (filter by distance from centre's coordinates) |
| C | Local capacity (child-days/wk) | `=B×5` |
| D | Children 0–4 in catchment | ABS small-area data (≈) |
| E | Participation rate | Regional rate from CCS data (≈) |
| F | Average days/child | Regional, from CCS hours ÷ 10 (≈) |
| G | Local demand (child-days/wk) | `=D×E×F` |
| H | **Local gauge** | `=G/C` |
| I | New places approved this qtr (2 km) | From register delta — **this is the leading column** |
| J | Pipeline places (DA lodged/approved, 2 km) | From council portal sweep (Ch 26) |
| K | Flash flag | `=IF((I+J)/B>0.10,"FLASH","")` |

Reading rules: column H is a crude level (three ≈ inputs) — compare *across* suburbs and *over time*, not to any absolute standard; columns I–K are hard facts and carry the warning value; a FLASH means re-price that suburb's centre in the portfolio view, per Chapter 26's thresholds.

### Tab 3 — LOG

One row per update: date, quarter entered, anything anomalous in the sources, what the gauge said, and — filled retrospectively — what later data showed. This is the miss log (Chapter 25's maintenance rule three) and the instrument's retirement evidence, per Chapter 24's rule four.

### Tab 4 — NOTES

Permanent record of: the B-column definition choice; the 1,000 and ×5 conventions; every rebasing (old and new series kept side by side); the standing faults list, copied here so it travels with the file:

1. **Lagging** — both inputs are history; the gauge confirms, never warns.
2. **Crude capacity** — the 1,000 convention ignores real places and centre-size drift (national tab only; local tab uses real places).
3. **Subsidised care only** — rule changes shift the series without children moving; watch the J flags.
4. **Quarterly + publication lag** — up to ~2 quarters blind; use Chapter 25's pencil-splice between prints, labelled pencil.
5. **Averaged hours** — attendance-pattern shifts move child-days and rooms differently (Chapter 2).

### Update routine

Quarterly, ≈1 hour national + ≈2 hours local once built: download CCS report → fill B, C; download register/snapshot → fill F and local B/I; sweep council portals → fill J; write the LOG row; annotate J flags. Done. Resist daily; the sources don't update daily, and the gauge's dignity is its schedule.

## Appendix C — G8 Education Master Timeline, 2010–2026 {#app-c}

Year by year, with the two numbers the book taught you to carry: the share count (the denominator) and occupancy (the machine's fullness). Values marked ≈ are estimates or reported figures the author could not fully re-verify against primary documents; occupancy definitions vary across eras (like-for-like vs group average — Chapter 17) and are labelled where known. Blank cells mean no reliable figure was found; honesty over completeness.

| Year | Key events | Shares on issue (≈) | Occupancy | Share price (year context, ≈) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2010 | Roll-up begins post-ABC vacuum; early acquisitions funded by share issues | growing from a small base | — | well under $1 |
| 2011 | Acquisition cadence builds; cash rate ≈4.75% and falling | growing | — | ≈$1 |
| 2012 | Multi-centre purchases at ≈4× EBIT | growing | high (≈80s, era norm) | ≈$1–2 |
| 2013 | ≈$3.00 (Sep); investment capacity $65–100m/yr; sector ≈6,300 centres | growing | ≈80s | $2–3 |
| 2014 | Peak roll-up: 63 centres for $104.7m (Feb); Sterling $228m at 5.8× (Mar); $150m raise; $50m notes; 114 centres H1; ≈400 centres total | ≈330m (≈) | ≈80s | peak above $5 (late 2014) |
| 2015 | Crash One completes: multiple halves on unchanged profits; hostile scrip bid for Affinity fails — Takeovers Panel "unacceptable circumstances" (Oct) | ≈360–380m (≈) | ≈80s, softening | ≈$3, down ≈40% from peak |
| 2016 | Flood begins: cheap-money construction wave opens on G8's doorstep; cash rate 1.5% | ≈380m (≈) | declining | ≈$3–4 volatile |
| 2017 | Oversupply grinds; beauty contest against purpose-built rivals | ≈450m (≈, raisings) | declining | ≈$3–4 |
| 2018 | Crash Two's trough year: LFL occupancy 74% (−1.9pp); revenue $858.2m (+7.7%); underlying EBIT $136.3m (−12.7%); CCS starts (Jul) | ≈460m | 74% (LFL) | fell to ≈$2–3; −13% on results day (Feb 2019) |
| 2019 | Crash Three: guidance $140–145m (Aug) cut to $131–134m (Nov); −17.8% in a week to $2.13; sold 25 WA centres; closed 16; 44-greenfield/$155m pipeline; 2-km competitor growth 1.0% vs 4.2% national; LFL occupancy +1.1% — first gain since 2015 | ≈460m | ≈75%, improving | $2.13 low; ≈$2 at year end |
| 2020 | Crash Four: COVID; free-childcare rations; $301m raised at $0.80 (Apr–May): $227m placement + $75m entitlement (33% take-up); ≈376m new shares; dividends suspended; leases onto balance sheet | ≈460m → ≈840m | collapsed, then partial rebound | ≈$0.80–1.30 |
| 2021 | Rebuild begins: pruning, quality push, staff investment | ≈840m | ≈70s low | ≈$1 |
| 2022 | Rates tighten (May onward); pipeline starts freezing; rebuild continues | ≈820m (buybacks begin, ≈) | ≈70s low | ≈$1 |
| 2023 | Cheaper Child Care (Jul) lifts demand; sector occupancy peaks (CBDC 81.8% Dec) | ≈800m (≈) | ≈70s | ≈$1–1.30 |
| 2024 | Rebuild metrics peak: retention rising, ratings 90s%; group avg occupancy ≈70.7%; births trough (2023: 286,998) reaches baby rooms | ≈780m (≈) | ≈70.7% (group avg) | ≈$1.20–1.40 |
| 2025 | Crash Five: charges public 1 Jul; enquiries weaken; 4 Nov downgrade ≈$115m → $91–98m, shares −13.7%; spot occupancy 68.3% (2 Nov, −6.6pp); FY: revenue $948.2m (−7.2%), underlying EBIT $93.3m (−18.9%), statutory loss $303.3m incl. $349.1m impairment; avg occupancy 65.8% (−4.9pp); 2c interim div (Oct) | ≈770m | 65.8% (avg); 68.3% (spot, Nov) | sub-$1 by year end (≈) |
| 2026 | Feb results: no final dividend; buyback paused; ≈40 centres closed/suspended; spot occupancy 54.4% (mid-Feb, −7.5pp); HostPlus 6.33%→5.29% vs Allan Gray 18.68%→19.87% (same week); 3 Day Guarantee starts (5 Jan); not guilty pleas (29 May); County Court directions (23 Jun); trial expected 2027; 395 centres | ≈770m | 54.4% (spot, mid-Feb) | ≈$0.60–0.90 (≈, volatile) |

### The two-line summary the table exists to teach

From 2014's peak to 2026: the price per share fell over 80%; the whole-company market value fell ≈60%; the difference is ≈440 million new slices. And occupancy — the number everything else in this book runs on — travelled from the mid-80s to 54.4% spot, with every crash's mechanism (multiple, supply, guidance, dilution, trust) legible in the rows above.

*Rows 2010–2013, 2016–2017 and all share-count interpolations are the roughest entries (≈); pin them against annual reports before quoting. Sources for verified figures: the Endnotes & Sources section following Appendix E.*

## Appendix D — Plain-Words Glossary {#app-d}

Every term the book taught, alphabetically, each with the chapter that introduced it. The named metaphors — this book's own technical nouns — follow at the end.

- **3 Day Guarantee** — from January 2026: 72 subsidised hours a fortnight for all eligible families. (Ch 9)
- **Activity test** — the old rule linking subsidised hours to parents' work or study; abolished 5 January 2026. (Ch 9)
- **Award** — a legal minimum pay scale for a type of work in Australia. (Ch 2)
- **Break-even** — the level of business (here, occupancy) where profit is exactly zero. (Ch 3)
- **Capital cycle** — the rhythm: high returns attract capacity, which destroys returns, which repels capacity, which restores returns. (Ch 13)
- **Capital raising** — a company creates new shares and sells them for cash. (Ch 5)
- **Cash rate** — the Reserve Bank's benchmark interest rate; the anchor for all other rates. (Ch 12)
- **Casual (staff)** — a worker with no fixed weekly hours, paid a higher hourly rate in exchange. (Ch 2)
- **Centre-based day care (CBDC)** — the official category for ordinary long-day childcare centres. (Ch 8)
- **Child Care Subsidy (CCS)** — the federal payment (since July 2018) covering part of each family's fee, paid directly to the centre. (Ch 9)
- **Child-day** — one child attending care for one 10-hour day; the book's unit for the demand pie. (Ch 7)
- **Contribution** — the fee from one customer minus the variable cost that customer causes. (Ch 3)
- **Costs** — the money a business pays out to run itself. (Ch 1)
- **Development application (DA)** — the request to a council for permission to build. (Ch 8)
- **Dilution** — each old share becomes a smaller fraction of the company because new shares now exist. (Ch 5)
- **Enquiry** — a parent's first contact with a centre: a call, a form, an email. (Ch 6)
- **Entitlement offer** — an offer of new shares to all existing shareholders, in proportion to holdings. (Ch 5)
- **Fixed costs** — costs that stay the same whether the business is busy or empty; rent is the classic one. (Ch 1)
- **Gap fee** — the part of the fee the family pays after subsidy; the only price the family feels. (Ch 9)
- **Goodwill / impairment** — the premium paid above a purchased business's physical assets, recorded as an asset; an impairment is the audited admission that some of it will never earn its keep. (Ch 21)
- **Guidance** — a company's own public forecast of its coming profit. (Ch 4)
- **Hourly rate cap** — the ceiling fee per hour up to which subsidy applies. (Ch 9)
- **Lagging indicator** — a number that confirms what already happened. (Ch 6)
- **Leading indicator** — a number that moves before the outcome you care about. (Ch 6)
- **Lease** — a long-term rental contract; the rent runs for the life of the contract, busy or empty. (Ch 1)
- **Like-for-like (same-centre)** — comparing only the centres owned in both periods; the honest lens on acquisitive companies. (Ch 17)
- **Listed company** — a company whose shares anyone can buy and sell on a stock exchange. (Ch 1)
- **Loss** — negative profit: costs were larger than revenue. (Ch 1)
- **Margin loan** — borrowing against shares; a price fall can force the lender to sell them. (Ch 12)
- **Market value (market capitalisation)** — share price × number of shares: the market's price for the whole company. (Ch 4)
- **Mean reversion** — the tendency of returns to drift back toward normal; it saves industries, not companies. (Ch 14)
- **Mosaic theory** — assembling many public facts into a picture nobody else holds; legal so long as every tile is public. (Ch 23)
- **The multiple (price-to-earnings ratio)** — how many years of today's profit per share buyers pay for a share; the confidence figure. (Ch 4)
- **Net Promoter Score (NPS)** — a survey measure (−100 to +100): promoters minus detractors on "would you recommend us?". (Ch 20)
- **Occupancy** — how full a place is, as a percentage of the places it could fill. (Ch 1)
- **Operating leverage** — fixed costs turning small revenue changes into large profit changes. (Ch 3)
- **Participation** — the share of children (0–12) attending subsidised care. (Ch 7)
- **Placement** — a fast sale of new shares to large institutions. (Ch 5)
- **Places** — the number of children a centre is licensed to care for each day. (Ch 1)
- **Profit** — what remains of revenue after all costs; revenue − costs. (Ch 1)
- **Profit margin** — profit as a share of revenue. (Ch 3)
- **Profit per share (earnings per share)** — company profit divided by the number of shares. (Ch 4)
- **Quality rating** — the public rating each Australian childcare service receives against national standards. (Ch 2)
- **Ratio law** — Australian rules setting the legal minimum number of educators per child, by age group. (Ch 1)
- **Receivership** — lenders take control of a company that cannot service its debt. (Ch 14)
- **REIT (real estate investment trust)** — a listed company that owns buildings and passes rent to shareholders. (Ch 11)
- **Revenue** — all the money a business collects from customers before paying any costs. (Ch 1)
- **Roll-up** — a company built by buying many small businesses, valued by the market above the sum of the parts. (Ch 14)
- **Roster** — the schedule of which staff work which hours. (Ch 2)
- **Scrip bid** — a takeover offer paid in the acquirer's own shares rather than cash. (Ch 16)
- **Service approval (licence)** — the regulator's permission to operate a childcare service. (Ch 8)
- **Share** — one equal piece of a company. (Ch 4)
- **Share price** — the price of the most recent trade in a share. (Ch 4)
- **Trading halt** — a pause in trading while material news is released. (Ch 18)
- **Trading update** — an announcement between scheduled reports, often used to correct guidance. (Ch 4)
- **Underlying vs statutory profit** — management's edit of the ordinary engine vs the full legal accounting result; read both, reconcile the gap. (Ch 3, 29)
- **Underwriter** — a firm that guarantees a raising by buying any new shares others refuse. (Ch 5)
- **Variable costs** — costs that rise and fall with how busy the business is. (Ch 1)
- **WALE (weighted average lease expiry)** — the average remaining length of a landlord's leases. (Ch 26)
- **Yield** — annual rent (or income) divided by the price paid for the asset. (Ch 11)

### The named metaphors — this book's technical nouns

- **The Profit Machine** (Ch 1) — the fixed-cost business: you pay for it, full or empty.
- **Staircase Costs** (Ch 2) — staff costs that move in whole-wage steps under the ratio law.
- **The Price Formula** (Ch 4) — share price = (profit ÷ shares) × confidence.
- **The Pizza Principle** (Ch 5) — more slices, same pizza: dilution.
- **The Broken Ruler** (Ch 5) — after the share count changes, old prices measure nothing.
- **Rear-view mirror vs upstream signals** (Ch 6) — lagging vs leading indicators.
- **Patience with signals vs patience with hope** (Ch 6, 33) — waiting on evidence vs waiting on wishes.
- **Forks and Pies** (Ch 8) — fixed local demand split by however many centres exist.
- **The Subsidy Engine** (Ch 9) — the machinery wiring the federal budget to the demand dial.
- **The Double-Edged Sword** (Ch 10) — subsidies lift demand, then summon the supply that destroys the lift.
- **The Beauty Contest** (Ch 17) — parents judging old centres against purpose-built rivals.
- **The $5 Illusion** (Ch 22) — the most expensive sentence in investing: "it was $5 once."
- **The SO index** (Ch 24–25) — the book's homemade fullness gauge; a mirror, labelled.

## Appendix E — Further Reading: The Capital Cycle Classics {#app-e}

Chapter 13 promised the shelf. Each book below extends one of this book's load-bearing ideas; the one-line note says which. Read in any order; the first two are the spine.

**Edward Chancellor (ed.) — *Capital Returns: Investing Through the Capital Cycle* (2015).** The Marathon Asset Management letters that formalised Chapter 13: judge industries by their supply side, follow the money entering and leaving, distrust demand stories. The single closest ancestor of this book's method.

**Edward Chancellor — *Devil Take the Hindmost* (1999).** The history of speculation — every boom in this book's Part II (cheap money, yield hunger, amateurs arriving late) has run identically for three centuries.

**Philip Fisher — *Common Stocks and Uncommon Profits* (1958).** The origin of "scuttlebutt": Chapter 30's field work, seventy years early. Fisher called centres, suppliers, and rivals before it had a name.

**Benjamin Graham — *The Intelligent Investor* (1949; updated editions).** Mr Market, margin of safety, and the discipline of price-versus-value that underlies Chapters 31–34. The chapters on investor psychology are Part V's grandfather.

**Peter Lynch — *One Up on Wall Street* (1989).** The amateur's structural advantages (Chapter 23's argument) made famous: watch what you can see locally, know what you own, and let patience do the compounding.

**Howard Marks — *Mastering the Market Cycle* (2018) and the memos.** Cycles as pendulums, positioning over prediction, and "you can't know the future but you can know where you stand" — Chapter 23's philosophy in a career's worth of variations.

**Michael Mauboussin — *The Success Equation* (2012).** Skill versus luck, base rates, and why processes beat outcomes in noisy domains — the intellectual backing for judging yourself by the log, not the P&L (Chapter 35).

**Daniel Kahneman — *Thinking, Fast and Slow* (2011).** Anchoring (Chapter 22), sunk costs (Chapter 34), narrative over statistics (Chapter 24), and the machinery of the whisper — from the scientist who mapped it.

**William Thorndike — *The Outsiders* (2012).** Capital allocation as the CEO's real job: buybacks, issuance, and discipline — the standard against which Chapter 20's rebuild-era capital pattern was graded.

**John Kenneth Galbraith — *A Short History of Financial Euphoria* (1990).** Ninety pages on why "this time" never is; the roll-up chapters in miniature, with better jokes.

**Fred Schwed — *Where Are the Customers' Yachts?* (1940).** The oldest warning about paying for certainty sold by people who don't have it — the epilogue's closing philosophy, in 1940s prose.

**On the sector itself:** the collapse of ABC Learning is best studied through the contemporaneous Australian financial press archives and the receivers' public reports (Chapter 14's sources); the ongoing policy architecture through the Productivity Commission's inquiry reports into early childhood education and care — the closest thing the sector has to a standing map of the Subsidy Engine.

*A note on what is deliberately absent: books of chart patterns, market timing systems, and forecasting services. Chapter 23 explains why; Chapter 24 shows what their track records are made of.*

# WORKING PAPERS (annex)

*The living documents used to build the book, included for reference.*


## Style Guide — "The Occupancy Game"

### The reader
An intelligent adult with zero finance background. Assume nothing. Explain everything the first time it appears.

### Voice rules
1. Dead simple layman English.
2. Short sentences. Aim under 20 words. One idea per sentence.
3. Active voice. "The centre pays rent," not "rent is paid by the centre."
4. No jargon without an immediate plain-words definition — and a glossary entry the same day.
5. Toy numbers first, real numbers second. Teach the idea with round, made-up numbers. Then show the real G8 numbers.
6. Nothing may be used before it is taught. Every chapter builds only on chapters before it.
7. Mark estimates with ≈. Never fabricate a number, quote, or source.
8. Chapter length: 1,800–2,500 words. Appendices may differ.

### ASD-STE100 rules (Issue 9, 15 January 2025) — adapted for this book
The book follows the writing rules of ASD-STE100 Simplified Technical English, Issue 9, adapted for narrative teaching.

**Sentences**
- Maximum 20 words in an instruction. Maximum 25 words in a descriptive sentence. Target: under 20.
- One topic per sentence. One instruction per sentence.
- Use vertical lists to break up complex material.

**Paragraphs**
- Maximum 6 sentences per paragraph. One topic per paragraph.
- Do not start many paragraphs in a row with the same word.

**Verbs**
- Active voice. Say who does what.
- Simple tenses only: present, past, future.
- Avoid "-ing" verb forms where a simple form exists.
- Prefer one simple verb to a phrasal verb ("remove", not "take out").

**Words**
- One word, one meaning, everywhere in the book.
- Use short, common words ("use", not "utilise"; "start", not "commence").
- Use articles ("the", "a"). Repeat the noun instead of an unclear "it" or "this".
- Keep a noun cluster to 3 nouns or fewer.

**Technical nouns (STE's escape hatch, used honestly)**
- STE permits domain terms as "technical nouns" and "technical verbs (terms)".
- In this book, the technical nouns are: (a) glossary terms, defined in plain words at first use; (b) the named metaphors of the lexicon below.
- Casual idioms, slang, and unnamed figurative phrases are NOT permitted. "The machine hums" is out. "The Profit Machine", as a defined term, is in.
- A comparison used to teach (toy centre vs fruit stall) is permitted. State it in plain, literal sentences.

### Chapter skeleton
- **Open:** one-line bridge from the previous chapter.
- **Body:** toy example → real example → what it means.
- **Close:** a boxed line:

> **The one thing to remember:** [one sentence].

### The metaphor lexicon (reuse these; never invent rivals)
| Metaphor | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **The Profit Machine** | Revenue vs fixed costs |
| **Staircase Costs** | Staff hired in steps under ratio law |
| **The Price Formula** | Share price = (profit ÷ shares) × confidence |
| **The Pizza Principle** | Dilution: more slices, same pizza |
| **Forks and Pies** | Fixed local demand split by supply |
| **The Beauty Contest** | Old centres vs purpose-built rivals |
| **The Double-Edged Sword** | Subsidies lift demand, then lure supply |
| **The Broken Ruler** | Never anchor to old share prices |
| **Rear-view mirror vs upstream signals** | Lagging vs leading indicators |
| **Patience with signals vs patience with hope** | Waiting on evidence vs waiting on wishes |

### Honesty rules
- This is education, not financial advice. Disclaimer in front matter and epilogue.
- The 2025 criminal case: charges, pleas, dates only. Sober tone. No graphic detail. The accused is entitled to a fair trial. The book's subject is the business consequence.
- Where sources conflict, state the range and the date. Don't pick a winner silently.
- Chapter 24 confesses that the author's own SO index was lagging. Keep that humility everywhere.

### Mechanics
- Paraphrase sources. No long quotations.
- Every number in a chapter must exist in `fact-sheet.md` with a source and access date.
- Currency is AUD unless stated. "Profit" means what the chapter has defined it to mean at that point in the book.

## Part I Recap — Foundations: How a Business Makes and Loses Money

*One page. Six chapters. One tower of ideas, each floor standing on the one below.*

**Chapter 1 — The Profit Machine.** A business is a machine: costs in, revenue out, profit is the difference. A childcare centre's costs — rent, and staff set by the ratio law — are almost all fixed. Revenue = children × fee, so revenue moves with occupancy while costs stand still. You pay for the machine whether one child attends or fifty. G8 Education runs about 395 such machines; in 2025 they averaged 65.8% full, and revenue fell 7.2% to $948.2 million.

**Chapter 2 — Staircase Costs.** The ratio law makes staff costs move in whole-wage steps. Baby number 5 forces a second educator; losing two babies saves nothing. Falling occupancy slides revenue down a smooth slope while costs wait on their step: half-full rooms carry full-room wages. Staff shortage, quality ratings, and human bonds make the real staircase stickier than the legal one.

**Chapter 3 — Operating Leverage.** Toy Sunny Corner breaks even at 43 of 50 children — 86% occupancy. Children 1–43 pay for the machine; children 44–50 are the entire profit. So a 10% occupancy dip cut profit 70%. In real 2025, G8's 7.2% revenue fall became an 18.9% fall in underlying operating profit. The lever works both directions: recovery lands on wages already paid.

**Chapter 4 — The Price Formula.** Share price = (profit ÷ shares) × confidence. The multiple is years of profit paid upfront, and it is fed by interest rates, growth, and trust in management. Prices crash three ways: profit falls, confidence falls, shares increase. The levers stack: on 4 November 2025, a few points of occupancy became an 18% guidance cut and a 13.7% price fall by late morning.

**Chapter 5 — The Pizza Principle.** Shares are slices of one pizza. A capital raising cuts new slices, and desperate companies sell them cheapest. In 2020, G8 raised $301 million at $0.80 — roughly 376 million new slices, nearly doubling the count. After that, every pre-2020 price is a Broken Ruler: full recovery of the company is not full recovery of the shareholder.

**Chapter 6 — Lagging vs Leading.** Occupancy is the last link of a chain: trust → searches → enquiries → tours → enrolments → occupancy → revenue → profit → price. Everything downstream is history; everything upstream is signal, months early. G8's own November 2025 words ran the chain in order: enquiries first, occupancy next, guidance last. The book's project: watch upstream, with patience for signals, not hope.

### Contradiction scan (run 2026-08-15)
- Toy numbers consistent across Ch 1–3: 50 places; $150 fee; $10 variable; fixed costs $6,000 ($1,000 rent + $4,500 wages + $500 other); break-even 43 children/86%; profits +$1,000 (full), +$300 (45), −$2,500 (25). Verified by recalculation.
- Ch 2 room table sums to 50 places and 8 educators; 70%-occupancy example recomputed (revenue $5,250, costs $6,100, loss $850). Two arithmetic slips found and fixed ($2,000 educator wages, not $2,250; 12.5% wage fall, not 11%).
- G8 figures consistent everywhere used: 395 centres; $948.2m revenue (−7.2%); 65.8% average occupancy; underlying EBIT $93.3m (−18.9%); Nov 2025 downgrade $115m → $91–98m, −13.7% intraday; 2020 raising $301m at $0.80 ($227m placement + $75m entitlement, ~33% retail take-up).
- Share counts around the 2020 raising: stated as ≈460m → ≈840m with an explicit "sources vary" caveat; precise counts deferred to Ch 19/22 against annual reports.
- Metaphor lexicon: only named metaphors used; each defined at first use; no rival metaphors introduced.
- Teaching order: no concept used before its chapter (shares named in Ch 1 with explicit pointer to Ch 4–5; statutory/underlying flagged to Ch 29).

## Part II Recap — The Playing Field: Australian Childcare

**Chapter 7 — The Pie: Demand.** Demand = children × hours, measured in child-days (~4.0m/week ≈). It grew ~28% in a decade while births stayed flat (peak 315,147 in 2018; 292,318 in 2024), driven by participation (≈30.7% → ≈35.0%), rising hours, the culture flip to "education", migration, and — fastest of all — subsidy policy. December 2025 brought the first decline in children in care (−0.7%).

**Chapter 8 — The Seats: Supply.** CBDC centres: ≈6,300 (2013) → 9,786 (Dec 2025). Forks and Pies: a new centre cuts every incumbent's occupancy by arithmetic (toy Millbrook: 95% → 71%). No party in the approval chain says no because a suburb is full; seats, once built, almost never leave. Sector occupancy 81.8% (Dec 2023) → 76.1% (Dec 2025); WA 70.5%.

**Chapter 9 — The Subsidy Engine.** Budget → CCS percentage → gap fee → days bought. CCS July 2018; Cheaper Child Care July 2023 (to 90%); 3 Day Guarantee 5 Jan 2026 (activity test gone, 72 hrs/fortnight). Cap $14.63 vs average CBDC fee ≈$14.40. The taxpayer is the sector's majority customer; wage deal trades 15% pay rises for fee caps (4.4%/4.2%).

**Chapter 10 — The Double-Edged Sword.** Demand answers budgets (months); supply answers property maths (2–4 years); nobody referees. Phases: announcement → sweet spot → flood → hangover. WA ran the script: places +17.6% in two years into flat demand. The 3 Day Guarantee has started the next cycle's clock.

**Chapter 11 — Meet the Players.** Nine rational players; only the operator is paid on occupancy. Developer profits before a child enrols; landlord REITs hold ≈15-year escalating leases; the lease trap makes chains sign the buildings that flood their own pies; the parent — the only real customer — votes on trust first, and her veto is total.

**Chapter 12 — Interest Rates.** Price = rent ÷ required yield: the 2010s yield slide alone lifted centre values >60% with no extra children. Cheap money (2015–2021, trough 0.1%) built the oversupply; the 2022–23 tightening (to 4.35%) froze the pipeline; 2025 cuts (≈3.6%) may thaw it. Rates also set the multiple on the shares. Demand barely notices — which makes rates a clean supply forecaster.

**Chapter 13 — The Capital Cycle.** High returns attract capacity; capacity destroys returns; capital flees; demand slowly rebuilds the floor — slowly, because childcare's exit is sticky (seats outlive operators). Judge the industry by its supply side's behaviour: approvals, auction yields, chain expansion, landlord tone, amateur money. Workhorse sum: excess seats ÷ pie growth = years of hangover.

**Chapter 14 — Ghost Story: ABC Learning.** 697+ centres, $4.1bn peak value, $1.8bn debt; profit −42% (H2 2007), shares suspended Aug 2008, receivership Nov 2008; Goodstart bought 570 centres Dec 2009. The sector recovered without the company: mean reversion saves industries, not companies. The roll-up's magic trick (buy at 4×, be valued at 20×) was public, and so were the four warning dials. Two years later, G8 began the same trick.

### Contradiction scan (run 2026-08-15)
- Occupancy figures kept distinct and labelled: sector CBDC occupancy (81.8% → 76.1%, The Sector/industry data) vs G8's own average (65.8% FY25) — never mixed.
- Millbrook toy numbers recomputed: 1,000/1,050 = 95.2%; 1,000/1,400 = 71.4%; year-3 flood 1,200/1,960 = 61.2%; twelve-year/16-year waits consistent with 3% growth arithmetic. Ch 13 worked wait recomputed: 1,150 → 1,378 at 3% ≈ 6.2 years ✓.
- Ch 12 revaluation arithmetic: 130k/0.065 = 2.0m; /0.05 = 2.6m; /0.04 = 3.25m; falls/gains as stated ✓.
- Subsidy dates consistent across Ch 7/9/10 (2018, July 2023, 5 Jan 2026); wage-deal caps consistent with Ch 3/9 (4.4%/4.2%).
- ABC figures used only from the logged source; Goodstart purchase price deliberately not stated (unverified).
- Ch 14's "roughly a fifth of places" for ABC: consistent with ≈1,000 AU centres of ≈5,000–6,000 mid-2000s stock — flagged ≈, acceptable.
- Metaphors: Forks and Pies introduced Ch 8 as planned; Beauty Contest only previewed (named for Ch 17); no rival metaphors coined.
- All G8 references consistent with Part I facts (395 centres ≈4% of 9,786 ✓; <$1bn revenue ✓).

## Part III Recap — G8 Education: A Full History

**Chapter 15 — The Roll-Up Machine (2010–2014).** Buy centres at ≈4× profit, be valued at ≈20×: accretion arithmetic made dilution virtuous while the multiple held. 63 centres for $104.7m (Feb 2014); 114 in H1 2014; Sterling at 5.8× broke the discipline publicly; $150m raise + $50m notes fed the treadmill. Peak above $5 (≈, late 2014), ≈400 centres, 21 brands kept. Three stacked assumptions: cheap targets, sticky acquired profits, uncrowded suburbs.

**Chapter 16 — Crash One (2014–15).** Profit held; the multiple halved; ≈40% of value gone with no disaster on any single day. Belief eroded holder by holder as old information was re-examined: dearer meals, unremarkable organic occupancy, the circular treadmill. The hostile scrip bid for Affinity failed — Takeovers Panel "unacceptable circumstances", Oct 2015 — confirming the strain. Track a story's props, not its results.

**Chapter 17 — The Flood (2016–18): Crash Two.** Cheap money's construction wave (conceived 2013–15) landed on G8's doorstep — built where the profits were, i.e. where G8 was. The Beauty Contest concentrated damage on the oldest fleet. Like-for-like occupancy 74% (−1.9pp, 2018); revenue rose 7.7% while underlying EBIT fell 12.7% to $136.3m; shares −13% on results day. Oversupply files its plans with the council: only the supply dials told the truth, years early.

**Chapter 18 — Crash Three (2019).** Two downgrades in three months: $140–145m guided (Aug) → $131–134m (Nov); a ~7% cut, a 17.8% weekly fall to $2.13. The multiple's revenge: a miss reprices the promiser. Morningstar's reasoned "excessive" call (fair value $3.08, occupancy back to 80% in three years) was then deleted — not refuted — by COVID. Good analysis narrows odds on futures resembling the present; it cannot price the future that doesn't.

**Chapter 19 — Crash Four (2020).** Free-childcare rations kept doors open at survival prices. 9 April 2020: $301m at $0.80 ($227m placement + $75m entitlement, ~33% retail take-up); ≈376m new shares; count ≈460m → ≈840m. The quietest crash — invisible on the price chart, permanent in the denominator. Emergency money is priced by the emergency. Leases moved onto the balance sheet; the system was guaranteed, shareholder returns explicitly not.

**Chapter 20 — The Rebuild (2021–2024).** The right list, executed: pruning, greenfields, staff retention 79%, teacher vacancies −25%, 95% quality ratings, record NPS, buybacks (≈840m → ≈770m shares), dividends resumed. Occupancy refused to follow: ≈70.7% (FY2024), below 2018 — seats never left, births sagged, the contest reset its bar. Operating excellence is necessary, insufficient, and always late; fund a turnaround only when controllables *and* uncontrollables turn.

**Chapter 21 — The Wound (2025– ).** Handled soberly: charges public 1 July 2025; 153 charges re 12 alleged victims at four Melbourne centres; accused worked at 23 services (2017–2025) with a valid WWCC; formal not guilty (holding) pleas 29 May 2026; County Court; directions 23 June 2026; trial likely 2027. The chain attacked at its first link: enquiries → occupancy 65.8% avg, 54.4% spot (mid-Feb 2026) → revenue $948.2m (−7.2%) → EBIT $93.3m (−18.9%) → price. Statutory loss $303.3m incl. $349.1m goodwill impairment — the roll-up's final invoice. Dividend withheld, buyback paused, ≈40 centres closed/suspended. Inside-the-gates metrics held; strangers walked. HostPlus sold down (6.33%→5.29%) as Allan Gray bought up (18.68%→19.87%) in the same February week.

**Chapter 22 — The $5 Illusion.** ≈770m shares today vs ≈330m (≈) in 2014: whole-company value fell ≈60% while the per-share price fell 80%+ — the difference is dilution's invoice. Honest $5 arithmetic: ≈$257m sustained profit needed at 15× — nearly triple today's $93m. Prices are not rubber bands; value the company that exists. Analysis always hands you homework; memory never does.

### Contradiction scan (run 2026-08-15)
- Five-crash taxonomy consistent and mutually exclusive: multiple (16), profit/supply (17), guidance trust (18), share count (19), product trust (21).
- Share counts consistent across 5/19/20/22: ≈460m → ≈840m (2020) → ≈770m (2025–26); 2014 count stated only as ≈330m with ≈ and range language; all flagged for pinning against annual reports (Appendix C uses ≈ accordingly).
- Occupancy series consistent: 80s (roll-up era, ≈) → 74% LFL (2018) → ~75% (2019, Morningstar reference) → ≈70.7% (FY2024) → 65.8% avg / 68.3% spot Nov 2025 / 54.4% spot mid-Feb 2026. Like-for-like vs group-average definitional difference noted in Ch 17.
- Guidance chain consistent with Ch 4: Aug 2019 $140–145m → Nov 2019 $131–134m; 2025: ≈$115m → $91–98m (both used identically in Parts I and III).
- 2018 figures: revenue $858.2m, EBIT $136.3m (−12.7%) — consistent with FY25's $948.2m/$93.3m trajectory; H1-2018 NPAT fact-pack figure ($25.6m, −24%) deliberately NOT used (unverified).
- Criminal case: only verified facts used; presumption of innocence stated; no graphic detail; business-consequence focus maintained.
- Toy company names kept distinct: KidCo (Chs 4–5, 12), RollCo (Chs 15–16); no cross-contamination of their numbers.
- Ch 22 whole-company sums recomputed: $5×330m ≈ $1.65bn ✓; sub-$1 × 770m ≈ $600–700m ✓; $5×770m÷15 ≈ $257m ✓.

## Part IV Recap — Building the Instrument Panel

**Chapter 23 — The Philosophy.** Turning points cannot be forecast; what remains is observing early, pricing scenarios, and positioning for asymmetry. Mosaic theory: public tiles, privately assembled — legally and humbly. The two golden questions sort all signals: knowable years ahead (supply) and months ahead (demand, trust). The amateur's real edges: horizon, size, attention. Eight-line creed included.

**Chapter 24 — Correlation Traps.** The SO confession in full: an index built from occupancy's own lagged ingredients (enrolments, centre counts) correlated beautifully and led nothing — a rear-view mirror labelled windscreen, exposed by the 2025 trust shock. The three jaws: correlation is cheap, mining finds what you seek, lucky fits fail at regime changes. The cause-first rule: no dial without "this moves first *because*…". Testing rules: causal sentence first, split samples, economic significance, pre-committed retirement conditions. Mirrors are kept — labelled.

**Chapter 25 — Build Your Own Gauge.** SO = 100 × child-days ÷ (centres × 1,000): 46.8 (2014) → ≈41.5 (2024) → ≈40.6 (Dec 2025) — the decade's flood in one lagging, honest line. The version that matters: the 2-km local gauge from the public register's real approved places (toy Millbrook: 0.91 → 0.69 the day a 120-place approval prints). Five faults recited; maintenance rules (annotate, rebase, log misses); frequency splicing in pencil.

**Chapter 26 — Supply Signals.** The conveyor: council DAs (3–4 yrs), service approvals (1–2 yrs), construction economics (the throttle), REIT books (vacancy, re-leasing spreads, WALE), the company's own 2-km disclosure — and its silences. Stage-weight applications; thresholds set in advance (e.g. pipeline >10% of local capacity = flashing). ≈5 hrs/quarter, all free. Current picture: pipeline thin, throttle half-open, landlords cautious — kindest supply era since the flood, guaranteed not to last unannounced.

**Chapter 27 — Demand Signals.** Six dials, slow to fast: births/cohorts (2018 peak → 2023 trough now in baby rooms), the quarterly CCS ledger (decompose children × hours; hours is the neglected factor and where the 3-Day Guarantee prints first), the policy calendar, search interest (YoY, brand baskets, divergence), review velocity/valence, and the fee-wage scissors (adverse: wages by right, fees by permission). Funnel arithmetic: a 15% search dip can bleed 3 occupancy points a year. Seasonal calendar: compare January only with January.

**Chapter 28 — Trust Signals.** Five new dials for the chain's first link: media heat and its decay slope (weekly log); the court calendar as scheduled shocks (hearings schedule headlines); company breadcrumbs and their menu-changes (vanished metrics = bad metrics; strangers' metrics outrank family metrics in a trust crisis); safety proof banked vs announced (CCTV ≈, ratings, reform milestones); reform announcements sorted into engine/compliance/legitimacy bins. Two trusts on two clocks — sector and operator — and the gap between them is where the price errs. Calibration honest: designed on a sample of one.

**Chapter 29 — Company Vital Signs.** Six published vitals: cash burn → runway in halves (run under two scenarios); the lease stack (the skeleton, printed); capital moves as the board's own runway forecast (Feb 2026 = third rung of the escalation ladder); substantial-holder filings (HostPlus out / Allan Gray in = maximum professional disagreement — itself information); labour vitals (retention 79%, vacancies −25% = recovery capacity); the guidance track record (the multiple's memory — maintain the table yourself). Underlying vs statutory untangled; leases ≠ headline net debt. ≈10 hrs/year via the announcement feed.

**Chapter 30 — Field Work.** The unautomatable edge, inside legal (mosaic vs inside information) and ethical (children's workplaces; tour honestly, photograph nothing) lines. Four practices: quarterly enquiry calls on a fixed script (spot occupancy at zero lag), sparing comparative tours, drop-off car counts from public space, and the barbecue's ground truth. Field log discipline: observations before interpretations; anecdotes may initiate and confirm, never override. Remote versions exist at two-thirds strength. Field knowledge compounds. Total Part IV apparatus: ≈3 days/quarter.

### Contradiction scan (run 2026-08-15)
- SO index arithmetic consistent across Ch 24/25 and fact pack: 3.09/6.6=46.8 ✓; 3.97/9.571=41.5 ✓; 3.97/9.786=40.6 ✓; 2014 centre count implied ≈6,600 stated as ≈ (fact pack's ~6,300 was 2013 — consistent).
- The confession (Ch 24) is consistent with Ch 25's rebuild: demoted to confirmation/calibration, never re-promoted anywhere in Part IV.
- Causal sentences present for every admitted dial (checked each chapter); chart-pattern signals refused consistently (Ch 23, 24).
- Funnel toy numbers (Ch 27) recomputed: 1,000→100→50→10; 15% search dip → 8.5 arrivals vs 10 outflow ✓.
- Millbrook local gauge recomputed: 1,725/1,900=0.908 ✓; 1,725/2,500=0.69 ✓; 380+120=500 places ×5=2,500 ✓; "32% capacity addition" = 120/380 ✓.
- Time budgets consistent: supply ≈5h + demand ≈4h + vitals ≈2.5h/qtr + field ≈1 day ≈ "three days a quarter" (Ch 30) ✓.
- 2019 2-km disclosure (1.0% vs 4.2%) used consistently in Ch 25/26 with Ch 18/Part III.
- No new unverified factual claims introduced; adjacent-history material (Ch 28) kept generic and unnamed; toy examples labelled toy; ≈ used for estimates throughout.

## Part V Recap — From Signals to Decisions

**Chapter 31 — Valuation Without Anchors.** The blank sheet: value = (sustainable profit ÷ shares) × defensible multiple, three scenarios wide. Recovery ≈$2.20–2.90 (occupancy to high-70s over 3–4 yrs; multiple 13–15); stagnation ≈$0.55–0.80 (≈today's price — the market prices stagnation-as-certainty); dilution ≈$0.35 (a $200m raise at $0.40 → ≈1.27bn shares). Weights come from the dashboard; trade only on a *named* disagreement with the price's implied weights. Multiples anchored via the three feeders and the earnings-yield cross-check. Structured guessing beats point forecasts because it fails informatively. All figures ≈, method not advice.

**Chapter 32 — The Dilution Nightmare.** The realistic worst case is rescue-by-dilution, not bankruptcy (the 2020 proof; the subsidy floor guarantees survival-to-be-diluted). Six escalating public tells: runway compression, the dividend/buyback ladder, covenant language, asset sales, adviser hires, the halt. Boards raise late for structural reasons — which makes the ritual dependable. Re-weight mechanically on tells, decide entitlement participation (a levy with a costly opt-out) in advance, re-value from scratch after any raising. The list runs in reverse too: descending the ladder retires the branch. Feb 2026: amber — rungs two-three climbed, no covenant noise, labour assets intact.

**Chapter 33 — Fairness Is Not a Price.** Markets price consequences, not verdicts: an unfair demand fall damages cash flows as truly as a deserved one — the parent owes no company her child's benefit of the doubt. "Trust always comes back": *always* is survivorship, *when* runs past shareholder patience, *to whom* splits sector from operator, *for whose benefit* depends on the share count. "They won't let it fail" protects the system, never the register (2020: rescue at $0.80). The mirror test separates patience with signals (can state, in writing, what would make it sell) from patience with hope (can only name the old price). Banned-vocabulary list installed.

**Chapter 34 — Self-Control.** The sunk-effort trap: effort bills itself, and "do nothing" feels like a refund refused — the deep researcher is most at risk. Four instruments, all timed *before* the first dollar: the written thesis (claim, evidence, price logic, confession); kill criteria (observable, dated, causal — never a price); sizing by the written worst branch (a live halving-branch caps any honest size); staging on signals, never on price schedules. The calendar of temptations marked (results, halts, rallies, quiet, anniversaries). Most honest analyses end with "not yet, and here is exactly what would change that" — the machinery working.

**Chapter 35 — Trusting the Dashboard.** Three speeds: weekly (20 min), monthly (an evening), quarterly (the sitting — the only routine decision point). Conflicts adjudicated by causal seniority (upstream healing + downstream rotting = trough-mapping; upstream rotting + downstream fine = the Nov-2025 shape), level-versus-change, and wait-one-cycle. Regime weighting, declared and dated: today trust > demand > supply; re-weight once per regime change, in writing. Overrides: categorical events only, written, ≤1/year. The log is the analyst's own track record; the rhythm's function is replacing the market's clock with yours.

**Chapter 36 — The One-Page Checklist.** The whole apparatus on one sheet: six dial families (trust, demand, supply, vitals, field, gauges), each dial with source / frequency / pre-committed trigger, plus the standing-rules spine of 27 one-line laws. Two pages and a workbook total: this page schedules attention; the thesis page decides. Ninety-day standing-up plan; four-step trigger protocol (verify, classify, re-weight, check the page); transferable to any fixed-cost occupancy business; thresholds expire, logic doesn't.

### Contradiction scan (run 2026-08-15)
- Scenario arithmetic recomputed: recovery 130–150m/770m ≈ 17–19c ×13–15 ≈ $2.21–2.85 ✓; stagnation 50–65m/770m ≈ 6.5–8.4c ×8–10 ≈ $0.52–0.84 ✓; dilution 55m/1.27bn ≈ 4.3c ×8 ≈ $0.35 ✓; blend 0.35×2.50+0.45×0.70+0.20×0.35 = 0.875+0.315+0.07 = $1.26 ✓ ("≈$1.27").
- Rights example (Ch 32): 10,000 + 5,000 at $0.55 = $2,750 ✓; post-raise ≈(2×0.80+1×0.55)/3 = $0.717 ≈ "$0.72" ✓.
- Kill criteria examples exclude price triggers, consistent with Ch 33/35 doctrine; staging-on-signals consistent with Ch 22's averaging-down cousin.
- Regime ordering (trust > demand > supply) consistent with Ch 21/28; scenario weights presented as illustrative (≈) everywhere; educational disclaimer restated in Ch 31 and via "method not advice".
- Checklist triggers match their source chapters' thresholds (10% pipeline, 4-halves runway, 2-miss guidance rule, etc.) — cross-checked ✓.
- No new factual claims requiring verification introduced in Part V; all real numbers reused from logged facts; toy companies (FallCo) kept distinct from KidCo/RollCo.
